Introduction 


The concept of time presents a challenge to a wide variety of disciplines, which 
fall within the domain of the humanistic as well as the natural sciences. Accor¬ 
dingly, many different methods and lines of approach have been followed in the 
investigation and treatment of this fundamental problem. 

History of ideas bears witness to the central role, that is played by the pro¬ 
blem of time in the philosophical and theological traditions across the cultural 
boundaries. Moreover, the endeavours to interpret the enigmatic character of the 
time-experience have given rise to a multiplicity of views within each tradition. 
An attempt at a global view, focusing on certain aspects of the wide variety of 
conceptual models of time, is made in the last chapter of this work. This inter- 
cultural perspective not only provides an interesting background scene for the 
study of time in Indian philosophy, but also brings into focus some of the similar 
features that the universal struggle of the philosophical intellect with this pro¬ 
blem has given rise to. Thus, for example, one finds controversies in the different 
cultures regarding such central issues as whether time is real or unreal, whether it 
is a conceptual constrution or is an objective category, etc. 

The study of time in Indian philosophy is rewarding in many ways. It makes 
one aware of the rich and varied philosophical speculations about the problem 
from the earliest phase of its history. 

Going through the forest of the early sources of the Indian tradition, one 
comes across a host of ideas touching upon different aspects of the problem of 
time scattered in the form of myths and allegories. These ideas are rich in 
suggestion and might even be said to anticipate some later theories but were not 
themselves fullfledged views about time. Mention should be made of such popu¬ 
lar ideas as are contained in the Puranzs , the epic Mahabharata and the Atharva 
Veda. 

The Atharva Veda contains hymns dedicated to time . 1 Here time is described 
as that which is in contact with all that there is, as that which regulates and 
controls all. Some lines describe time to be the cause of the origin, maintenance 


1 cf. 3/2/6/3/8, 19/6/53/7/3-10 and 19/6/53/8/1-5 of Atharvaveda Samhita with the commentary 
of Sayanacarya [1], 



12 


Introduction 


and destruction of the universe. Some ascribe to time the status of God, hailing it 
to be the ultimate, the all-powerful principle. 

The idea of time as power of the Absolute is ancient and can be seen worked 
out in the integral philosophy of Kashmir Saivism. 2 

In the Pardnzs one finds the idea of the four yagas 3 -satya, treta , dvapara and 
kali-along with that of the world-cycles, which provide a vision of vast expanse 
of time in astronomical terms. Thus the Brahmanda Parana dramatically descri¬ 
bes the four yagas as the four faces of time which are said to create and destroy 
all beings. Again, the idea that the solar motion is the basis for time-divisions is 
found in the Agni Parana (chapter 122). 

In the Mahabharata one comes across the well-known simile where time is 
described as the wire-holder ( satradhara ) 4 of the universe, permitting events and 
preventing them from taking place. Time is thus responsible for the order and 
sequence of events. 

The Svetasvatara Upanisad (1/2) refers to the view which held time to be the 
cause of everything. 5 

Examples can be multiplied. But before we go deeper into the matter, let us 
note the significance of the word kdla which in Sanskrit stands for time. The 
etymological root of the term kdla is y/kal which means to count (cf. Latin 
calcalo). 6 Thus according to some the word kdla signifies that which can count 
up the age of all. 

The root y/kal also signifies ‘to devour’, which leads to the ascription of the 
destructive power to time. Thus, kdla also refers to death. 

The different nuances of the word kdla can be seen in the Bhagavad-Gita in 
the various utterances of Lord Krsna. 

Thus, in Bhagavadglta X/30: 

- kdlah kalayatdm aham - 
‘Of calculators I am Time’. 

Again in the same work, XI 32 

Kalo'smi , lokaksayakrt - 
‘Time am I world-destroying’. 


2 cf. kdla as kancuka in the Pratyabhijha philosophy. 

3 world ages. 

4 i.e. stage-manager. 

5 Kalakdranavada. 

6 Sanskrit-English dictionary by Monier Williams [104]. 



Introduction 


13 


Here the supreme spirit as the destroyer of the universe is seen as a personifi¬ 
cation of the destructive principle, which is identified with time. 

The idea of time as the Supreme Principle or Lord, found in the Atharva Veda 
and in Visnu Purana , can also be found in the Bbagavadgita X/33: 

- aham eva'ksayah kalo - 
‘I also am imperishable time’. 7 

Bhartrhari, the great grammarian philosopher, also focuses on the etymologi¬ 
cal meaning of kala through illuminating imageries and metaphors. Thus, we 
read in his Vakyapadiya (3/9/14) - 

»By means of activities similar to the turning of the water-wheel the eternal 
and all-pervasive Time turns out ( kalayati ) all the fragments ( kaldh = objects) 
and thus acquires the name of kala (time)« [75]. So much for the etymological 
meaning of the word kala. 

In this work, however, we have selected for our study the well-formulated 
views about time that have emerged in the history of Indian philosophy. An 
attempt is made, first and foremost, to give a systematic presentation of the 
distinct conceptual models concerning time, which allow for a philosophical 
scrutiny and appraisal. 

A careful perusal of the philosophies of India, as this study amply bears 
witness to, discloses a spectrum of views about time. The philosophical contrasts 
of ideas involved in the different theories are indeed fascinating. On the one 
hand, there is the view which emphasises the reality of time mentioning its 
ontological properties e.g. all-pervasiveness, etc.; on the other hand, there is the 
view which dialectically rejects the ontological reality of time pointing to its 
phenomenal character. Again, we find the view of time as indivisible, ubiquitous 
and unitary, as opposed to the theory which vehemently holds time to be essenti¬ 
ally discrete, all ideas of time-continuum being merely a conceptual construc¬ 
tion. Moreover, the differences even amongst the various theories of time as 
discrete disclose a variety of philosophical patterns. As an example of such 
observations based on the present investigation, we refer to the section VII (ii), 
‘A Comparative Note on the Concept of Instant’. 

More significant, as this study points to, is to note the drastic consequence of a 
specific view of time on a given philosophical scheme which aims at interconnec¬ 
ting the major concepts in a systematic, harmonious whole. Thus, the impact of 
a specific view about time on such major issues as those of being, non-being, 
creation and causality are noted in different sections of this work. 


7 The translations of quotations from Bbagavadgita are from Radhakrishan [2]. 



14 


Introduction 


To give a major example of how through a radical formulation of time as 
instant one arrives at a novel conception of being, reference could be made to the 
Buddhist doctrine of ‘being as instantaneous*. Again, the impact of this doctrine 
as destructive of Brahmanical metaphysics is evident and accounts for the whole¬ 
sale attack on this idea by all Brahmanical schools of thought. For comments on 
this subject see section VII (iv) on ‘Being and-Time*. 

Based on the original Sanskrit texts as well as important secondary literature, 
an effort is made to expose the principal contending views about time, which can 
be detected in the conceptual structures that have have emerged in Brahmanism, 
Buddhism, and Jainism. For the purpose of focusing on the major as well as 
subtle differences of the views and their implications for other important philo¬ 
sophical issues, the historical documents of the polemics between the schools 
have been collected and used when necessary and relevant. In the case of certain 
important schools, where preoccupation with time does not take the form of a 
direct treatment of a distinct philosophical problem, it is nevertheless found to 
be implicit in the general structure of their metaphysical thinking. An attempt is 
then made to see the significance of their stand regarding time within the com¬ 
pass of the entire framework of the system or on the basis of the record of their 
rejection of an opponent’s position. 

Observations and comments are made in separate sub-sections of the earlier 
expository chapters of the book. The last two chapters are, however, more 
exclusively devoted to an overall view within the panorama of Indian philosophy 
as well as to a global view of the problem with an inter-cultural perspective. 
Chapter VII contains reflections resulting from our study. It is emphasised that 
the problem of time is central and decisive in the history of the development of 
philosophical thought in India. Chapter VIII contains an outline of some of the 
major philosophical interpretations of time found in the history of Western 
philosophy and theology. An attempt has been made to throw light on the 
notion of the so-called cyclic time in the context of Greek and Indian philoso¬ 
phy. Finally, the problem of time is seen as a bridge for an encounter between 
different theological structures. 



General background 


A survey of philosophical and theological thinking, pertaining to the problem of 
time, reveals a continued search for greater clarification and understanding, 
involving different approaches and methods, and consequently resulting in wi¬ 
dely divergent philosophical formulations. 

It is evident that a study of such a fundamental problem as that of time cannot 
be isolated and disconnected from other major philosophical issues of a specific 
system in a given tradition. It is interrelated and interwoven with such basic 
concepts as those of being and becoming, change and causality, creation and 
annihilation. 

The present endeavour to study the problem of time in Indian philosophy 
emphasises the fact that the significance of the different views on time, that have 
emerged in the course of its history, can be grasped only in the framework of the 
general scheme of thought - in all its metaphysical, logical and epistemological 
aspects - of each particular philosophical school. 

This calls for an understanding of the general conceptual structures of the 
principal schools of Indian philosophy as a context for our specific study. As the 
investigation proceeds, it will be clear to what extent the basic metaphysical 
intuitions of the different schools, supported by their epistemology and logic, 
provide for alternative conceptual models which shape-their respective views 
regarding the problem of time. 

We shall, therefore, attempt to give a broad outline of the major streams of the 
Indian philosophical tradition, of which the principal ones are Brahmanism and 
Buddhism and the relatively minor one is Jainism. The historical perspective of 
the study is meant to take account of the fundamental conceptual patterns of 
each of these philosophico-religious traditions and is not intended for a chrono¬ 
logical record of ideas. 

The predominating source of speculative ideas in pre-Buddhistic India is the 
Brahmanical tradition. It has its roots in the Vedas and the Upanisads. The 
fundamental intuitions contained in these scriptures - which are considered by 
the tradition as revealed - form the very basis on which is built the whole edifice 
of Brahmanism. We shall leave aside here the fascinating transition from the early 
Vedic to Upanisadic thought and the development of ideas that took place in the 
process of intense philosophical quest of the Upanisadic seers. However, let us 



16 


General background 


take note of the most important idea, emerging from the Upanisads , which 
pervades the entire Brahmanical philosophy, viz. that of the identical, immutable 
reality underlying all change and becoming. This is the core of the dtma -doctrine 
of the Upanisads , which sets the pattern of all subsequent philosophisations on 
the part of those who carried on this tradition historically down the centuries, 
long after the Vedic period. 

For the purpose of the present investigation we shall focus our attention 
exclusively on the stages of philosophical thinking crystallised into well-formu¬ 
lated schools of philosophy. The six principal schools that developed in the 
Brahmanical tradition are Sankhya, Yoga, Nyaya, Vaisesika, Purva-MImamsa 
and Uttara-Mlmamsa. Remaining faithful to the basic intuition of the Upanisads 
- the reality of the immutable Atman - they venture to formulate and explain the 
phenomenon of change in diverse ways. In their effort to interpret and systema¬ 
tise the Upanisadic ideas these schools developed different metaphysical struc¬ 
tures. 

Historically the oldest school, Sankhya is also from the philosophical point of 
view a system, with reference to which other systems could be fruitfully studied. 

It sets up a dualistic metaphysics, tracing the whole course of the universe to an 
interplay of two ultimate principles - Purusa and Prakrti. Purusa is the unchan¬ 
ging principle of consciousness, Prakrti being the ever-changing principle of 
matter. The Yoga system shares the Sankhya metaphysical attitude, but whereas 
the latter is atheistic, the former is theistic. The inherent tension present in the 
Sankhya metaphysical idea of two reals led eventually to the rise of the pluralistic 
metaphysics of the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools on the one hand and to the develop¬ 
ment of the philosophy of non-dualism via the monistic metaphysics of the 
Uttara-Mlmamsa on the other. In opposition to the atmavada of the Upanisads 
emerges the anatmavada of Buddhism, the second principal philosophico-reli- 
gious tradition of India. Buddhism, unlike Brahmanism, does not owe its origin 
to any sacred, revealed scripture. The tradition owes its inspiration entirely to 
the experience and teaching of Gotama, the Buddha, 1 the enlightened one. 

The Buddhist tradition stands out as a denial of the atmavada tradition, which 
expressed the nature of the ultimate real as unchanging and unchangeable. It is 
the negation of the atmavada , i.e. the substance-view of reality, which forms the 
very basis of Buddhist philosophy. Accordingly, the tradition is termed anatma- 
vada. It sets up an altoghether different metaphysics, where the ultimate princip¬ 
le is conceived as dynamic. Everything is in a state of flux. There is absolutely\ 
nothing which remains identical and immutable. Change is total. Thus, at the 
very point of departure it is clear, that the substance-view {atmavada) and the 


1 The birth of Buddha is generally accepted to be around 560 B.C. 



General background 


17 


modal view (anatmavada) are entirely opposed to one another in their approa¬ 
ches. One has a static view, the other a dynamic view - one champions the 
doctrine of soul, the other stands out for its no-soul doctrine. Brahmanism and 
Buddhism, thus set apart by their respective metaphysical standpoints, develo¬ 
ped in harmony with their original intuitions in the course of centuries, giving 
rise to many schools and sub-schools. 

We have mentioned briefly the six chief schools of the Brahmanical tradition. 
The entire history of Indian Buddhism, as it is known to us, can be broadly 
divided into three periods, 2 which are known as »the three swingings of the 
wheel of law« (< dharmacakrapravarttana ). 3 The first period is characterised by its 
pluralistic and realistic trend. The no-soul, no-substance theories dominate early 
Buddhism. As in the external world, so in consciousness, no stable, immutable 
element is to be found. But an important idea which emerges here is, that this 
process of perpetual becoming is not just haphazard, accidental, but governed by 
the law of »dependent origination (pratitya-samutpdda ). This doctrine is of 
such enormous significance, that some scholars go so far as to say that the entire 
history of Buddhism can be said to be nothing but an attempt to understand this 
doctrine of pratitya-samutpdda - the Buddhist theory of causality, which is 
intimately connected with the doctrine of momentariness. The early period is 
known as Hinayana Buddhism, the most important schools being Theravada, 
Vaibhasika and Sautrantika. The second phase of Buddhism saw the rise of the 
Madhyamika school. In contrast with the first period, which denied the substan¬ 
ce but accepted the reality of elements, this phase denies also the elements 
(dharma-nairatmya). The doctrine of pratitya-samutpdda is interpreted in new 
light - interdependence shows the unreality of the elements. The emergence of 
the idea of two truths ( samvrti-satya and paramartha-satya i.e. empirical and 
ultimate) is characteristic of the period. The last phase of Buddhism is embodied 
in the idealistic philosophy of the Vijnanavada school. It maintains the absolute 
reality of consciousness as against the Madhyamika thesis, that consciousness 
and object are relative to each other and therefore both are unreal. The Buddhist 
idea of time will be carefully studied in and through a critical exposition of the 
doctrine of momentariness in its various versions, as it appeared in the three 
phases of the history of Indian Buddhism. 

A study of the development of the Brahmanical and Buddhist schools disclo¬ 
ses a fascinating history of philosophical exchanges which shaped and stimulated 
their growth. They reacted against and were influenced by each other while 


2 cf. Stcherbatsky [63] p. 3-13; according to this author, the three periods are, roughly speaking, of 
a duration of 500 years each, beginning 500 B.C. 

3 cf. Bu-Ston [58] vol 2, p. 51-56. 


2 A study of time 



18 General background 

steadfastly holding on to their original intuitions. The tension between Brahma¬ 
nism and Buddhism, created-by their opposite points of departure, resulted in a 
fascinating and fruitful philosophical development. The Nyaya-Buddhist dispu¬ 
tes, for example, which went on for centuries, contributed immensely to the 
growth of logic. The profound influence, that Vedanta and late Buddhism exer¬ 
ted on one another, is also evident. 

In contrast to the atmavada and anatmavada , Jainism seeks to set up a yet 
different conceptual structure, namely anekantavada i.e. the many-sided view 
of reality. We shall refer to this metaphysical approach and follow up the Jaina 
treatment of the problem of time in sections IV (i) and (ii). 



Creation, causality, and time 


Each of the well-formulated systems of the different schools of Indian philoso¬ 
phy has its distinct theory of causality in support of and in accordance with its 
specific metaphysical structure. As we shall see on the following pages, it is 
within these conceptual schemes that one has to search for their respective views 
regarding the problem of time and detect also the philosophical motivation 
which makes these specific views necessary and indispensable for the alternative 
structures of metaphysics. However, before we venture to expose and analyse 
the critique of causality - which is a central problem of Indian philosophy and a 
key for the present investigation regarding the contending conceptual models of 
time-let us note the philosophical development of this idea in the earliest specu¬ 
lations about the creation of the universe. 

The philosophical wondering about the origin of the universe is found univer¬ 
sally, across the cultural boundaries. The consequent reflections, however, have 
given rise to ideas and views which have parallels and oppositions within as well 
as outside of a given tradition. As we focus our attention on the Indian scene, we 
find a variety of speculations attempting to account for the origin of the world. 
We pass aside the wealth of myths of the Puranas and select only those which 
have definite content for a philosophical analysis, leading to the discernment of a 
principle which is characteristic of the entire Brahmanical tradition. 

A number of views termed as akasmikavada - sudden occurrence, yadrccha- 
vada - haphazard happening, svabhavavada - naturalism, attributed to the 
materialists, must have been current at a very early date. Whatever subtle diffe¬ 
rences the views may have from one another, what they all seem to advocate in 
common is, that the occurrence of an event is without any invariable cause 
(niyata karana ), that the happening has no necessary dependence on anything 
else. These theories of spontaneous, indeterminable origination were obviously 
challenges offered by the sceptics against any explanation of the universe 
through the idea of causal operation. They claim that the occurrence of the event 
is not due to any cause but it is in the nature (svabhdva) of the event that it at all 
happens. These views, to which we find references in the Ufianisads ,* are treated 
by the Brahmanical philosophers as views of the opponent, and they have at- 


1 Kdlah svabhavo niyatiryadrccha - Svetdsvatara 1/2 and Susruta samhita, Sarirasthdna 1/11/3 



20 


Creation, causality, and time 


tempted to expose the fallacies, that are inherent in them. To give an example, 
Udayanacarya 2 - an exponent of the Nyaya-Vaisesika school - elaborately 
discusses the question, indicating why the idea of causality cannot be dispensed 
with. He argues to prove his thesis, that events cannot be said to occur hapha¬ 
zardly, b^y showing the absurdities that are involved in the opponent’s position. 
The fact, that an event occurs only at a particular time and neither always nor at 
any other time, can be accounted for only on the premise that there is a necessa¬ 
ry, antecedent cause to it. If the cause is absent, the event in question cannot take 
place. The events of the universe are tied to such a chain of causal operation, 
which accounts for rule and order in every case of occurrence and happening. 
The sudden occurrence as well as dissolution of an effect or why a particular 
effect does not occur all the time cannot be accounted for without the idea of 
causation. The above, in brief, points to why the opponents’ views are not 
acceptable. It is, however, also important to note that the Carvaka materialists 
did raise significant objections against the idea of necessary relation between the 
antecedent and consequent as the basis of the idea of causality. They point out 
that there is no ontological or logical connection between them. It is merely 
imagined to be there as a habit of thought. The details - which we omit - of 
Udayanacarya’s 3 answers, demonstrating the objective validity of causal opera¬ 
tion, make the notion of contingency ( kadacitkatva) clear. He points out that if 
the occurrence is held to be without a cause, it will lead to one of the absurd 
conclusions, viz. it is to be accepted as ever-present or never-present. Its contin¬ 
gency - that it is sometimes there and not always - cannot be accounted for. 
Thus, it has to be accepted that the contingent must have a cause. In other 
words, the question of causality arises only in relation to what is contingent. The 
eternal (ever-present), the fictitious (never-present) can be regarded as uncaused. 

This view - that the contingent is always caused - is supported both by the 
Sankhya 4 and the Vedanta systems. It is equally shared by the Buddhist 5 philo¬ 
sophers, in whose metaphysical structure, as we shall see, there is no room for 
any uncaused category of existent at all. Again Brahmanical philosophers, as 
they direct their enquiry towards discerning whether cause could be of the 
nature of non-being, unanimously deny the position. The view - termed asatka- 
ranavada - that being can come out of nothing - is held to be the opponents’ 
view and every system rejects it. The view, which is referred to in the Upanisads 

2 Nydyakusumdnjah 1/5 [24] 

Hetubbutinisedho na svanupakbyavidbima* ca svabhavarnana naivamavadbemiyatatvatab 

3 Nydyakusumanjali 1/4 [24]. 

4 Vacaspati Misra's commentary on Sdhkbya-Kdrikd 9 and 10 [11]. 

5 Pramdnavdrttika 3/34 by Dharmaklrti [53], nityam sattvam asattvam vd'hetor anydnapeksandt 

apeksdto bi bbdvandm kaddeitkatvasambhavab . 



Creation, causality, and time 


21 


and repudiated by all subsequent Brahmanical philosophers, is of interest as it 
gives the logic behind their cosmological speculations. We shall allude to some of 
the representative discussions from each system to indicate the philosophical 
argumentation for the unanimous repudiation of this idea by the Brahmanical 
tradition. 

This question is discussed at length in the Nyaya texts - such as the Nyaya 
Siitra 4/1/14 - 4/1/18 [15]. If it is held that the principal cause of this world is 
non-being but the world is a positive entity ( bhava-vastu ), the position is un¬ 
acceptable on the ground that the effect has to be of the same essence as the 
cause. 

The Sankhya argument as put forward by Vacaspati Misra in his Sahkhyatatt- 
vakaumudi - commenting on the 9th karika of Sahkhya-Karika - is that if a 
positive entity can be said to come out of nothing, »it would involve the absurd 
contingency of all things being produced at all places and at all times« [11]. In 
Nydyavarttikatatparyatlka [21] 4/1/14-15, a work of the same author, the dis¬ 
cussion has been taken up from the Nyaya standpoint. One of the significant 
arguments there is directed to show that nothingness, being devoid of any diffe¬ 
rence within itself, cannot give rise to any difference in effects. Moreover, an 
effect being in principle of the same essence as cause, it would remain inconcei¬ 
vable how nothing could give rise to being. 

Advaita Vedanta also refutes the position. Sankara, in his commentary on 
Brahma Sutra 2/12/26 [39], puts the question as to how to distinguish the non- 
being, which is taken to be the cause of different effects. If one admits of 
different kinds of non-being, non-being ceases to be non-being and becomes 
indistinguishable from being. Moreover, no account for rule and order in the 
causal operation can be given. Nothing could give rise to just about anything, in 
that case. 

The Brahmanical tradition, thus, repudiates the two positions, viz. that the 
contingent can be without a cause and that nothing can give rise to being. We 
shall note that in all philosophical and theological formulations the principle of 
ex nihilo nihil fit is never transgressed. This basic tenet of the entire Brahmanical 
tradition is expressed in the Bhagavad-Gitd II, 16 (see p. 130). 

If this brings us to a central point of agreement in the pattern of philosophisa- 
tion of this tradition, this is also a point of departure from where we can begin to 
trace the differences of the divergent metaphysical structures, with their distinct 
understanding of creation, supported by different formulations of the concep¬ 
tion of causality. Let us only briefly mention here, that arambhavada of Nyaya- 
Vaisesika is supported by their theory of causality, termed asatkaryavada; abhi - 
vyaktivada of Sankhya goes hand in hand with its theory of causality, termed 
satkaryavada; vivartavada of Advaita Vedanta goes with its distinct formula 



22 


Creation, causality, and time 


tion, termed satkdrana or sat-vivartavada. The meaning of the terms will be 
clarified shortly. The different positions will be stated here only in brief, as in the 
subsequent chapters we shall expose in detail the structure of each system and try 
to discern, how the philosophical treatment of the problem of time is harmoni¬ 
sed in each system with its total vision of reality. Let us take note of the different 
formulations about creation in the above mentioned systems. 

For Sankhya, all that is manifest could not have come out of nothing and must 
necessarily be caused. Moreover, all that is manifest was potentially existing in 
the cause prior to its actualisation. This manner of conceiving the causal opera¬ 
tion is the basis of the Sankhya theory of causality, termed satkaryavada. Crea¬ 
tion is manifestation, actualisation ( abhivyakti ) of that which is unmanifest (avy- 
akta). A philosophical enquiry leads the Sankhya philosophers to postulate two 
ultimate principles: matter, which is dynamic, and consciousness, which is ever- 
constant. Sankhya gives reasons for the postulation of these two principles, as 
we shall see later on. Here our task would be to focus on, what is - in this 
dualistic metaphysical structure - the philosophical account of the time-experi¬ 
ence. In this connection we shall go deeper into the specific understanding of the 
Sankhya theory of causality and note, how Sankhya could do without a concep¬ 
tion of time as an objective category of existence. The significant contribution of 
Yoga philosophy to the understanding of the problem of time will also be dis¬ 
cussed. 

If satkaryavada supports the Sankhya metaphysical structure, it is asatkarya- 
vada , the theory of causality of the Nyaya-Vaisesika system, which goes along 
with its doctrine of creation - arambhavada - and its pluralistic metaphysics. 
Here, the causal operation is conceived not as a mere actualisation of what was 
pre-existent in the cause - as in Sankhya - but as the cause giving rise to the effect 
which was non-existent (asat). In the arambhavada the effect is a beginning 
(drambha ) of that, which was not, before the causal operation. The system sets 
up a metaphysical pluralism and lists the ultimate metaphysical entities, in which 
figures time. To the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers it is only with reference to 
time as a category of existence, that one could at all talk about causal operation. 
In this system one comes across a mode of conceiving creation in time, and time 
is granted an ontological status. 

In the school of Advaita Vedanta there is to be found yet another conceptual 
pattern. Its philosophy of non-dualism goes with its understanding of causalit^ 
which is to be distinguished not only from the Nyaya-Vaisesika theory of asat - 
karyavada , but also from the satkaryavada of the Sankhya-Yoga schools. In the 
metaphysical scheme of Advaita-Vedanta there is no room for plurality and 
movement. This is expressed in the doctrine - termed as satkaranavada - where 
the cause alone is conceived as real, the effect has only an epistemic status. The 



Creation, causality, and time 23 

problem of creation, along with the problem of time, are reduced here to pro- 
blems of appearance. 

Thus, as this broad outline of the Brahmanical tradition with its three princi¬ 
pal and distinct conceptual structures points to, the conception of time propoun¬ 
ded in each system is motivated by and connected with their respective ideas of 
being, causality, etc. 

An analytical exposition of the different views of time, to be taken up system 
by system in subsequent chapters of this work, is intended to bring out the 
interdependence of philosophical concepts as the different schools formulate 
their distinct stands. 

This becomes more accentuated, when we come to consider the Buddhist 
philosophical stand, which formulates its theory of causality in a pattern, which 
is destructive of the Brahmanical metaphysical structures, leaving no room for 
any uncaused category. In the Buddhist conceptual structure there emerges a 
novel conception of being and time. It offers a bold challenge to the atmavada 
tradition, which is taken up by the adherents of the latter tradition - giving rise 
to lively philosophical exchanges. 

With this brief introduction as a guideline to the enormous wealth of ideas 
found in Indian philosophy, we shall now launch into a study of the problem of 
time in all its philosophical variations as found in the history of Indian thought. 
Speculations regarding this fundamental problem - a problem which has drawn 
wide attention of philosophers across cultures - know, as we shall see, of an 
enormous divergence from school to school, tradition to tradition. 




(i) On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


A study of the problem of time in Indian philosophy is of special interest in 
connection with the Nyaya and Vaisesika schools of thought. It is here that one 
comes across a bold realistic view of time. These schools, advocating a pluralistic 
metaphysics, focus on the reality of time as vital to their entire conceptual 
framework. Their philosophical stand regarding the problem of time is distinctly 
different from that of other schools, not only outside of the pale of the Brahma- 
nical tradition but also within it. A specific view on time has a necessary impact 
on many other issues of basic importance. Therefore, the philosophical challen¬ 
ges offered by the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of time is taken up by the other 
Brahmanical schools as well as by the various Buddhist schools as an important 
subject of controversy. 

Let us note that the main difference between the Nyaya and the Vaisesika 
schools lies in their orientation and emphasis. The Vaisesika deals with metaphy¬ 
sical questions, whereas Nyaya concentrates mostly on logic and epistemology. 

As one proceeds, one observes that the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers have 
studied the problem of time in its various aspects . 1 It is significant that a wide 
variety of questions, that suggest themselves to any investigation of the problem 
of time, such as what could be the ontological properties of time - granted it is a 
real per se - how is time known, how does it stand in relation to such basic 
problems as causality, motion and space, are raised and squarely faced by the 
philosophers of these schools, in harmony with their general conceptual frame¬ 
work of thinking. 

The importance of the problem of time in the context of the Nyaya-Vaisesika 
metaphysical pluralism comes into focus in all their philosophical assessments, 


1 cf. S. Bhaduri [28] p. 183-213. 



26 


On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


such as regarding the classification of the whole universe of reals, their concep¬ 
tion of causal operation, etc. 

It is interesting to observe that their classification of reals in two, as those 
which are products or perishable ( krtakas ) and those which are unproduced or 
imperishable ( akrtakas ), eventually has a significant bearing on time and its 
specific function. In his commentary on the Nyaya-Sutra [16], Vatsyayana deve¬ 
lops the Nyaya position by rejecting two distinct views. First he exposes and 
refutes the view that claims all is impermanent (4/1/25), since all is subject to 
production and destruction; secondly he discusses and denounces the view 
which maintains that all is eternal (4/1/29). Against the first position Vatsyayana 
urges, that there is no valid means of knowledge (pramdna), which can establish 
that the whole universe of reals is subject to production and destruction. He 
insists, to mention only a few from the list of entities (padartha) of their system 
of metaphysical pluralism, that such categories as soul {atman)> atoms (parama- 
nu), ether ( akasa ), space (dik), and time per se ( kala) (4/1/28) are never appre¬ 
hended by any valid means of knowledge as subject to production and destruc¬ 
tion. 

It is important to make the observation, in this connection, that although the 
other Brahmanical schools do not accept the Nyaya-Vaisesika list of plurality of 
eternal entities, they all agree with them in rejecting the position which denies 
the category of the eternal, i.e. the unproduced and indestructible, ontological 
status. The eternality of Atman lies at the very foundation of the Brahmanical 
tradition. 

Vatsyayana argues, that all that is liable to production must have a material 
cause, in which it inheres. The parts of a pot, for example, are its material cause 
as it is sustained by these constituents. But a substance ( dravya ) which is uncom¬ 
posite, partless ( niravayava ), must be eternal. In other words, one cannot main¬ 
tain that a substance is subject to production unless one can indicate its material 
cause and this is not possible in the case of time. The idea is later taken up in 
connection with the ontological properties of time. 

The above, in other words, amounts to saying that the whole universe of reals 
cannot be classified as non-eternal, since production and destruction cannot be 
attributed to all of them. Subsequently, Vatsyayana attempts the refutation^6f 
the second view - in two versions - which claims that all is eternal. According to 
the upholders of this view - in the first version - since all arises from the ultimate 
eternal elements (viz. the atoms of fire, earth, water, etc.) their products also 
have to be characterised equally to be eternal. 

In his commentary on Nyaya-Sutra (4/1/30) Vatsyayana elaborates the objec¬ 
tion that is raised against this position. Some entities, like a pot, are clearly 
apprehended as non-eternal. They are perceived as being subject to production 



On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


27 


and destruction. To say, that the products share the properties of the ultimate 
elements ( bhutalaksandkranta ), does not entail that the former are not different 
from the latter. The eternality of the elements cannot be attributed to that which 
is produced and therefore necessarily non-eternal. Moreover, if the product is 
not taken as a distinct whole (prthak avayavt) in contrast to the constituent 
parts, i.e. atoms, it could not even be perceptible, as the atoms are imperceptib¬ 
le. In other words, to be liable to production and to be composed of parts are 
indicative of the non-eternal. Thus, in brief, the proposition that all is eternal 
cannot be accepted. 

Again, the commentator exposes and refutes a second version (4/1/31) of the 
position claiming that all is eternal. The refutation of this view, attributed to the 
Yoga school, is highly significant as it brings forth the Nyaya philosophical stand 
in clear perspective and enables us to perceive the importance of the conception 
of time as a real per se in this system. 

In the Yoga analysis of change (parinzma) all the characteristics of abhivyak - 
tivada , 2 also shared by Sankhya, are present. Production and destruction are 
attributed only to the qualities ( dharma ), whereas the ultimate substance ( dhar - 
mi) is conceived as eternal, i.e. beginningless and endless. Moreover, the quality 
or the attribute {dharma) is not granted any independent existence. The Yoga 
conceptual pattern holds to the idea that the quality {dharma) is existent in the 
substance {dharmi) prior to production as well as posterior to its destruction. 
This is reflected in their theory of causality, satkaryavada . 3 It is, however, neces¬ 
sary to refer to the conception of laksarta parinama, 4 which has direct bearing on 
die temporal aspect of change. The conventional usages of past, present and 
future are taken as referring to the sub-latent, actual, and the potential states of a 
dharma. And in so far as the dharma has no distinct existence, it is in a relation 
of identity with the dharmi which is taken as eternal. 

The Nyaya philosophers take it up for refutation. 5 The criticism has immedia¬ 
te impact on their interpretation of the time-experience. It is pointed out that 
existence has its locus in the present time. If, as in the view of the opponent, a 
dharma is to be conceived as existent - prior to its production and posterior to 
its destruction - the idea and the experience of temporal sequence is violated. It 
is not possible to attribute past or future to such an entity. In other words, no 
meaningful reference can be made regarding such an entity in terms of produc¬ 
tion and destruction. They point to the fact, that production can be meaningful¬ 
ly ascribed to that, which now is (at present) but was previously not (in the past). 

2 The technical term for the Sankhya theory of evolution. 

3 See section II (i). 

4 See section II (ii). 

5 Nyaya-Siitra 4/1/25-4/1/33 [15]. 



28 


On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


Again an entity is non-existent posterior to destruction. If any entity in question 
is to be conceived as existent prior to its production and even subsequent to its 
destruction, there would be no account of any temporal order possible. This 
does not do justice to our experience and cannot, therefore, be accepted. 

Thus, both the propositions-viz. the one claiming universal impermanence 
and the other claiming eternality - are rejected. What experience as well as 
philosophical analysis testify to is that the universe of reals is to be classified into 
two: those liable to production and destruction (the non-eternal) and those 
which are not so (the eternal). 

Now, the immediate question that presses for consideration is whether pro¬ 
duction is possible without any causal operation. This is to be understood in the 
context of a series of objections brought up against the principle of causality by 
the sceptics and the materialists. Fully aware of the chaotic situation that would 
arise if there were no necessary, invariable relation between that which is produ¬ 
ced (the effect) and its cause, the philosophers of this school sought to answer 
these objections that are raised against the idea of causality. 

A summary review of these exchanges is found in Nyayakusumanjali written 
by Udayanacarya [24]. 6 If an event is to be taken as uncaused, it could happen at 
any time or all the time. The fact that it happens only at a determinate time and 
not otherwise indicates that there is an antecedent cause for its production. The 
coming into being of all that is contingent can be explained only on the basis of 
the assumption of a cause, which alone can account for the cessation of the prior 
non-existence of the effect. Again, no event can be said to produce itself, as a 
sceptic would have it. Its occurrence is determined by the cause, which is tempo¬ 
rally antecedent. Thus cause and effect are distinct from one another. Lastly, an 
event or an entity cannot come out of nothing. In that case, it could happen just 
about any time or at any place. 

The Nyaya and the Vaisesika philosophical literature contains detailed discus¬ 
sions on the problem of causality and its implications. Their theory of causality, 
refuting the other contending theories, discloses a conceptual structure, where 
the question of time reveals itself to be of vital importance. It is interesting to 
follow the steps of philosophical argumentation, which lead to the formulation 
of the idea of absolute time as a necessary presupposition for causal operation. 

The distinction between the eternal ( nitya ) and the contingent ( kaddcitk ^) is 
clearly formulated. An entity which is ever present or never present cannot be 
amenable to production or destruction. The contingent is precisely that which is 
not existent prior to its production and ceases to be after its destruction. 


6 1/4-5 and the prose commentaries thereon. 



On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


29 


The idea of non-existence, as we shall see in a later section, receives a pro¬ 
found philosophical treatment in these systems, leading eventually to controver¬ 
sies with other schools. It is only in the context of an objective time, that the 
category of non-existence enables one to put into perspective the idea of the 
contingent as caused. 

An entity like ether ( akdsa) is never non-existent, whereas an absolute non¬ 
entity like the hare’s horn {sasasrhga) is ever non-existent. The idea of cause is 
applicable only in connection with that whose existence is qualified by a previo¬ 
us non-existence. This prior non-existence is evidently a temporal reference, 
pointing to the fact that all causal operation is necessarily an event in time. No 
event is conceivable or possible without time as its receptacle {adhara). 

To deny time an objective reality is to confront a static universe, where there is 
no room for any change or movement. As time is indispensable for all that can be 
classified as contingent, it is characterised as the instrumental cause for the 
latter. 7 Again, contingency and causality imply each other. But for the idea of 
causal operation, entities are conceivable either as ever-existent or never-exi- 
stent. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers advocate asatkaryavada as their theory of 
causality. The theory is so called as the effect is conceived as non-existent {asat) 
prior to the causal operation. This is precisely the idea which differentiates this 
theory from the Sankhya theory of causality, viz. satkaryavada , where the effect 
is conceived as pre-existent, i.e. as potential in the cause. The metaphysical 
commitments of Sankhya and the impact of its causal theory on the conception 
of time are noted in the next chapter. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika rejects the conceptual pattern of abhivyaktivada of 
Sankhya and advocates a distinct doctrine of creation - viz. arambhavada. The 
doctrine puts in relief - which is supported by their theory of causality - that the 
effect is a new beginning {arambha) of that which was nonexistent {asat). 

In the Nyaya-Sutra [15], 4/1/15-4/1/24 and its commentary several views, 
attributed to the opponents, are reviewed and rejected in order to bring to light 
the logical and the philosophical advantages of the theory of causal operation 
that asatkaryavada stands for. The Nyaya and Vaisesika schools devote a consi^ 
derable study to the problem of causality in its different aspects. The metaphysi¬ 
cal, logical and epistemological implications that are drawn from their analysis 
lead to exchanges and controversies with the views of other schools. The schools 
of Nyaya and Vaisesika attempt at precise formulations of definitions, classifica¬ 
tion and the various problems that are connected with it like that of the plurality 
of causes, etc. 

7 cf. janyanam janakab kalak - Bhasa-Pariccheda by Visvanatha [22], 45. 



30 


On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


Here, however, their conceptual analysis of the problem of causality will be 
discussed only in so far as it is found to be pertinent to the understanding of their 
specific conception of time. 

The idea of causal operation, in the Nyaya-Vaisesika conceptual framework, 
presupposes time. 8 Even the very definition of cause is possible only on the 
premise of the ontological reality of time, with reference to which the idea of 
sequence of the cause and the effect can be meaningfully understood. 

Cause is defined as that which is a necessary and invariable antecedent to an 
event. 9 Here, the idea of antecedence has an immediate temporal reference. The 
effect succeeds the cause which evidently involves the idea of time. 

There are, however, other aspects to the cause besides its being temporally 
antecedent. All that precedes the occurrence of the event is not necessarily the 
cause. Moreover, for the effect to take place what is required is not only a single 
cause, but the sum-total of all the necessary causal antecedent factors (samagri) - 
an idea which is worked out in their classification of causes. Moreover, their 
distinct causal theory which denies the Sarikhya idea of the effect being nothing 
but a manifestation of the cause can be viewed and appraised, as will be seen, 
only in the light of the idea of time as a real per se. The production of the effect is 
its first conjunction with time. The causal operation does not consist of merely 
manifesting the potentially existent, as the Sarikhya claims, but of creating de 
novo what was non-existent. In other words, the occurrence of the effect is an 
event-in-time. Note that a necessary implication of the idea of causal operation is 
the idea of time as an ontological reality. 

What can, then, be said of time following the Nyaya-Vaisesika texts? 

The ontological properties of time are carefully enumerated in the philosophi¬ 
cal literature of both the schools. As no beginning or end of any event or entity is 
conceivable without reference to time, time itself is classed with the eternal 
(nitya) categories of existence (padartha ), i.e. is itself beginningless (anadi) and 
endless (ananta). Time in their pluralistic metaphysics occupies a distinct posi¬ 
tion. It is a real per se. It is a substance ( dravya ), since it has qualities like unity, 
conjunction, disjunction, etc. 

Moreover, it is argued that a substance (like time, space, etc.) which is uncom¬ 
posite ( niramsa) and does not presuppose any substratum (anasrita) for its Exi¬ 
stence has to be ascribed eternality. It is an independent real (svatantra). Tin(ie is 
further characterised as all-pervading (■ vibhu ), as it is in contact with all that is 
finite 10 and consequently regarded as the receptacle (adhara) of all that is contin- 

8 Vaisesika Sutra 2/2/11 [17] kdrane kaldkhyd. 

9 Ananyathasiddhaniyatapurvabhavitam karanatvam - Tarkabhasd of Kesava Misra [25]. 

10 Sarvamurtasamyogdnuyogitva - Bbasa-Pariccheda [22]. 



On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


31 


gent (kadacitka). The all-pervasive substances are never contained in anything 
( adheya ). It has no form ( amurta ). Time is uniform and ubiquitous. It has no 
parts ( niravayava ). As no change in terms of action or modification is concei¬ 
vable without time as its necessary frame of reference or possible if time were not 
an objective category of existence, it is itself inactive or unchanging ( niskriya ). 
The argument is that time, being a necessary condition for all movement and 
change, must itself be free from these attributes. 11 The ontological reality of time 
is an idea, which the schools vehemently insist on, as opposed to the idea of time 
as merely a subjective construction ( buddhi-nirmana ). 

Again, the schools insist that it is inherently unitary ( eka ) and indivisible 
(avibhajya). The idea of oneness and indivisibility of time leads to controversies. 
It is asked, if these were ontological properties of time, how should in that case 
the conventional usages of the plurality of time-divisions be accounted for. The 
Nyaya-Vaisesika answer will be given shortly. 

In this connection let us note that inference is the valid means of knowledge 
(pramdna ), according to the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, for proving the exi¬ 
stence of time. There is controversy amongst the Indian realists regarding the 
question of how time is known. According to one denomination of the Mimam- 
sa school time is amenable to sense-perception. We refer to the controversy in 
section I (iii). 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers maintain that time is inferred as the basis of 
such notions as priority (paratva ), posteriority (aparatva), of simultaneity (yau - 
gapadya) and succession ( ayaugapadya), of quickness (ksipratva) and slowness 
(i ciratva ). In other words, the above are the grounds ( lihga ) for the inference of 
the existence of time. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers reject the position which proposes solar 
motion to be the basis of such notions. Their analysis is as follows. When an 
object is taken as prior in relation to one and posterior to another, they ask, v^hat 
serves as the basis for the attribution of such qualities to a given object? If it is 
urged that one is connected with a larger number of solar revolutions than the 
other, the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers raise the problem that there is a direct 
relation of inherence between the sun and solar motion but not between the 
object in question and the sun, which are remotely separated from one another. 
Thus, there must be a common medium which can serve as a link between the 
two, and which, moreover, is capable of transmitting the quality of one to the 
other. Through the application of the method of exclusion it is argued, that no 


11 The above discussion is based on Sridhara’s Nyayakandalx [19] as well as Sankara Misra’s Upa- 
skara [17] b) p. 82-85, where detailed enumeration of the ontological properties of time is found. 



32 On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 

other substance can perform this specific function, leading to the inference of 
time. 

Thus it is urged, that solar motion can never substitute the function of time. In 
their analysis of quickness and slowness also the same fact is pointed to. The 
point of the argument is to focus on the fact, that not only solar motion but no 
motion whatever can replace time as an objective reality. 

To those who insist that solar motion can be the basis for such conventional 
usages, the question may be posed that in that case, how is one to account for 
such usages as »the sun is set since long«? The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers 
object to the answer that it is done with reference to other planetary motion, as 
this would lead to the fallacy of infinite regress. In other words, time as a real per 
se is to be accepted. 

Now, to go back to the question of time as unitary, the philosophers of these 
schools point out, that all the marks or grounds ( lihga ), which serve as basis for 
inferring time, point to it as one and the same objective real. The many ness of 
time, i.e. the conventional time-divisions, can be explained with the help of 
limiting adjuncts such as the solar motion etc. on the basis of the postulation of a 
singular, ubiquitous time. It is the actions which are past, present and future. 
These do not imply any inherent division or plurality in the time per se. 

Now, let us note some aspect of the problems relating to time and space - as it 
is conceived in these systems. 

In the Nyaya-Vaisesika metaphysical pluralism space, as time, is granted a 
distinct ontological reality. It is to be noted that some of the ontological proper¬ 
ties attributed to space are similar to those of time. Space, like time, is classified 
as a substance (< dravya ), as beginningless and endless, i.e. eternal ( nitya ), 12 as 
unitary ( eka ). 13 Moreover, the conventional plural usages of space, i.e. the space- 
divisions - despite that space per se is one - are accounted for as due to its 
relation to finite bodies, just as the so-called time-divisions are not intrinsic to 
time but are due to external adjuncts (upadhi). 

A question that has been raised is whether one cannot conceive of one eternal 
substance, instead of two, i.e. space and time, in this scheme of pluralistic 
metaphysics. To this the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers answer in the negative. 
The literature records the position, with an example from common experience, 
precisely to demonstrate that, despite their similar ontological characteristics, 
space and time cannot be reduced to one substance. There is no question of 
substituting one by the other, as their specific functions are absolutely distinct. 
In this connection, we refer to the Vaisesika-Suifa 2/2/6, where a clear analysis 


12 Vaisesika Sutra 2/2/13 [17]. 

13 Vaisesika Sutra 2/2/14. 



On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


33 


of the notions of priority and posteriority focuses on the distinct categories of 
existences that space and time are. That there is a clear-cut distinction between 
our awareness of temporal and spatial priority and posteriority is brought out by 
the example of the old man sitting nearer in point of space than a young man at a 
remoter distance, with reference to the same observer. The point that the examp¬ 
le seeks to make is that the properties of priority and postriority involved in the 
apprehension of oldness and youth cannot be attributed to space, which leads 
eventually to the inference of time as their instrumental cause. Space cannot, 
therefore, be a substitute for time, or vice versa. Their exclusive functions point 
to their distinct existences. 

In the Prasastapadabhasyam [20] also we find an elaborate discussion on the 
difference of the priority and posteriority attributed to space ( dikkrte paratvapa - 
ratve) - as the state of being far or near to a certain given point - from that which 
is caused by time ( kalakrte paratvaparatve) - as a happening previous to or later 
than a certain given event. 14 

The Nyayakandali too elaborates on the same distinction in order to focus on 
space and time as ultimate and independent reals. But the philosophical argu¬ 
mentation is more or less alike. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers are also aware of the inherent distinction 
between space and time regarding the fact that time has only one form of mensu¬ 
ration. This is to say that the temporal order of events is such that it does not 
allow for reversibility or inversion ( niyata ), whereas the spatial order makes 
room for it (aniyata). 15 It is also equally pertinent to note how space and time are 
considered in relation to motion. As the very presupposition of all movement, 
time and space are held to be without movement ( Vaisesika-Sutra 2/2/21). 
Things are in space and in time in terms of location and duration. Similarly, all 
motion is conceived as occurring in space and time. Space is taken as a non- 
inherential cause (asamavayi-karana) and time as the instrumental cause ( nimit - 
ta-karana) of all that is non-eternal. 

Thus, according to the realists, space and time are characterised as non-com¬ 
posite ( niravayava ), indivisible ( avibhajya ) and ubiquitous ( vibhu ). Things are 
in space and time ( adhara ), but space and time cannot be said to be contained 
(adheya) in anything. These two categories of existence are conceived as all- 
pervading ( sarvagata ) and as having no form ( amurta ). Space and time are classi¬ 
fied as substances, since they possess qualities which are enumerated in the 
Nyaya-Kandali as infinite extension, numerical unity, separateness, conjunction 
and disjunction. 

14 Cf. I'addegon [29], 

15 Upaskara (2/2/10) by Sankara Misra [17]. 


3 A study of time 



34 


On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


The conception of time in the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools as single, ubiquitous 
and all-pervading has been an important subject of controvesy. This idea of 
absolute time is challenged not only by the Buddhists and the Jaina philosop¬ 
hers, but has also been refuted by the Brahmanical philosophers belonging to 
Sarikhya, Yoga, and the Advaita Vedanta schools of philosophy. 

We have referred to these philosophical debates centering on various aspects of 
the problem of time in different sections of this work. These exchanges between 
the exponents and opponents of a given view bear witness to the keen philoso¬ 
phical subtleties involved in the analysis of the problem of time. 

At the end of this discussion let us note an interesting development, that took 
place regarding the problem of time in the history of the Nyaya-Vaisesika 
thought itself. 

Raghunatha Siromani, a reputed philosopher of the Neo-Nyaya (Navya-Nya- 
ya) school - a phase which witnessed remarkable development in the area of 
logic - questioned the traditional view of absolute time. In his work Padartha- 
tattvanirupanam [18] he challenged the entire categorical framework that was 
current in the old Nyaya-Vaisesika pluralistic metaphysics. He criticised the 
plural categories of existence and questioned their raison d’etre. It is in this 
connection that he also scrutinised the traditional understanding of time and 
space. 

It is to be recalled that the arguments for accepting both space and time, in the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophy, are analogous. The two substances, accepted as all- 
pervading and ubiquitous, are inferred as the basis of all temporal and spatial 
notions and judgements. The time-divisions (khan dak ala ) - as the old school 
insisted - could be accounted for only on the presupposition of the universal, 
indivisible, all-pervasive time ( maha-kala ) limited by qualifying adjuncts ( upad - 
hi). The spatial relations are also similarly explained on the basis of an all- 
pervasive, singular substance called space (dik) with the help of qualifying ad¬ 
juncts. 

Raghunatha Siromani does not see the necessity for accepting two all-pervasi¬ 
ve substances which require anyhow the limiting adjuncts in order to account for 
spatial and temporal divisions. He does not find enough justification for maintai¬ 
ning two separate categories of existence. Curiously enough, he seeks to reduce 
the traditional number of the ontological categories of the school by identifying 
space and time with God, which again is characterised as an all-pervading sub¬ 
stance. Thus, although the variety of judgements dependent on qualifying ad¬ 
juncts limiting an all-pervading substance remain the same in his conceptual 
system, the number of substances is reduced from three to one. He has also 
further curtailed the number of categories. But what concerns us, in this present 
investigation about time, is to note that although Raghunatha gives up the con- 



On the reality of absolute time - the Nyaya-Vaisesika view 


35 


ception of absolute time, he proposes a new category called moment (ksana). 

Here it is to be noticed, that he does not approve the manner, in which the old 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers defined moment with the help of motion. Here 
we refer to an important Nyaya text, viz. Siddhantamuktavali of Visvanatha 
[22], where it is exemplified how moment is defined with the help of motion 
with reference to the absolute time as its basis. 16 

Raghunatha Siromani rejects this procedure as inadequate. Evidently the who¬ 
le procedure is based on the notion of absolute time. He, therefore, critically 
demonstrates through a thorough examination of these definitions that the no¬ 
tion of moment cannot be validly accounted for in this manner. He rejects the 
idea of absolute time as a real per se, but sets up a new category »consisting of 
individuals which are not motions« ... but »which share the universal, repeatable 
property of momentariness« [18] p. 75. 

But this amendment of Raghunatha Siromani to abandon the conception of 
absolute time was not approved by the later adherents of the school. The idea of 
an absolute time as a real per se is necessitated by and is integral to the metaphy¬ 
sical pluralism of the school which advocated arambhavada as its doctrine of 
creation. 

Viewing the different philosophical stands regarding time within the Brahma- 
nical tradition, 17 the distinct character of the Nyaya-Vaisesika position is evi¬ 
dent. Their philosophical difference from the Sarikhya, Yoga and Advaita Vedan¬ 
ta is striking. The Sarikhya refusal to accept time as an independent category of 
existence is wholly untenable to the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers. In opposi¬ 
tion to Sarikhya, they firmly assert that no change, with reference to an entity or 

16 A technical analysis is given to account for the four moments of motion. They are: 

1) “The motion as limited by the prior absence of disjunction produced by that motion.” 
Suppose an individual x is moving from place y to place z. Then this first moment is the moment 
when x is just about to leave y. At this moment, the disjunction from y (which will be produced 
by the motion itself) has not yet come into being, so that there is prior absence of disjunction 
from y. 

2) “(The motion as limited by) the disjunction limited by conjunction with the earlier place.” 
The “earlier place” is place y, and the “later place” z. At this second moment x has not yet left y, 
but the trope called disjunction has arisen in x with reference to y. 

3) “(The motion as limited by) the prior absence of conjunction with the later place, which prior 
absence is limited by the destruction of conjunction with the earlier place.” x has now left y but 
has not yet arrived at z. 

4) “The motion as limited by contact with the later place.” x arrives at z. 

The old theory, then, was that any one of these four moments, which are motions, may be used to 
mark off the limits of a temporal span. (Quoted from [18] p. 73). 

Note that this text, long after Raghunatha Siromani, again adopts the procedure of the old Nyaya 
school. 

17 See chapters II and III. 



36 An exchange regarding the idea of present time (vartamdna kala) 

process, is either possible or conceivable without the ontological reality of time 
as its presupposition. The Sankhya view of the reality of change without time as 
a distinct, independent real is unacceptable to Nyaya-Vaisesika. All events are 
events in time, they maintain, and time is to be granted a separate existence. It is 
only with reference to the ontological background of time, that the reality of 
change - as production, persistence or destruction - can be conceived. 

The atomic view of time, propounded by the Yoga school, is equally unaccep¬ 
table to the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers. The conception of time as ubiquitous 
and indivisible is no mere construction of thought ( buddhi-nirmana ), as the 
Yoga school would have it, but an objective reality which is the very condition 
which makes the conventional time-divisions such as moment etc. possible. In 
other words, the idea of the reality of time as instant of the Yoga school is 
rejected by the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, for whom, on the contrary, what 
is basic to time is its indivisibility, its unity. 

To the Advaita Vedanta, on the other hand, the problem of time is a problem 
of appearance. The issue of the reality of time is just as much detrimental to the 
Advaita Vedanta metaphysics as it is essential to the Nyaya-Vaisesika. To the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers time is an all-pervading, ontological substance 
(i dravya ) which can by no means be reduced to or characterised as a problem of 
appearance. 

To sum up briefly, in vivartavada time is an appearance, i.e. it has no ontolo¬ 
gical reality; in parinamavada change is ontological, but time is no independent 
ontological category, whereas in arambhavada time is a real per se, the very 
presupposition of the reality of change. 


(ii) An exchange regarding the idea of present time (vartamana kala) 


In the context of the discussion regarding inference as a valid means of knowledf 
ge the question of the time-divisions is once more scrutinised. The mark o^ 
inference has reference to all the three time-divisions, past, present, and future - 
which enables one to, establish the universal relation ( vyapti ) between the major 
and the minor terms. 

An opponent - unidentified - raises an objection in which the validity of the 
three time-determinations is questioned. In the Nyaya-Sutra 2/1/39 and the 
commentary thereon the opponent’s objection is summarised as follows. It is 
held that there cannot be any present-time, as one could discern with reference 
to motion and space only a past and a future time determination. The example 



An exchange regarding the idea of present time (vartamdna kdla) 37 

given in support of this claim is that of a fruit loosened from its stalk falling 
towards the ground. The space traversed, through which it has fallen (patita - 
dbvan ), is associated with time-past, and the space which is yet to be traversed 
before it reaches the ground (patitavyddhvan ) with the time-future. There is no 
conceivable in-between these two, which could obtain a time-determination 
called time-present. Therefore the Nyaya claim, that inference can furnish 
knowledge of entities of all three times, cannot be accepted. 

To this objection the Naiyayika points out in reply, that if there were no 
present time, there cannot be past or future time either. The time-denominations 
of past and future are entirely dependent on the time-present. 18 The objection is 
based on a false appraisal of time by confounding it with space. Vatsyayana 
pointedly brings out, that spatial divisions are not the basis for the three time- 
distinctions. The different parts of space are co-existent and remain unaffected 
by the motion - here of the falling fruit - before and after its commencement and 
cannot therefore be taken as indicating time. It is action ( kriyd ) which reveals 
it. 19 The three time-distinctions are to be understood with reference to action. 
The past and the future actions of an entity - the fruit - are absent and they can 
be discerned only as relative to the action which is present. Action is, then, the 
basis for the conventional time-divisions. Its previous absence (prdgabhava ) is 
its future, and subsequent absence ( dhvamsdbhava ) is its past. The present is 
precisely the temporal denomination, when the action is actually apprehended, 
perceived; without this the future and past would be inconceivable. It is clear 
from this analysis, that the so-called temporal divisions do not pertain to the 
time per se, but it is only with reference to action that these can be meaningfully 
understood. Moreover, as Vatsyayana argues, 20 the past and the future of an 
action can never be understood except in relation to the present. Thus, the time- 
present is to be accepted. Moreover, he points out that not only action but the 
very existence of a thing manifests the reality of the present time. Any talk of 
existence of whatsoever would be meaningless without its locus as present time. 
Again, in the next section, 21 he launches an important argument in support of the 
objectivity of present as a time-determination. If time as present were not an 
objective fact, the very possibility of knowledge would be at stake. Perception, 
for example, presupposes sense-object contact (pratyaksam indriyarthasannikar - 
sajam), and that is not possible unless the object in question is present, that is, 


18 Nydya-Sutra 2/1/40 [15]. 

19 Nyaya-Bhasya 2/1/40 [16]. 

20 Nyaya-Bhasya 2/1/41. 

21 Nydya-Sutra and Nyaya-Bhasya 2/1/42 [15,16]. 



38 


An exchange regarding the idea of present time (vartamdna kala) 


Has for its locus the present time. 22 Again, a denial of the present time would not 
only lead to the impossibility of perception but as a consequence would rule out 
the possibility of even inferential (anumana) and verbal (sabda) knowledge 
which have their eventual basis on perception itself. This is, in brief, a significant 
point regarding the question as to why all the three time-determinations are to be 
acknowledged as objectively real. A further analysis again discloses that the 
time-divisions are external to time per se, the usages being made possible with 
the help of other qualifying factors ( upadhi ). The purpose of the entire philoso¬ 
phical argumentation is to disclose that no knowledge of any kind is possible 
without relating it with time. If perception reveals an object, the object by its 
very presence points to its temporal locus, which again leads to the inference of 
time per se as a category of existence 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika literature abounds in such exchanges where over and 
over again the reality of time is demonstrated through a refutation of different 
kinds of objections brought up against the position by its opponents. 23 Thus 
Vatsyayana argues for the objective reality of the present time in two distinct 
modes, through an analysis pertaining to action as well as to the very existence of 
an entity in question. He goes into further details regarding the conventional 
linguistic usages. The very existence of a substance bears witness to its present¬ 
ness - a fact necessarily referring to the present time. Thus, even when the 
qualities can be expressed in past and future terms, e.g. the jar was black, it is yet 
to be red, the substance does not cease to be present - as the conventional 
linguistic usage itself points to. 

He has also given an illuminating analysis of such conventional usages, where 
the verb is used in present tense even when referring to events of near future or 
past, e.g. instead of T came* - T come shortly’, instead of T will come’, ‘I am 
coming*. These legitimate grammatical uses are the subsidiary (gauna) uses of the 
present tense which presuppose that there is a principal ( mukhya) use bearing 
witness to the actual objective time-determination as present. 


22 An interesting discussion is documented by P.B. Tarkavagis in Nyayadarsana (in bengali) [30]^ 
Vatsyayana points out that a sense-object contact is the basis of ordinary perception ( laukikapra - 
tyaksa ), and such a contact is not possible with a past or a future object, but only with that which 
has its locus in the present time. But the question may be raised here, that in the case of such 
extraordinary perception (alaukikapratyaksa) - accepted by the Nyaya-Vaisesika school - as that 
of the Yogi, the future and the past object is also claimed to be perceived. In that case the former 
argument, that perceptibility points to the reality of present time, cannot be accepted as valid. 
Some of the Nyaya philosophers like Uddyotakara in reply to this admit the point contained in 
this objection, but nevertheless support the reality of the present as time-determination on the 
basis of the fact, that all cases of perception, so far as designated as an event ( kdrya ), have the 
present time as the locus, regardless of the temporal status of the perceived object.
