(iii) Is time perceived or inferred? - a debate amongst the Indian 
realists 


An interesting subject for a philosophical debate amongst philosophers, who 
otherwise fully agree about the reality of time as an objective category of existen¬ 
ce, center on the question as to how time is known. The adversaries in the 
controversy belonged to the MImamsa and the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools. To be 
more precise, one denomination of the MImamsa school, the Bhattas, held that 
time is perceived, whereas the other denomination of the same school, the 
Prabhakaras, 24 joined the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers in maintaining inference 
to be the valid means for knowing time. 

We find a discussion on the perceptibility of time in the work Sastradipika of 
Parthasarathi Misra. 25 The Bhatta MImamsaka stand, as is brought out here, 
points to the fact that all perceptual cognitions include as well the time of the 
perception. Misra takes recource to such common experiences expressed in state¬ 
ments such as ‘we are perceiving the present pot’, or ‘we are perceiving it from 
the morning’, as clearly bearing witness to the fact that time is perceived. It 
would be impossible for us to use such expressions as now, then, in our percep¬ 
tual cognition if time were not also perceived along with the perception of the 
object. Just as the referrents of the words pot etc. are accepted as objects of 
perception, so, he maintains, are the referrents of such words as ‘now’, ‘then’ 
etc. These words refer to nothing other than the time-content of the perception 
itself. The Bhatta MImamsaka point is to focus on the fact, that the temporal 
dimension of all our perceptions would remain unaccounted for without the 
contention that time is amenable to perception. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika objection is that time cannot be said to be perceived, as it 
has no sensible qualities such as colour, form etc. It can only be inferred. 

To this the Bhatta MImamsaka reply is that the objection is based on a fallaci¬ 
ous argument. That, which is amenable to perception, need not necessarily 
possess such qualities as colour etc., although it is a fact that a coloured object 
can be perceived. In other words, such sensible qualities are not the criteria of 
perceptibility. 

The Bhatta Mlmamsakas, however, acknowledge that time as such is never an 
object of perception, but it is perceived always as a qualification of sensible 
objects. This is to say that time is perceived as a qualifying element (visesa guna) 


24 Prabhakara MImamsa used the same conceptual model of time as that of Nyaya-Vaisesika. We 
refer to Prakarana Pahcika by Salikanatha Misra, p. 155, [34], see also [36], [37]. 

25 [33], p. 45, 1/2/5. - kalapratyksanirupanam. 



40 


Is time perceived or inferred? 


and never independently of the sensible object. Thus we perceive events as slow, 
quick etc., which involve a direct reference to time. 

In the opinion of the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, however, perception can 
never disclose the ontological properties of time as unlimited, beginningless and 
endless, indivisible and ubiquitous. A directly perceived time would only point 
to a limited, divided time, which is not the time per se, but a conventional 
temporal usage determined by such extraneous factors as the solar motion etc. In 
other words, time as an ontological real can never be an object of perception; 
inference is to be admitted as the valid means for knowing it. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers have worked out a series of inferences 
towards the establishment of time as an independent, objective category of 
existence. The grounds {lingo) of the inferences are such notions - indicated in 
the Vaisesika-Sutra 2/2/6, also in Nyaya-Kandall of Sridhara - as priority (parat- 
va ), posteriority ( aparatva ), simultaneity (yaugapadya ) and succession {ayauga- 
padya) y quickness {ksipratva) and slowness {ciratva) etc. etc. 

Time per se is established through inference as the very basis which makes 
such notions possible, as mentioned above, and is also considered as a cause for 
all such entities, to which production and destruction can be attributed. 

The issue regarding the perceptibility or inferrability of time as a philosophical 
debate acquires an interesting flavour, as the opponents basically agree on the 
objectivity of time as an ontological real in their respective realistic conceptual 
structures. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika position regarding the issue is challenged by the Bhatta 
Mlmamsaka Narayana. In his Mdnameyodaya y 26 a notable Mlmamsa work, the 
question is discussed once more. The aim of the discussion is to refute the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika claim, that time is inferred on the basis of such notions as 
simultaneity etc. This, the Bhatta Mlmamsaka points out, cannot be accepted as 
a valid ground for inference. He points to such experiences as, for example, 
‘Devadatta and Yajnadatta came simultaneously’, or ‘the son came late’ and raises 
the question whether such awarenesses, obtained by perception, can be denied 
to have time as their essential component. The point is to bring out that such 
direct, immediate awarenesses as simultaneity or lateness cannot be ascertained, 
as in these examples, through anything but sense-perception. ‘Devadatta’, ‘Yaj- 
nadatta’ or ‘son’ are related directly to time, and the latter is perceived along with 
the substantives which it qualifies. 

The Bhatta Mlmamsaka points out that time cannot be said to be inferrable, as 
the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers would have it, precisely because nothing, 
other than time, is the very content of the notion of simultaneity etc. The latter 


26 [35], Kdnka 85. 



Is time perceived or inferred? 


41 


cannot be again used as the ground ( hetu) for inferring time. This would lead to 
the fallacy of self-dependence ( atmasraya ). Moreover, they point out that time 
could not be inferred on the basis of any other ground either. 

Briefly, the awareness of simultaneity etc. is produced by sense-organs, which 
have time for their content. This indicates that time is itself a content of sense- 
perception, which invalidates the claim of the inferrability of time. 

The Bhatta-Mlmamsa insistence on perceptibility of time as corroborated by 
everyday experience is met by some of the distinguished Nyaya-Vaisesika philo¬ 
sophers, who adopted a novel approach to the question. 

Sridhara in his Nyaya-Kandali [19] 27 takes up the question. Again in Jayanta 
Bhatta*s Nyaya-Manjari [27] 28 we find reference to this exchange, put in his 
characteristic brilliant manner. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers meet the problem by pointing out, that 
time (as much as space) cannot be established as objects of ordinary perception 
(laukikapratyaksa). As we find in Nyaya-Kandali y it is their contention that to 
establish a substance through visual perception, it must have a limited dimension 
and have colour. But time is unlimited, infinite, and it has no manifest colour or 
form (udbhutarupavattvam). The sense of touch can also, according to the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, be said to perceive substances, but that presup¬ 
poses that they possess manifest tangible quality. Evidently, time cannot be 
established in this way either. 

Therefore, the so-called perception of ‘the present pot* is analysed by the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers in the following manner. They urge that it is a 
case of, in Nyaya-Vaisesika terminology, extra-ordinary perception ( alaukika - 
pratyaksa ), classified as jndnalaksana. 29 This is the type of perception where the 
perceived object is presented to the senses through a previous knowledge of the 
same, as for example in the case of the perceptual judgement ‘I see the fragrant 
sandalwood*. Here, the visual perception of fragrance is accounted for as due to 
a previous knowledge of it in the past experience. Thus, Sridhara explains the 
perception of ‘the present pot* as being composed of two elements. Firstly, ‘the 
pot* is the object of ordinary perception, whereas the second factor, i.e. the time- 
element indicated by the word ‘present*, is being derived from previous know¬ 
ledge of time resulting in the extraordinary perception of ‘the present pot*. In 
other words, Sridhara agrees with the general Nyaya-Vaisesika contention that 
the knowledge of time is inferential. The sense-perception of time as a qualifying 


27 Kdlaprakaranam (p. 155-161). 

28 part 1 y pramanaprakaranam (p. 123-127). 

29 It is defined as visayi yasya tasyaiva vydpdro jnanalaksanah in Bbdsd-Pariccheda by Visvanatha 
[ 22 ]. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika deals elaborately with the problem of perception. 



42 


Is time perceived or inferred? 


element, of the object, as the Bhatta MImamsaka would have it, is analysed by 
him as based on the previous inferential knowledge of time. 

Jayanta Bhatta, the reputed advocate of the school, presents the view of the 
Oncological reality of time as inferrable. The discussion is put in the form of an 
exchange first with the principal opponent, the Buddhists, who deny the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika conception of time as an all-pervading, ubiquitous, ontological catego¬ 
ry of reality. 

The Buddhist objection to the inferrability of time is summarised as follows. 
They point out that it is not possible to establish the universal relation ( vydpti ) 
between the major (sadhya) and the middle term ( hetu ) in the said inference - 
that is, between time and such marks as quickness, slowness, simultaneity etc. 
This is not grounded in any previous perceptual experience. 30 

Moreover, the usages of ‘now 5 , ‘then 5 etc. do not point to the existence of an 
indivisible time as substratum. These can be accounted for as pertaining to a 
sequence of acts. Again, such planetary movements as solar motion do not 
indicate any separate existent called time. Moreover, if time is accepted as an 
indivisible, unitary real per se, the usages of past, present and future will be 
impossible. 

Here Jayanta Bhatta presents the Bhatta Mlmamsa view, seeking to establish 
the reality of time by immediate perception. They argue that quickness, slow¬ 
ness, simultaneity are not merely attributes of acts, but that they also indicate a 
substance as the substratum of these notions. And that is time. Without the 
ontological reality of time one could not account for the awareness of ‘the 
present pot* in contrast to ‘this pot\ The attribute ‘present’ is indicative - as a 
perceptual datum - of the existence of time. In other words, the purpose of 
presenting the Bhatta Mlmamsa view is to refute the Buddhist contention, that 
the conventional temporal usages do not require the postulation of time as a 
distinct real. 

Now, as the Nyaya-Vaisesika is in full agreement with the Mlmamsa in their 
view regarding time as a distinct category of reality, the dispute centers around 
the issue of what is the valid means of knowing time. The Nyaya-Vaisesika 
challenges the Bhatta Mlmamsa view, that the ontological reality of time as 
ubiquitous, uncomposite, can be established by perception. The arguments on 
either side presented in this connection are similar to those found in Sridhara’s 
Nydya-Kandali. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika maintains that time as a substance could be established 
by visual perception, only if form/colour ( rupa ) could be attributed to it. But 


30 This is a requirement for valid inference. In the case of inferential knowledge ‘this hill is fiery’, 
‘smoke’ is the lihga (mark) which is previously perceived as associated with fire. 



Is time perceived or inferred? 


43 


since it is not the case, inference is to be acknowledged as the valid means of 
knowing it. 

To this the Mlmamsaka retort is, that the atoms (paramanu ), according to 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, have form and yet they are not amenable to 
perception. This goes to indicate that ‘having form’ itself is no criterion for 
perceptibility. In other words, the dictum that only a substance having a sensible 
form/colour can be visually perceived, as the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers 
would have it, is itself in contradiction with experience and is, therefore, unte¬ 
nable. The judgement ‘I see the pot now’ would not be possible without contact 
with the visual organs and does not need any other means of knowledge as its 
basis. It is however so, the Mlmamsaka agrees, that time cannot be perceived 
independently, but only in its attributive capacity. But that it is perceivable is a 
fact of experience, and no law can invalidate it. 

To this the Nyaya-Vaisesika reply is, that if both the substance and its attribute 
can be shown to be separately amenable to perception, it could also be maintai¬ 
ned that the qualifying element is perceptible along with the substance. But on 
the strength of being alone an attribute of a substance, not otherwise indepen¬ 
dently perceivable, it cannot qualify itself as a perceptual datum. Such being the 
case of the perception of time, always as an attribute - which is itself the MI- 
mamsa contention it cannot be established as an independent real by percep¬ 
tion alone, but on the basis of inference. Moreover, the existence of time is not in 
jeopardy because it is not amenable to visual perception, it is conclusively pro¬ 
ved through inference. 

The so-called perceived time as a qualifying element is, of course, a case of 
extra-ordinary perception - an idea to which we have already referred. But in all 
cases, it is only inference that can establish the ubiquitous, unitary, all-pervading 
time. 



II 


(i) Time as aspect of concrete becoming - the Sankhya view 


The conceptual frameworks of the Sankhya and the Yoga schools have many 
essential features in common. There are, however, divergences regarding certain 
philosophical issues, other than the well-known distinction between Sankhya 
atheism and Yoga theism. 

In their treatment of the problem of time - although these schools operate 
within a basic ontological structure which is common to both - a careful investi¬ 
gation shows certain subtle philosophical differences. 

The common point of agreement between the two schools is their denial of the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika view of a unitary time as an absolute and objective existent, as 
one among the ultimate real entities constituting a metaphysical pluralism. It is 
interesting to note in this connection, that time does not figure in the list of the 
categories of existence (tattvas) of either Sankhya or Yoga. 

For an understanding of their philosophical formulations regarding the pro¬ 
blem of time it is indispensable to have an overall idea of the specific metaphysi¬ 
cal frame of the systems, along with their distinct theory of causation, termed 
satkaryavada. Propounding a metaphysical dualism, these schools operate with 
two ultimate principles which are conceived as having no beginning or end and 
as being independent of one another. 

The whole course of the universe is attributed to an interplay of these two 
principles - Purusa and Prakrti, to use Sankhya terminology. The dichotomy of 
matter and consciousness is expressed in the conceptions of Prakrti and Purusa. 
Purusa, which Sankhya identifies with consciousness, is conceived as uncompo¬ 
site, without attributes ( gunas ), free from all movement and change. Prakrti, on 
the contrary, is the ever-active, ever-changing principle of matter, 1 constituted of 


1 This is often given as the English equivlent of the term Prakrti, some people prefer energy, some 
nature. In any case, Prakrti is to be understood as the subtle unmanifest, unconscious, dynamic 
principle, which is the cause of the manifest, gross experiencable world. 



Time as aspect of concrete becoming - the Sankhya view 45 

the three attributes ( gunas ). Both the principles are, however, uncaused and 
indestructible. 

In the Sankhya-Kdrikd of Isvarakrsna along with its lucid commentary by 
Vacaspati Misra, 2 the philosophical argumentations for postulating these two 
principles, their nature and functions are elaborately worked out. These contain 
illuminating documentation of the Sankhya conceptions regarding the major 
philosophical issues, in the light of which we have to seek to understand and 
interpret the Sankhya position regarding time. 

We shall therefore, following the Sankhya philosophical literature, note the 
principal ideas developed in their distinct mode of conceiving these two princip¬ 
les and then attempt to grasp their philosophical implications about the problem 
of time as such. The clue to the Sankhya position on time is to be found in an 
understanding of the points of similarities and dissimilarities - which are careful¬ 
ly noted and enumerated in the literature as indicated below - between all that is 
manifest and Prakrti as the ultimate, unmanifest principle as well as between 
Prakrti and Purusa. The structure of the system developed through an elabora¬ 
tion of these concepts offers interesting contrasts with those of other systems of 
Indian philosophy. 

The Sankhya concern for detecting a principle, which lies behind all manife¬ 
stations whatsoever, leads to the postulation of Prakrti as an unmanifest i.e. pre- 
empirical reality, accounting for all experiences inclusive the processes involved 
in all actualisations from a latent or potential state. This involves the Sankhya 
conception regarding matter as an independent, objective, dynamic principle and 
a development of a theory of causality. 

The distinction between the manifest and the unmanifest is, therefore, clearly 
indicated in Sankhya-Kdrikd 10, where we find that the differences are mentio¬ 
ned thus: the manifest is caused, transitory, limited, whereas the unmanifest 
Prakrti is uncaused, eternal and all-pervasive. 3 

The Sankhya philosophers also note the similarity between all that is manifest 
and the unmanifest Prakrti, in that they are constituted of the same essence, i.e. 
of the three gunas - a conception, which we shall discuss shortly. Sankhya 


2 The Tattvakaumudi , transl. by G. Jha, [11]. 

3 Hetumad anityam avyapi sakriyam anekam asritam lihgam. Savayavam paratantram vyaktam , 
viparitam avyaktam . 



46 


Time as aspect of concrete becoming - the Sankhya view 


conceives of Prakrti as a tri-unity of gunas 4 - sattva , which is described as the 
illuminating, rajas as the active and tamas as the restraining forces, which 
through their mutual co-operation, subjugation and support give rise to the 
common objective world. This idea of an independent, objective world is to be 
contrasted with the view advocated by subjective idealism. Leaving aside the 
elaborate discussions regarding the nature and function of the gunas , we shall 
turn to the basic question as to why Sankhya comes to postulate Prakrti as the 
ultimate, uncaused, unmanifest, eternal principle supporting the entire course of 
evolution. Prakrti is dynamic, matter which evolves incessantly. It is the materi¬ 
al, formal and instrumental cause of the universe. 

It is here that one comes across the Sankhya views about such basic philoso¬ 
phical issues as the conception of causality, involving distinct conceptual models 
for understanding change, origination and destruction. To retrace the principal 
philosophical steps, which finally lead to the postulation of Prakrti, one has to 
turn to the Sankhya theory of causality, termed as satkaryavada. This theory is 
of central importance to the system. All that is manifest, Sankhya argues, is 
dependent (paratantra) on a cause. This is what underlies the constitution of all 
that is contingent, that its origin indicates a causal dependence. To be without a 
cause would imply - as Vacaspati Misra points out in his commentary - that the 
entity in question is either eternal or fictitious. The understanding of the mani¬ 
fest as caused, in Sankhya, leads finally to the postulation of Prakrti as the 
unmanifest, uncaused principle. 

It is important to note, that the Sankhya understanding of time as an aspect of 
concrete becoming and why time does not figure as a category of existence 
(tattva) in this system are questions, to which answers have to be sought in this 
entire metaphysical frame of the system along with the specific theory of causali¬ 
ty which it advocates. The ground for rejecting the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception 
of absolute time as an independent, objective reality can also be better compre¬ 
hended, when one takes into consideration the entire Sankhya perspective. 

It is to be noted that the idea of concrete becoming (parindma ) is central for all 
questions regarding temporal sequence in Sankhya. In order to understand the 

4 The term guna as found in the Sankhya literature is not easily translatable in English. The three 
meanings of the word guna in Sanskrit are i) quality, ii) subordinate or inferior and iii) string, rope. 
The difficulty in rendering the term as quality in the Sankhya context is that it tends to distort the 
Sankhya view if Prakrti is held to be a substance with these three qualities. G. Jha [11] translates 
it as attribute, S. Das Gupta in Yoga Philosophy [14], p. 70 calls them the ultimate reals. 

Vacaspati Misra [10], commenting on the term, points out that they are so-called literally subsidi¬ 
ary or secondary ( gauna ), as they exist for the sake of others (parartha ) i.e. for the Pumsas. It 
may also be interpreted, following the meaning of the term as string, as the gunas bind the Purusa to 
the transitory object. 



Time as aspect of concrete becoming - the Sarikhya view 


47 


Sarikhya view, that the principle of change is ultimate and cannot be reduced to 
or derived from any static, unchanging principle, it is necessary to refer to the 
Sarikhya conception of homogeneous manifestations ( sadrsa-parinama ) of 
Prakrti. 

To Sarikhya, the movement in Prakrti, which brings forth the heterogenous 
categories of creation with all its multi-form, presupposes a primordial move¬ 
ment which persists even when the heterogenous manifestations cease, i.e. the 
state of cosmic dissolution. This primal movement that is unceasingly present in 
Prakrti is due to the inherent restless nature of the gunas. Vacaspati Misra in his 
commentary on the Sankhya-Kdrikd 16 emphasises this ever-changing character 
of Prakrti- the three gunas do not abandon their intrinsic nature and continue 
their homogeneous modification even in the state of cosmic dissolution (pratisar- 
gdvasthd). No distinct evolute emerges from this homogeneous modification as 
the gunas do not combine. Yet the process of self-reproduction {sattva giving 
rise to sattva , etc.) goes on. Dynamicity being the very nature of gunas , they can 
never remain unmodified, even for a moment. 5 The creative movement of 
Prakrti, on the other hand, brings forth heterogeneity ( visadrsaparinama ). 

This intrinsic dynamic nature of Prakrti, disclosed in the conception of sadrsa- 
parinama , is evidently pertaining to the transcendental aspect of the ever-active 
Prakrti. In other words, the persistently active character of Prakrti - when the 
gunas modify incessantly without combining and therefore being unable to bring 
forth heterogeneous evolutes - does not involve any idea of empirical time- 
phases. Some scholars, for this reason, have characterised Prakrti as the very 
personification of time. 6 To put it differently, the principle of change embedded 
in Prakrti, as the tri-unity of gunas in a state of homogeneous modification, is a 
conception of time in its transcendental aspect. This is an attempt to conceptuali¬ 
se the incessant movement of Prakrti in its pre-empirical state. 

The analysis, at this point, leads to the question, which is of great interest 
here, whether the process of objective movement is enough to account for time- 
experience. It is indeed important to emphasise again, that to Sarikhya the ever- 
changing Prakrti can never know itself as changing. On the other hand Purusa, 
which knows change, is itself free from all movement and change. It is conceived 
as ever-constant ( kutastha ). If Prakrti is rightly characterised as a personification 
of time, Purusa is the timeless principle. Both remain, however, according to 
Sarikhya, beginningless and endless. This is an important issue which will be 
taken up later on in section VII (i) entitled ‘time and consciousness’. 


5 parindmasvabhdvd hi gurtd ndparinamyya ksanamapyavatisthante. 

6 e.g. Madhavacharya in his commentry on Parasara-Samhita , cf. [5]. 



48 


Time as instant - the Yoga view 


To sum up, Sarikhya does not postulate any conception of time as independent 
of change. Change, on the other hand, is not conceived in abstraction, but as a 
concrete becoming. This again is further analysed, as we have noted, into homo¬ 
geneous, being the basic and fundamental, and heterogeneous - involving the 
idea of a blending and combination of gunas. The notions of temporal sequence, 
as past, present and future, are to be understood in the perspective of the 
heterogeneous manifestations of Prakrti. The Sarikhya finds it superfluous to 
postulate an empty time as a real per se. 

Vacaspati Misra, in his commentary on Sdnkhya-Kdrika 33, observes that in 
order to account for the temporal usages, if one has to first postulate an indivi¬ 
sible unitary time as a real per se and then adjuncts like action, solar motion, etc. 
(as the Vaisesika philosophers do), it would simplify the matter, if the conventio¬ 
nal time-divisions are accounted for with the qualifying adjuncts alone. In other 
words, it is unnecessary to admit of a separate category called time. 7 

A direct reference to the problem of time is scarce in Sarikhya literature where, 
however, the problem of becoming undergoes minute philosophical scrutiny. 
The reason is clear, Sarikhya understands the temporal phases with reference to 
the creative movement of Prakrti. There is no time apart from this concrete 
movement. The idea of temporal sequence is intermingled .with the idea of 
objective succession in Prakrti. The primordial movement in Prakrti does not 
involve any idea of heterogeneity, with reference to which empirical time-phases 
can be meaningfully understood. Thus, in the pre-empirical aspect - described as 
the state of cosmic dissolution - Prakrti is to be taken not only as a principle of 
unconscious matter but as the principle of time itself in its transcendental aspect. 
In the conception of the dynamic Prakrti, Sarikhya combines time and matter in 
the same principle. 


(ii) Time as instant - the Yoga view 


Contrary to the Sarikhya school, where involvement with the problem of time is 
implicit and therefore open to a certain extent to interpretation and construction, 

7 Again, Vijnanabhiksu, a notable Sankhya commentator, advances a slightly different argument 
for the Sankhya refusal to accept time as a real per se. He says, in his Samkhyapravacana Bhdsya [12] 
2/12, that time is nothing apart from the ubiquitous dkasa or ether, which is an evolute of Prakrti. 
The same qualifying adjuncts, which are said to account for time-divisions, can do so with reference 
to dkasa , making it unnecessary to postulate a separate ubiquitous substance called time. 



Time as instant - the Yoga view 49 

we find in the Yoga Sutra of Patanjali and in the subsequent philosophical works 
of the commentators profound, pertinent discussions which have direct bearing 
on the problem of time. 

The basic ontological structure of the Yoga school of philosophy is akin to that 
of Sankhya. The system occupies itself with the question of time and develops its 
specific stand on the reality of the instant ( ksana) and the merely subjective 
construction of sequence (krama). These ideas, which are pronounced and expli¬ 
citly worked out in the system, support the Sankhya denial of the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika conception of an indivisible, unitary time as an independent category 
of existence. 

The distinct philosophical features of the conceptual model for understanding 
time in the Yoga school provide a striking contrast with the Nyaya-Vaisesika 
view, emerging in the Brahmanical tradition, but also offers points of subtle 
difference with the Buddhist view. Separate sections are devoted to such studies 
and observations. 8 

As in Sankhya, so in Yoga the treatment of the problem of time is closely 
related to their specific conception of becoming (parinama ). The problem of 
becoming, both for Sankhya and Yoga, is a metaphysical problem which cannot, 
by any means, be reduced to a problem of appearance - as in the system of 
Advaita Vedanta. The Yoga literature - specifically the Yoga Sutra of Patanjali 
[^0] (III, 13-15) and all the important commentaries and subcommentaries there- 
/Ibn - devotes considerable attention exactly to the diverse aspects of this pro¬ 
blem. The analytical exposition of their conception of becoming, as we shall see, 
clearly indicates why in the Yoga metaphysical structure there is no necessity for 
admitting time as an objective category of existence - a position held and defen¬ 
ded by the Nyaya-Vaisesika school - and also brings out its difference from the 
Buddhist understanding of the universal instantaneous character of being. The 
conventional usages of the three times are accounted for in accordance with the 
idea of change inherent in the heterogeneous becoming of Prakrti, which is an 
essential feature of the Yoga metaphysical structure. 

The Yoga Sutra III, 13 focuses on the three modes of immanent change, which 
in Yoga terminology are termed as the dharma -, laksana - and avastha-parinama. 

Change is incessant in the ever-active Prakrti - the ultimate substance ( dhar- 
min). When the change is perceptible as a change of quality in a substance it is 
called dharma-parinama. 

Laksana-parinama refers to the changes involved in the different stages of the 
mutation of the entity in question in terms of its temporal phases, past, present, 
and future. The potential state is designated as future, the manifested is the 


8 See sections VII (ii), (iii). 


4 A study of time 



50 


Time as instant - the Yoga view 


present and when it is back in its sublatent state, it is the past phase of the thing. 
For the purpose of the present undertaking the most significant is this concep¬ 
tion of laksana-parindma, which directly involves the notion of temporal phases. 
The Yoga conception of instant ( ksana ), as we shall see shortly, is to be grasped 
in the context of this analysis. 

That aspect of change is avasthd-parinama, which involves the notion of new 
and old, i.e. pointing to the mutations affecting the constitution of the entity in 
question. Let us take the example of the earth as the substance ( dharmin ), which 
can be gradually transformed into a jug. The change of quality through the 
different stages of the mutation of the earth constitutes its dharma-parinama. 
When the temporal aspect of the change is emphasised as in the statement that 
the earth which is at present a jug was in its past phase a lump, it is laksana- 
parindma. Again, when the changes in the same jug lead to its characterization as 
new or old, it is called avastha-parinama. It is to be noted that the earth as the 
substance ( dharmin) remains in and through its various mutations. 

The above classification of the three parinamas or changes gives the Yoga 
understanding of the problem a distinct pattern, which stands out in contrast to 
the Buddhist formulation of the problem, as will be seen later. 

Let us observe that the formulation of the problem of change here is in perfect 
consistency with the Saiikhya-Yoga metaphysical stand and its theory of causali¬ 
ty, viz. satkaryavada. Emergence or birth does not entail for Sarikhya-Yoga the 
coming into existence of the non-existent, just as death or destruction does not 
imply the annihilation or passing into non-existence of the existent. The under¬ 
standing of change or becoming is in full conformity with the dictum of ex nihilo 
nihil fit. The Yoga conceptual model becomes explicit in Yoga Sutra III, 14, 
which states in unambiguous terms that the substance ( dharmin) remains and 
persists through all the three stages, past, present and future, characterised as the 
quiescent, the uprisen and the indeterminable. 9 Thus, it is to be noted that 
temporal phases are attributed to the external aspects, i.e. qualities ( dharma ) of 
the substance, which is itself conceived as persistent through all these phases. 

Let us now note the conception of instant in this system. The Yoga-Bhdsya , 
the important commentary on Yoga-Sutra by Vyasa [10] contains the remark that 
the whole universe undergoes change in a single moment. 10 In other words, the 
contention is that “the collocation of atoms at any moment is different from 
what it was at the previous moment”. 11 


9 Santoditavyapadesyadharmanupati dharmi. 

10 Tenaikena ksanena krtsno lokah parinamam anubhavati (111,52). 

11 A History of Indian Philosophy by S. N. Das Gupta, [7], vol. I, p. 256. 



Time as instant - the Yoga view 


51 


What, then, is a moment to Yoga? The Yoga Sutra III, 52 deals directly with 
the ideas of ksana and krama> i.e. moment/instant and sequence. We read in the 
commentary on the same Sutra that “just as the atom is the minimal limit of 
matter, so the moment is the minimal duration taken by an atom to change its 
position” [10 b] p. 28. 

It is important to focus on the conception of laksana-parinama in order to 
grasp fully why to the Yoga perspective the moment is real and why it designates 
all ideas about sequence or objective temporal series as nothing but a conceptual 
construction. 

As we have noted, the past and the future are explained in this system as 
inherent in the mutations. Vyasa, in his commentary on the Yoga Sutra IV, 12 
goes deeper into the problem and points out that amongst the three time-forms, 
“one time-form is present, the other two time-forms are of course inherent in the 
substance. Future is that, the phenomenalisation of which is yet to come, the 
past is that, the (individual) phenomenalised (form) of which has been experien¬ 
ced. The present is that which has entered into its functional activity” [10 b] p. 
315. 

Let us observe that the idea of a succession of moments as an “absolutely 
existing continuum” 12 is, as the Yoga analysis shows, a subjective, conceptual 
construction. The moment is real, it is the basis for the idea of sequence, but 
whereas the former is objective, the latter is merely subjective. 

It is clear that the Yoga is advocating a discrete view of time. The idea of time 
as a collection of moments does not correspond to anything objectively real 
(vastu). It is a mental structuring. The very idea of sequence would not be 
possible if two moments existed simultaneously. The real fact is that two mo¬ 
ments cannot and do not occur other than successively. There is only the present 
moment, the earlier (past) and the later (future) moments have no objectivity. 

The Yoga school is pronounced and clear in advocating its specific stand about 
the problem of time. Vyasa’s commentary on the Yoga^Sutra 111,52 (10a) clearly 
says that the moment is real, 13 whereas the idea of an unitary objective time 
either as a collection of moments or as an objective series is a subjective represen¬ 
tation, devoid of reality. 14 No two moments can exist simultaneously. 15 

It is evident that the idea of the reality of the instant is to be traced only in the 
present time-form, where the phenomenalising function is actual. It is the pre¬ 
sent moment which alone is causally efficient and objective and therefore is to be 


12 Das Gupta - Yoga Philosophy , [14], p. 215. 

13 Ksanastu vastupatitah kramdvalambi. 

14 Sa khalvayam kalo vastusunyo buddhinirmanah sabdajnandnupati. 

15 Na ca dvau ksanau saha bhavatah. 




52 Time as instant - the Yoga view 

distinguished from the past and the future moments, which are merely subjec¬ 
tive. 

As we review the Sarikhya and the Yoga positions regarding their understan¬ 
ding of time, certain points of distinction strike a critical reader. 

In Sarikhya, Prakrti as time personified is a conception of time as eternal, i.e. 
beginningless and endless, tending more towards time as unitary than as discrete. 
In that case, the instant - following this interpretation - is only an abstraction. 
The analysis of time in Yoga philosophy explicitly advocates a discrete view of 
time. Therefore, before we conclude our discussion of the Sarikhya-Yoga views 
about the problem of time, let us further investigate into the question. 

For both Sarikhya and Yoga the problem of becoming is to be grasped in and 
through an analysis of the causal operation of Prakrti, which is held as an 
independent (svatantra) and objective principle. They both reject the coneption 
of time as advocated by the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools. 

It is important to understand why in spite of their common basic metaphysical' 
structure, the treatment of the problem of time is not akin in Sarikhya and Yoga. 
We have seen that direct reference to the question of time is explicit and clearly 
pronounced in the Yoga literature, whereas the Sarikhya view despite certain 
clear indications of disapproval of a conception of an empty, absolute time is 
open to interpretation. 

A search for understanding this disparity, which is overlooked in general, 
finds illuminating expression in the writings of K.C. Bhattacharya. Fully aware 
of this difference in their views of time, he traces its source in the subtle distinc¬ 
tion in the views of causality of these two schools. Despite the similar conceptual 
structures of the schools, their emphasis on the formulations regarding the pro¬ 
blem of becoming has a certain difference which emerges in their views of time. 
The philosophical significance cannot be overlooked. 

Bhattacharya presents it in the following manner: 

“Neither system admits time as a separate tattva : time is to both concrete 
becoming or parindma , which is not conceived as an event in time. Parinama 
may be understood as the cause turning up its own limitation or as the limited 
effect having got manifested. In the former conception, the real objective fact is 
the act of the cause, its act of turning up or the production, the succession of 
cause and effect being only a retrospective construction as in the Bergsonian 
view. In the latter conception, the real fact is the antecendence of the cause to the 
effect, the prior becoming of the effect, but the causal act as the initial moment of 
this becoming is but an abstraction. Hence to Yoga, which presents the former 
conception, time is real as the causal act, the initiation or the initial moment of 
becoming ( ksana ) ... To the latter or Sarikhya conception of becoming, the effect 
being pre-existent in the cause is never turned up, so that the time-relation of 



Sankhya and Vaisesika on time - a comparative note 


53 


sequence alone - the prior process of manifestation constitutive of the manifest¬ 
ness of the effect - is real time”. 16 

The philosophical involvement of the Yoga school in the problem of time - 
indicating the reality of instant and the mere mental construction of the sequence 
- is motivated by the ultimate goal that the system sets before itself, i.e. the 
discrimination between Purusa and Prakrti, which constitutes the salvatory 
knowledge. This, the Yoga school claims, demands the one-pointed (ekdgra) 
concentration, which is a mental state to be distinguished from the dispersive, 
distracted states. The Yoga Sutra provides a technical classification of these 
mental states, describing their subtle differences from one another. It is impor¬ 
tant to note that it is precisely in this context that the Yoga conceptual pattern for 
understanding change - with reference to the substratum which remains self¬ 
same amid its mutations - is discussed. 

It is with reference to the same mind ( citta ) that its different states - inimical or 
conducive to yogic concentration (samadhi) - are explained. The philosophical 
analysis of the past, present and future time-phases is made in connection with 
the passing-away, the uprising, and the coming forth of the states of the same 
mind. Consequently, the ideas concerning the mental structuring of the temporal 
sequence and the reality of the instant as referring to the minutest mutation that 
is constantly present in all the evolutes of the ever-changing Prakrti, also gradu¬ 
ally take shape. 


(iii) Sankhya and Vaisesika on time - a comparative note 


It is now possible to note the philosophical bridge, that connects the views of 
time found in these schools with their respective conceptions of causality. The 
status of the effect is evidently assessed differently by these schools in accordan¬ 
ce with their different causal theories, which in turn have significant bearing on 
their treatment of the problem of time. 

As we have noted, in the arambhavada framework of Vaisesika the theory of 
causality viz. asatkaryavada focuses on the effect as a new emergence, as a 
beginning ( drambha ) of something which was not before. The effect was non¬ 
existent (asaty hence the theory is called asatkaryavada) as opposed to the San- 
khya theory, where the effect is existent {sat, hence the theory is termed satkarya - 
vada) previous to the causal operation. This philosophical difference in the 


16 K.C. Bhattacharya, Studies in Philosophy [101], Vol. 1, p. 234-5., 



54 Sankhya and Vaisesika on time - a comparative note 

interpretation of the causal operation implies important differences in their views 
of time. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers found it necessary to hold a view of time as 
an all-pervasive, unitary real per se, with reference to which alone contingency is 
conceivable. The effect is not merely an actualisation of that which was potenti¬ 
ally present in the cause - as the Sankhya philosophers would have it - but a 
beginning of a phenomenon which did not exist before. This prior absence 
( prdgabhava ) of the effect is conceived within a metaphysical structure, where 
time is attributed a beginningless, endless reality. This is the absolute back¬ 
ground for all that is contingent. Time is, therefore, characterised, as has been 
noted, as substratum of all that has a beginning and/or end, but to which no 
beginning and end can be attributed. It is classed as an independent substance in 
support of the Nyaya-Vaisesika pluralistic metaphysics. 

This notion of absolute, real, objective time, let us note, is not required as a 
frame of reference for a theory of causality where the effect is conceived - unlike 
by the Vaisesika philosophers - not as non-existent but as pre-existent in the cause 
prior to the causal operation. This is why in the conceptual framework (abhivy- 
aktivada) of the Sankhya-Yoga schools the specific theory of causality ( satkdrya - 
vada) which is advocated, could go hand in hand with their denial of the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika view of time as an absolute, unitary, independent existent. 

The theories of causality, fully worked out in each system, have significant 
implications for the problem of time. The philosophical exchange between the 
schools regarding the status of the effect, whether it is non-existent or pre¬ 
existent in the cause prior to the causal operation, is therefore of interest as they 
neccessitate different views of time. The Sankhya answers to the objection raised 
by the Nyaya-Vaisesika - as to why the effect cannot be regarded as non-existent 
- by pointing out that no systematic account of a definite causal relation between 
the non-existent effect and the existent cause would be established. In that case, a 
cause could give rise to any effect without any restriction whatsoever. 17 Evident¬ 
ly, experience does not testify to that. The specific effect emerges as it is already 
latent in the specific cause. 

Thus, in the Sankhya formulation, the effect is not viewed as a new beginning, 
marked by prior absence, but as a manifestation, an actualisation of that which 
was present in the cause. This theory, therefore, can do away with the notion of 
an empty time as a category of existence. It is not required as a necessary 
reference for any understanding of causal operation which, however, is the case 
in the Nyaya-Vaisesika metaphysical scheme with its specific view of causality. 


17 Sarvasambhavabhdvdt, Sankhya Kdrika 8 [11]. 



Sankhya and Vaisesika on time - a comparative note 


55 


For Sankhya time is nothing independent of the concrete becoming of Prakrti. 
This is why the Sankhya philosophers urge, that the usages of past, present and 
future do not indicate the existence of or call for a postulation of time as a 
unitary substance, but that such usages can be accounted for »... as being the 
modes of the constitution of the things in its different manifesting stages of 
evolution ( adhvan) ...«. 18 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, however, insist that the conventional usa¬ 
ges of past, present, future not only indicate but are made possible by the very 
existence of an all-pervading, partless, uniform time. This is the basis for all such 
conventions, which, however, do not introduce any division in the unitary time 
but are measures by such actions as the solar motion. The contingent is in time. 
The indivisible time is a reality per se. 

A search through the available Sankhya literature shows that a direct reference 
to the problem of time is scarce, which is, again, a contrast to the Vaisesika 
literature, where ample material concerning the problem of time is to be found. 
This is understandable, as the philosophical perspective in each case differs and 
along with it their involvement with the problem. It is causality which is the key 
problem for understanding change, becoming, in Sankhya. To Vaisesika philo¬ 
sophers even the very problem of causality presupposes the existence of the 
absolute time as a feature of reality. Their philosophical concern for the problem 
of time is, therefore, of a different dimension. 

A reference to the problem of time, however, arises in the context of the 
Sankhya account of the subdivision of organs as external and internal. The 
author of Sankhya Karika mentions in Karika 33 that an important point of 
difference between them is that the former - the sense organs - act at the present 
time whereas the latter act at all three times, making inferential knowledge about 
past, present and future happenings possible. It is,while commenting on this 
Karika that Vacaspati Misra in his Tattvakaumudi points out in unambiguous 
terms the difference of the Sankhya position regarding time from that of the 
Vaisesikas. His comment is that even the Vaisesika philosophers according to 
whom time is one, indivisible, attribute the conventional usages of time-divi¬ 
sions as past, present and future to certain qualifying adjuncts ( upadhi-bheda ) - 
like the solar motion. The Sankhya teachers maintain that these qualifying ad¬ 
juncts can in that case account for such usages and they, therefore, do not admit 
of time as a separate category of existence. In other words, the postulation of an 
indivisible time-substance ( kaladravya ) is held to be unnecessary. 

Thus the philosophical perspective of Sankhya offers an interesting contrast to 
that of Vaisesika - both systems arising within the Brahmanical tradition. The 


18 Das Gupta [7], Vol. 1, p. 310. 



56 Sankhya and Vaisesika on time - a comparative note 

Sankhya position can be seen as a challenge to the very conception of vacant time 
as a separate, independent category of existence (tattva)> a position held by the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers. This assumption appears superfluous and unte¬ 
nable to the Sankhya analysis of experience, adopting a different metaphysical 
structure. There is no room for an event-in-time conception in this system. 



Ill 


(i) On time as appearance - the Advaita Vedanta appraisal 


The most significant ideas in the system of Advaita Vedanta, that are to be noted 
in the context of our present undertaking, center primarily on the conception of 
being as timeless, involving the notion of time as appearance. 

The continuing philosophical march of Advaita Vedanta, covering historically 
a long period, has witnessed the emergence and growth of a bewildering mass of 
literature of great philosophical depth and logical subtleties. The masterpieces of 
Sankara, as well as the significant formulations of his predecessors - of whom 
Gaudapada is notable - and the post-Sankara contribution in defence of the 
school against its opponents, have all without exception fociised on the ontologi¬ 
cal conception based on the Upanisadic expression of the reality as “the-one- 
without-a-second” ( ekamevadvitiyam ). 1 

The conceptual framework of this system has a unique character of its own, 
having its stamp on all the different aspects - epistemological, logical, metaphy¬ 
sical worked out in consistency with its fundamental philosophy of being. 

Advaita Vedanta, considered as representing the culmination of the Brahmani- 
cal tradition, rejects the pluralistic metaphysics of Nyaya-Vaisesika as well as the 
dualism of Sankhya-Yoga. As the very term advaita signifies, a systematic effort 
is made to construct a metaphysical structure on the basis of the idea that the real 
is non-dual. 

This understanding, which the Advaita Vedanta maintains to be the final goal 
of all the teachings of the revealed texts of the Upanisads , has led to a rigorous 
formulation of the conception of the non-dual real - Brahman - as timeless, 
which reduces on the other hand all duality i.e. difference to a problem of 
‘appearance*. 

It is, therefore, indispensable to have a brief summary of the philosophical 
tenets of the school, their implications and consequences, in order to bring out 


1 Chandogya Upanisad - 6/2/1 [3]. 



58 On time as appearance - the Advaita Vedanta appraisal 

its subtle differences from the conceptual models present in the other Brahmani- 
cal schools. 

We have already noted the metaphysical structures along with the respective 
theories of causality of the dualistic Sankhya-Yoga and the pluralistic Nyaya- 
Vaisesika. The parinamavada of the former has satkaryavada as its supporting 
theory of causality, whereas the arambhavada of the latter has its theoretical 
basis for causal operation in the asatkaryavada. The Advaita Vedanta metaphysi¬ 
cal conception, which is more rightly termed as non-dualistic than monistic, 
requires a corresponding development in its understanding of causal operation, 
which is formulated in its vivartavada. The specific Advaita stand can be made 
clearer in the light of these other systems, as the controversies between the two 
theories philosophically paved the way for the emergence of a full-fledged causal 
theory, which is peculiar to this system. A global assessment of these theories 
brings into clear perspective the differences in the respective conceptual structu¬ 
res with their eventual implications pertinent to the problem of time. 

In the dualistic structure of Sankhya, besides the principle of Purusa - a con¬ 
ception modelled closely after the Upanisadic pattern of the real as unchanging 
and unchangeable - there is the conception of Prakrti, the principle of concrete 
becoming. Both are granted ontological status. The parinamavada of Sankhya 
interprets causal operation as involving a real transformation of the cause into 
the effect. 

In Advaita Vedanta, however, there is no room for two reals. The correspon¬ 
ding principle of multiplicity and change that Prakrti stands for in the Sankhya 
system is replaced by a still more complex conception of avidya to which no 
ontological status is granted. The quest for the ontological real as non-dual and 
immutable cannot admit of either the Sankhya metaphysical structure nor its 
theory of causality. 

In this connection, let us recall the implications of the problem of time in the 
parinamavada framework. The Sankhya theory of causality does not advocate 
the effect to be non-existent prior to its production. The causal theory maintai¬ 
ning the prior non-existence {pragabhava) of the effect has a meaningful tempo¬ 
ral reference in the Nyaya-Vaisesika which operates with the event-in-time con¬ 
ception. The Sankhya does not hold time as a separate ontological category, as 
apart from the process of concrete becoming. In the Sankhya formulation of 
causal operation, in terms of manifestation of the effect from potentiality to 
actualisation, the conventional temporal usages are accounted for differently. 

As we have discussed before, the conventional usages of time can be viewed as 
an aspect of the heterogeneous evolution of Prakrti (visadrsa-parinama). The 
Yoga school holds the empirical time-phases of past, present and future to be 
equivalent to the sub-latent, actual and potential states of an entity in question. 



On time as appearance - the Advaita Vedanta appraisal 


59 


In other words, the time-phases refer to that aspect of evolution which breaks 
through the homogeneous process of self-reproduction bringing forth the hete¬ 
rogeneous categories of existence. Emergence as present, dissolution as past and 
the potential as future can thus account for conventional temporal usages with¬ 
out having to grant time a distinct existence per se, as the Nyaya-Vaisesikas 
would have it. 

We have also noted that time could be, as some Sankhya interpreters maintain, 
taken as co-eval with Prakrti itself. Viewed thus, the state of homogeneous 
modification ( sadrsaparinama ) could be identified with time in its pre-empirical 
aspect. Here, the intrinsic movement - involving self-reproduction of the three 
gunas - is, as it were, the temporal throb, which continues. In this state the three 
gunas, however, do not combine and therefore cannot evolve any novel category 
of existence with reference to which the three temporal usages of past, present 
and future can be made possible as we gather from the Yoga-Sutra. The 
significant point, which is relevant for comparing the position with that of 
Advaita Vedanta, is that the time-aspect of Prakrti, involved in its nature of 
constant modification (parindmi-svabhava ), is ontological in character. It is to 
be noted that although the problem of time receives distinctly different philoso¬ 
phical treatment in Sankhya in comparison to Vaisesika, yet the ontological reali¬ 
ty that it grants to Prakrti, as eternal-in-change, makes the position radically dif¬ 
ferent from that of Advaita Vedanta. 

The above account of the implication of the problem of change and time in 
parinamavada is intended, on the one hand, to focus on its philosophical close¬ 
ness to Advaita Vedanta - and on the other hand to point out its difference from 
the latter. 

Advaita Vedanta agrees with Sankhya in denying a distinct reality to the effect, 
which - as has been amply demonstrated in the Nyaya-Vaisesika structure - 
requires the presupposition of the ontological reality of time. Advaita Vedanta 
refutes this realistic view of time. We take up some examples of the Advaita 
Vedanta refutation of the reality of time in section III(ii). 

It is, however, of basic importance to note where Advaita Vedanta parts com¬ 
pany with Sankhya. It is clear as one considers the metaphysical structure as well 
as the theory of causality found in the Advaita Vedanta philosophical literature. 

As we have already noted, the Sankhya and the Vaisesika conceptions of the 
effect (karya) - as expressed in their respective theories of causality - vary 
according to their metaphysical commitments. 

In the arambhavada structure of Nyaya-Vaisesika pluralism the effect is 
viewed as a distinct existent, different ( bheda ) from the cause. In the pannama- 
vada of Sankhya dualism, the effect is conceived as pre-existent in a potential 
state in the cause and is, therefore, non-different ( abheda ) from the latter. 



60 


On time as appearance - the Advaita Vedanta appraisal 


In the Advaita Vedanta metaphysics of non-dualism, the effect is characterised 
- in its specific theory, viz. vivartavada , as ‘no other* (ananya) to the cause. This 
term ananya acquires a technical significance, which not only bears witness to 
the underlying metaphysical motivation of the system, but also enables one to 
distinguish its position from both the Sarikhya and Vaisesika schools of 
philosophy. 

In his commentary on the Brahma-Sutra 2/1/14 (arambhadhikarana) 2 San¬ 
kara brings out the significance of the term clearly. Again, Vacaspati Misra in his 
sub-commentary, Bhamati , on the same Sutra minutely works out the import 
and the implication of the term. From the above literature we gather that the 
effect is not to be conceived as having a distinct existence apart ( bheda ) from the 
cause, as the Vaisesikas would have it. On the other hand, the Sarikhya term 
abheda does neither appropriately express the Advaita appraisal of the effect. 
The term ananya is to be understood as the effect being no other to the cause, 
which is a subtle and significant difference from the Sarikhya commitment to 
regard the effect as of identical status with the cause. This is the core of the 
vivartavada of Advaita Vedanta. The significance of the term vivarta is 
explained in the Vedantaparibbasa [44], 3 an important work on the Advaita- 
Vedanta glossary of technical terms. The philosophical import of the term vivar¬ 
ta is brought out by contrasting it with the term parinama . It is said that when 
the cause and the effect are taken to be realities of the same order, the effect is 
designated as parinama of the cause, e.g. Prakrti and its evolutes in Sarikhya. But 
if the effect cannot be ascribed the same ontological status as its cause, it is styled 
vivarta , as in the case of the world as effect in relation to its cause, Brahman, in 
Advaita Vedanta. It is important to note that the idea of the cause in vivartavada 
is so conceived, that the cause, unlike in parinamavada , is not said to undergo 
any transformation or modification. It is thus by denying both the arambhavada 
and parinamavada that Advaita Vedanta develops its vivartavada . The concep¬ 
tion of the effect as indeterminable, i.e. neither a relation of identity (as in 
Sarikhya) nor that of difference (as in Vaisesika), is of great theological consequ¬ 
ence. The distinct approach of this philosophical school leads to the formulation 
of the effect as the ‘appearance* of the cause, which is the real. 

That the effect cannot be granted an independent status of its own is a recur¬ 
rent theme in all the commentaries of Sankara. The point is made emphatically 
that the effect cannot be meaningfully ascribed a separate existence of its own, 
other than a difference of name (nama) and form (rupa) - just as the earthen pot 

2 tadananyatvamarambhanasabdadibhyah [39]. 

3 parindmo ndmopdddna-samasattdka-kdrydpattib vivarto ndmopaddna-visama-sattdka-kdrydpat- 
tih (pratyaksa-pariccheda ). 



On time as appearance - the Advaita Vedanta appraisal 


61 


cannot have reality apart from the earth, its material cause ( upaddna-karana ). 4 
The earth, i.e. the cause, does not, on the other hand, undergo any change. 

Advaita Vedanta constantly focuses attention on this idea that no change or 
modification can be ascribed to that which is real. In other words, change can 
never be granted an ontological status. 

In Appaya Dlksita’s Kalpataru-Parimala 5 , a sub-commentary on Bhdmatl , we 
find the author again bringing out in bold relief that to deny difference ( bheda) is 
not to assert identity between the effect and the cause. This is the precise techni¬ 
cal significance of the term ananya. 

The philosophical consequence of vivartavdda is enormous. It brings forth 
the conception of appearance - a key idea in Advaita Vedanta philosophy and 
theology. It is motivated to create an awareness of a shifting of standpoint as one 
confronts the problems of change and becoming, multiplicity and difference. 
Advaita Vedanta grants to it empirical reality (vyavahara), thus focusing on its 
causal dependence on the real per se (paramdrtha ), which is immutable and 
impartite. The cause as the real is the ground for the effect as appearance. 

The Advaita philosophical literature abounds, therefore, in analysis regarding 
the implication and consequence of this key conception of appearance in its 
epistemological, logical and metaphysical aspects. 

It is commonplace in the philosophical works of the school to analyse a case of 
empirical illusion - such as the rope appearing as a snake or shell as silver - in 
order to bring out all the relevant philosophical features of such experiences. 

That the Advaita epistemological stand is far removed from that of the subjec¬ 
tive idealists becomes at once evident, as one considers the philosophical conse¬ 
quences worked out by its advocates. 

The real as the cause (rope, shell) is given, i.e. its existence is not constituted 
by or dependent on the knowing function. The appearance as the effect (snake or 
silver) is entirely dependent on the cause, which is a given fact ( vastutantra ). 
Appearance cannot claim any independent status of its own. Moreover, appear¬ 
ance is always appearance of something given, the real. The philosophical impli¬ 
cations of these ideas are worked out in great detail. 

But for the purpose of our present study we shall only focus on the Advaita 
conception of the real as that, to which no change - neither beginning, end nor 
modification - can be ascribed. This is of immense interest for an understanding 
of the conception of being as timeless. This specific Advaita Vedanta formulation 
of the idea of being as timeless is taken up in a separate section. We shall, 


4 As e.g. on Cbandogya-Upanisad 6/1/4 [38]. 

5 Commenting on Brahma Sutra [39] 2/1/15. 



62 On time as appearance - the Advaita Vedanta appraisal 

however, discuss briefly the impact of these ideas in terms of their theological 
consequence. 

The quintessence of Advaita Vedanta is often expressed thus - Brahman is real, 
the world is false, the jlva (the individual) is non-different from Brahman. 6 The 
philosophy contends that the realisation of the world as appearance, which is 
equivalent to false in Advaita Vedanta appraisal, at once leads to the awareness of 
the real. This is the salvatory knowledge of Brahman arrived at by negating 
appearance. 

The vital question that arises at this point is, what makes the world a falsity, an 
appearance? 

This is exactly where the analysis of the empirical illusion comes in to clarify 
what constitutes the criterion of appearance, what is the status of appearance and 
its implications about the real. The Advaita practice is then to extend this analo¬ 
gically to the idea of world-appearance and to point out its significance. 

Advaita Vedanta works out minutely its theory of illusion, termed anirvaca - 
niyakhydti , through a detailed analytical exposition and refutation of all other 
rival theories propounded by other schools. 7 

In the Advaita-Siddhi [43] an attempt is made at a clear conceptual formula¬ 
tion of what is understood by falsity ( mithydtva ). Some of the illuminating 
definitions are: The false is subject to negation in all three times {traikalikanised- 
hapratiyogitvam). The real is, by implication, unsublatable ( trikaldbddhyam ). 
Again, the false is that which is removable by knowledge (jndna-nivartatvam ). 
Knowledge, here the non-discursive intuition, reveals the real. The false is also 
defined as that which is neither real nor totally non-existent (sad-asad-vilaksa- 
natvam). Thus, the false/the appearance is termed as indeterminable (, anirvaca- 
niya ), as its sublatability deprives it of being called a real per se, nor can it be 
classed as non-existent since it appears. 

This is precisely the status of the world in the Advaita appraisal. The world as 
effect signifies that it hs no independent existence apart from its cause. The cause 
is self-existent, the effect is contingent. Brahman, the real per se, is the very 
ground for the world appearance. 

Thus the Advaita Vedanta position is to be distinguished from the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika conception of the reality of the world supported by its causal theory, 
where the effect is taken as an independent existent. 

The Advaita Vedanta reduces the problem of creation to a problem of appea-

ranee. Just as the fact, that the pot has a distinct name and form ( ndma-rupa ), 
does not indicate its existence to be independent of the ‘earth’; similarly the 
world as effect has no independent existence without its cause, Brahman. 8 

Here it is interesting to note that the Advaita Vedanta does not accept the 
theological formulation - as in Brahmaparinama-vada - where the world is 
conceived as a real transformation of Brahman. Advaita Vedanta maintains Brah¬ 
man as the cause, but cannot accept any transformation (parinama) or modifica¬ 
tion ( vikdra ) of it. The empirical world has no reality of its own, it is entirely 
dependent on the self-existent Brahman, whereas Brahman is the indispensable 
ground of the world-appearance. This is the position which is variously charac¬ 
terised as mayavada/anirvacyavada/vivartavada. 

It is important to bear in mind, that the idea of appearance does not imply that 
the world for Advaita Vedanta is merely a subjective dream or illusion. Sankara 
in and through his refutation of Vijnanavada, the Buddhist school of subjective 
idealism, makes the Advaita conception distinctly clear. 9 

In this connection, it is of great theological interest to see the implications of 
Brahman, the real per se, as cause. The conception of Brahman as the cause 10 of 
the origin, sustenance and cessation of the world gives expression to the challen¬ 
ge that is faced by all theologians, across cultural boundaries, viz. the formula¬ 
tion of the idea of being as timeless as the very presupposition of the temporal. 
Here is a search for a formulation of a notion of cause which does not hamper 
the conception of being as immutable, non-dual. 

Brahman as cause, in the Advaita Vedanta philosophical literature, is taken as 
an accidental or modal definition of it ( tatastha-laksana ). Brahman, conceived as 
the unmodified ground ( adhisthana) of the world-appearance points to the 
transcendent character of the cause. There are significant theological implica¬ 
tions of the vivartavada approach. It intends to bring out that ’the real’ is the 
implicate and not implicatory of appearance; the appearance is implicatory of the 
real and not its implicate. 11 The world as effect is no ontological real, but 
Brahman as its ground is self-existent. 

Thus the modal definition indicates Brahman as the ground of the world- 
appearance. The Advaita Vedanta advances, however, a non-relational definition 
(svarupa-laksana) as being-consciousness-bliss (sat-cit-ananda). Much has been

9 In his commentary oin the Brahma Sutra 2/2/28 nabhava upalabdheh and 2/2/29 vaidharmyacca 
na svapnadivat [39], Sankara refutes the Vijnanavada position, which brings into focus that the 
world is empirically real. The idea of the falsity or appearance of the world is intended to disclose 
its entirely dependent status, i.e. it is no real per se.

On refutation of the reality of time 


written and discussed on these ideas which are of profound theological consequ¬ 
ence. Advaita Vedanta is held as interpreting justly the claim of the Upanisads 
that it is the knowledge of Brahman - “by knowing which one knows all”. 12 
Brahman is the one-reality-without-a-second. 

It is, however, important to observe that for Advaita Vedanta the realm of 
duality and difference is the temporal order - that which is designated as appea¬ 
rance. The real as unchanging and unchangeable is timeless. Where there is no 
change - i.e. no beginning, end or modification, where there is no room for even 
a numerical difference, i.e. duality, there is no question of time either. “Within 
the world of experience time has universal scope”. However, the point remains 
that “the world is not self-sufficient. The temporal is not the real”. 13 Thus the 
problem of time is necessarily a problem of appearance. 


(ii) On refutation of the reality of time 


In the person of Sri Harsa, the system of Advaita Vedanta finds an ardent 
advocate, who uses his dialectical skill in its defence. A major target of his attack 
has been the system of Nyaya-Vaisesika realistic pluralism. Sri Harsa’s analysis 
[45] aims at showing that the laksanas 1 and the pramdnas , 2 with the help of 
which the Nyaya-Vaisesika systems have constructed the categories of existence 
( padartha ), are themselves defective. Thus, in the context of the dialectical ana¬ 
lysis of the idea of causality, Sri Harsa is led to examine the definition of cause as 
proposed by the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, which, as we shall see shortly, 
has bearing on the problem of time. Sri Harsa’s refutation is tantamount to 
denying the conception of the reality of the objective time as a category of 
existence {padartha ) as held by the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools. It is evident that 
the goal of Sri Harsa, as an Advaitin, is to establish vivartavada y which calls for a 
dismissal of the realistic approach to the question of causality. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika schools have defined cause as an invariable, necessary 
antecedent to the effect. The cause as antecedent to the effect necessarily refers to 
time. The causal operation, involving this idea of temporal precedence (of the 
cause) and succession (of the effect), in the Nyaya- Vaisesika system is worked 


12 Chandogya Upanisad 6/1/3 [3]. 

13 Indian Philosophy , by Radhakrishnan, [8], Vol. II, p. 528. 

14 Characteristic marks. 

15 Valid means of knowledge. 



On refutation of the reality of time 


65 


out - as we have already discussed - in the framework of the notion of unity and 
reality of time as an objective, ontological category. 

Sri Harsa’s objection is first aimed at the very definition of cause as the 
temporally antecedent. The chief points of his analysis can be summarised as 
follows. He raises the question whether these time-distinctions, as past, present, 
and future, are integral to time as such or not. 

If it is held that this distinction of three time-forms is inherent in the very 
nature of time, in that case the unity (ekatva) of the real time, as the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika philosophers would have it, cannot be maintained. In other words, if 
our apprehension of the past time, present time, and future time is due to the 
very nature of time as such, then differences would be integral to time and 
consequently the notion of the unity of time has to be given up. 

On the other hand, if one maintains the unity of time as essential and the 
differences of time-forms as inherent in it as its three-fold nature, then the 
designations of past, present and future could be made at random for the want of 
a strict basis for such usages. This is to say that one cannot accept the differences 
of time-forms as integral to one time either. 

But if, as the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers maintain, the time-distinctions are 
attributed to other external factors such as the solar motion, Sri Harsa finds that 
too equally untenable. His objection is that the solar motion cannot account for 
the determinations of the different time-forms, since it is used as the common 
criterion for the distinctions of all three time-forms - past, present, and future. 
The idea that the same qualifying factor, viz. the solar motion, which distinguis¬ 
hes the past day from the present, also distinguishes the present day from the 
future, is not a basis for valid argumentation. 

If the Nyaya-Vaisesika now urges that although as such the solar moton is the 
same, distinctions are conceivable with reference to it because of the time- 
distinctions and thus can account for the conventional usags of past, present, and 
future, - this too cannot be accepted. Sri Harsa brings the charge of the fallacy of 
circular definition (itaretarasraya) against this argumentation. He points out 
that the argument is tantamount to saying that the present time is determined by 
the act, and the presentness of the act is due to time. Moreover, if the time- 
distinctions are due to the act, then they can be just as well accounted for by the 
act, making the assumption of one, indivisible, substantial time superfluous. 

This discussion, found in the Khandanakhandakhadyam by Sri Harsa, 16 is an 
example of the rejection of the reality of time through a critical analysis of the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika definition of cause as antecedent to the effect. The temporal 
determination is the basis which makes it possible to demarcate the effect - as 

16 [45] pp. 682-684. 


5 A study of time 



66 


On refutation of the reality of time 


present - from its cause which precedes it. The main aim of the analysis is to 
expose, that this very definition of cause as entailing time-distinctions requires 
that the latter are accounted for. Tha Advaita understanding of .the indeterminab¬ 
le ( anirvacaniya ) character of the empirical world cannot accept the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika notion of causal operation implying the reality of time. 

The Advaita Vedanta philosophical march aims at the establishment of Brah¬ 
man as the sole reality, one without a second ( ekamevadvitiyam ). This is evi¬ 
dently, as we have discussed earlier, a conception of being as timeless of which no 
difference - external ( vijatiya ), internal ( svagata ), or numerical ( svajatiya ) - can 
be predicated. In this metaphysical structure, therefore, there cannot be any 
room for a conception of an objective, real time. To accept time as a substantial 
reality leads to, as exemplified in the Nyaya-Vaisesika structure of thinking, 
realistic pluralism. 

Thus, although there is not much direct involvement with the problem of time 
in the system, there are documents in the philosophical literature of the Advaita 
Vedanta on refutation of the Nyaya-Vaisesika position regarding the reality of 
time. 

We have noted an example of that in Sri Harsa’s work. In the important work 
called Tattvapradipika [41] by Citsukha we find still another example of it. The 
challenge of the reality of time is taken up by the Advaitins as the position 
cannot be compromised with the idea of Brahman as worked out fully in the 
philosophy of Advaita Vedanta. To accept time to be real is to make way for 
pluralism. The world of multiplicity and change cannot be pronounced, in that 
case, as appearance. In the arambhavada structure of Nyaya-Vaisesika the theo¬ 
ry of causality, asatkaryavada , operates with the conception of the effect as real, 
as a new emergence. The Advaita Vedanta, which intends to discard pluralism of 
ontological categories, formulates the problem of causality in a manner so as to 
clearly distinguish it from both the asatkaryavada of Nyaya-Vaisesika as well as 
from the satkaryavada of Sarikhya-Yoga. The Sarikhya model of causal opera¬ 
tion, as we have already studied, is expressed within a framework involving the 
conception of being as eternal-in-change, viz. Prakrti. This too as an objective, 
ontological principle, although closer to the Advaita standpoint than the Vaisesi- 
ka position, is yet untenable. Advaitins seek to establish vivartavada. The 
Advaitins have therefore examined and refuted these two theories of causality. 
Thus, it is in the context of a review of the Nyaya-Vaisesika theory of causality 
that Citsukha comes to examine the Nyaya-Vaisesika view of time. 

Citsukha raises the question as to how the Nyaya-Vaisesika establishes the 
reality of time. This is in order to scrutinise the pramanas used for the argumen¬ 
tations in favour of their position. The Nyaya-Vaisesika understanding of the 



On refutation of the reality of time 


67 


causal operation as involving a sequence presupposes the reality of time. If time 
were real, the reality of precedence and succession of cause and effect could be 
established, as a consequence of which the reality of the world too would have to 
be granted. Thus, by refutation of time as ontologically real, the realistic view of 
causal operation is also shown as untenable. As a consequence the significance of 
the world as appearance, as in the vivartavada appraisal, comes into focus. 

Citsukha argues to show that neither perception nor inference can establish 
the reality of time. Historically, the target of Citsukha’s attack is the realistic 
position about the problem of time as advocated in the Nyayalildvati [26] of 
Vallabhacharya. The ontological reality of a substance can be established through 
perception only if - and this is in accordance with the Vaisesika stand - the 
substance in question can be said to be perceived by vision or touch. Citsukha 
points out that since it is held that time is without form and is intangible, sense- 
perception cannot be the source of knowledge, which can demonstrate it as 
having an ontological status - as the Nyaya-Vaisesikas seek to establish. 

His next consideration is whether it could be so maintained that time is known 
not through external sense-perception but through internal or mental perception 
(mdnasa-pratyaksa). This too cannot be maintained, as internal perception can¬ 
not refer back to an objective entity, which cannot otherwise be established by 
sense-perception. But since the substantial time cannot be, as we have seen, said 
to be an object of sence-perception, internal perception cannot establish it. 

Again, it is possible to point to the existence of such mental states as sadness, 
happiness, etc. which are known through introspection and need not to be first 
known by sense-perception. Could time be established in a similar way? Evi¬ 
dently not, as it is only possible for such facts which are subjective ( dntara - 
vastu ), but time as an objective category of existence cannot be thus established. 

At this point it is necessary to recall that the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers do 
not seek to establish the reality of time by perception. It is inference (anumdna) 
which is used as the valid means of knowledge (pramana ). So Citsukha now 
turns to examine the validity of the Nyaya-Vaisesika claim that time can be 
established by inference. 

The point of controversy refers back to the Nyaya-Vaisesika contention, that 
the existence of time is inferred on the basis of such apprehensions as priority 
and posteriority, which serve as the middle term (/mga) in the said inference. To 
this Citsukha objects. He points out that inference is possible only where uni¬ 
versal relation (vyapti) between the major term ( sddhya ) and the middle term 
(lihga ) can be established. But in this particular case of inference how have the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers obtained the knowledge of vyapti (i.e. where 
there is apprehension of priority/posteriority, there is time) - or universal rela- 



68 


On refutation of the reality of time 


tion between the middle and the major term? It has never been perceived in any 
previous experience. Thus, this inference is fallacious. Time cannot be said to be 
established by inference either. 17 

Citsukha further investigates into the question. He asks whether priority, 
posterioty, etc. are to be understood as effects ( karya ) of time or are due to any 
external adjuncts ( upadhi ). If, he points out, the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers
