would maintain them as effects, then time as an instrumental cause cannot be 
held as passive ( niskriya ) - as the realists would have it. On the other hand, if it is 
due to such external adjuncts like solar motion, the latter can just as well be said 
to be sufficient basis in order to account for such conventional temporal usages. 
In other words, Citsukha points out that in neither case is it essential to postulate 
time as an ontological category. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika appraisal is however different. To them, priority, poste¬ 
riority are accidental or relational properties of given entities. The argument thus 
runs as follows: that (i.e. time) by contact with which entities (pinda) get these 
properties or denominations, (e.g. this body is old, that body is young, etc.) - 
that must be a universal, ubiquitous substance. 

Space (dik) cannot substitute for time. The spatial nearness and remoteness 
cannot account for temporal distinctions of which there is clear apprehension 
(pratiti ). Citsukha asks, cannot the self ( atman ) be a substitute for time? (Note 
that in Vedanta, atman is universal and has no special property, unlike in the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika system). This is, however, not acceptable to the realists, for 
whom the self has special qualities like desire ( iccha) etc. In other words, it is not 
sufficiently neutral in character to perform the function of time. 

This holds equally true of all other substances ( dravya) accepted by the rea¬ 
lists. It is time alone which can perform the exclusive function which enables the 
contact between the body (pinda ) and the solar motion. Earth, water, air, fire, - 
none of these can replace time as they are not all-pervasive. In other words, the 
realist search for a substance which is universal, all-pervasive, having no special 
qualities (visesaguna) of its own leads to the detection of time as an ontological 
category. 

Citsukha further objects that if time is universal and uniform, how can there 
be differences in our apprehension of time-distinctions such as day, month, etc.? 


17 It is to be noted that the objection raised against this position is due to the fact, that the Vedantins 
accept only one form of inference ( anvayavyatireki ), which has for its prerequisite that the 
association of the middle term with the major term has to be known, e.g. by perception, to make 
a valid inference. 

To the Nyaya-Vaisesika this does not hold true of all forms of inference. The inference of time is a 
case of parisesanumdna where this requirement need not be fulfilled. 



Being as timeless in Advaita Vedanta 


69 


To this, the realist answers that time-divisions are due to such external adjuncts 
as solar motion. Time as an ontological all-pervading substance has to be accep¬ 
ted as that which makes these conventional usages at all possible. 

Note that the solar motion is accepted by both parties as indispensable for 
accounting for conventional usages of time-distinctions. But whereas the realists 
are accepting time as a separate substance, as an ontological category, Citsukha 
finds such an assumption untenable and even entirely unnecessary. It is only 
needlessy increasing the number of ontological categories ( gaurava ), which is 
fallacious, going against the law of parsimony. 

In conclusion, to an Advaitin the reality of time as a separate ontological 
category is wholly superfluous. No valid means of knowledge can be said to 
establish such a contention. 


(iii) Being as timeless in Advaita Vedanta 


The Advaita Vedanta conception of being as timeless deserves special attention. 
It is here that one comes across the most clearly pronounced expression of the 
idea in its philosophical refinement and purity. The conception is formulated 
with a philosophical rigour and depth, which surpasses all other attempts by the 
different schools in the Brahmanical tradition. 

It is to be noted that the conception of nitya vastu , - being as uncaused and 
indestructible i.e. beginningless and endless - is present throughout the philo¬ 
sophical history of Brahmanism. All the principal philosophical schools have 
this conception, as their common heritage from the Upanisads , which plays a 
predominant role in their respective metaphysical structures. But it is of great 
philosophical interest to note how the understanding of nitya vastu varies from 
school to school, the philosophical development reaching its culmination in the 
school of Advaita Vedanta. In this system it gives rise to a strict, rigorous 
formulation, as an outcome of penetrating analysis bearing on all its implications 
and consequences. 

In the Nyaya-Vaisesika metaphysical realism there is room for a plurality of 
nitya vastu, conceived as ontological categories - beginningless/uncaused and 
endless/indestructible. It is not only atman which is nitya, but so also are space, 
time, etc. They have distinct existences, where one cannot encompass the other. 
The janya vastu, i.e. that to which beginning and/or end could be ascribed, can 
be so encompassed. Thus time, a nitya vastu, encompasses all that is contingent 



70 


Being as timeless in Advaita Vedanta 


{adhara-adheya sambandha). The multiplicity and the differences of ontological 
categories of Nyaya-Vaisesika metaphysical pluralism evidently find no room in 
the Advaita Vedanta scheme of thought. 

Again in the Sankhya metaphysical dualism both Purusa and Prakrti are con¬ 
ceived as nitya , beginningless and endless, uncaused and indestructible. But 
Prakrti, as Sankhya specifies it, is a parindmi nitya , i.e. it undergoes modifica¬ 
tions. There is change of states ( avastha ) in Prakrti. Prakrti is the underived 
principle of concrete becoming, movement is intrinsic to it. As we have already 
discussed earlier, even where the discernible time-phases are not present as in the 
visadrsa parinama or heterogeneous modification, it still preserves the principle 
of temporality in its transcendental aspect in the state of sadrsa parinama ot 
homogeneous modification. The Advaita Vedanta has no room for change and 
becoming in its understanding of nitya vastu , which is conceived as unchanging 
and unchangeable (i.e. kutastha!aparinamT), 

The Sankhya conception of Purusa comes closest to the Advaita conceptual 
pattern, in so far as it is conceived as unmodified, unchangeable consciousness, 
but its objection to Sankhya would be the latter’s idea of the plurality of Purusas. 
In other words, the Sankhya conception of many Purusas is against the Advaita 
Vedanta understanding of the ultimate real. The absence of duality, i.e. numeri¬ 
cal difference (sankhyd-rahitya) may not be a requisite for the Sankhya concep¬ 
tion of kutastha nitya , but in Advaita Vedanta a plurality of similar ( sajatiya ), 
eternal {nitya) beings, as the Sankhya idea of many Purusas implies, has no 
room. The nitya is not only beginningless, endless, unchangeable but also non¬ 
dual ( advaya ). Numerical difference is still difference {bheda) which belongs to 
the order of empirical reality {vyavahara). Brahman as Being par excellence is 
the ‘one-without-a-second’ {ekamevadvitiyam)> no change {parinama ), no dua¬ 
lity/plurality {dvaita) can be predicated of it. 

In this connection it is highly significant to note that the Advaita Vedanta 
arrives at this conception of being as non-dual, immutable, impartite, through 
the negative method of denying all differences {bheda) to it. This deserves special 
treatment. The conception of being as timeless - a philosophical idea on which 
Advaita Vedanta focuses in all intensity - demands a thorough and profound 
intellectual expression. 

We, therefore, turn to Pancadasi, an important work on Advaita Vedanta by 
Vidyaranya [42], where a summary of the denial of differences is brilliantly 
worked out. All differences are classified under three heads - svagata , svajdtiya , 
and vijatiya. As an example of the former kind, Pancadasi (II, 20) mentions such 
differences as in a tree, of leaves, flowers, fruits, etc. The difference of one tree 
from another is an example of the second kind. The third kind of difference is 
exemplified as that of a tree from a stone. In other words, difference is concei- 



Being as timeless in Advaita Vedanta 


71 


vable as existing within an entity, as difference in species falling under the same 
class/genus or differences of one class/genus from another. 

It is precisely in order to remove all possible doubts about the conception of 
oneness of Brahman that these three kinds of differences (bheda-traya) are to be 
negated and rejected, thereby arriving at the right understanding of the import of 
the idea. 

Thus, through denying any svagata-bheda in Brahman, the impartite charac¬ 
ter of being is indicated. It rejects the conceptual pattern present in the Sankhya 
formulation of the notion of Prakrti. Although beginningless and endless, modi¬ 
fication and movement are intrinsic to Prakrti. Moreover, Prakrti is composite. 
As contrast to this, Brahman is conceived as uncomposite, it has no parts. No 
change of state/transformation can be attributed to it. It is aparinami , unchan¬ 
ging and unchangeable. 

By rejecting svajatiya bheda of Brahman, it is the non-dual (advaita) being 
which is indicated, where there is no room for numerical difference of similar 
entities. The Sankhya model of many Purusas cannot be permitted. 

Moreover, Brahman cannot be conceived as having any vijatiyabbeda either. 
The Nyaya-Vaisesika pluralistic model as well as the Sankhya dualism operates 
with the conception of heterogenous, dissimilar ultimate ontological existents. It 
is not possible to conceive of an ‘other’ to Brahman, which is being or sat. Non- 
being or asat is non-existent and therefore, as we read in Pahcadasi II, 25, cannot 
serve as a counter-correlate (pratiyogT) to Brahman. 

There is, thus, no room for duality or difference in any conceivable form 
applicable to the idea of being, that Brahman stands for in the philosophy of 
Advaita. 

Let us now observe that the idea of nitya vastu has profound philosophical 
implications. Although all the Brahmanical schools agree in defining nitya as 
beginningless and endless, as unoriginate and indestructible, yet the subtle philo¬ 
sophical differences in their understanding are unmistakable. 

The conception of nitya as in the Sankhya model of Prakrti implies eternality, 
but not timelessness. By insisting that the nitya must be aparinami , immutable, 
the Advaita Vedanta is driving towards the conception of being as timeless in 
contrast to being as eternal. 

Moreover, a yet more rigorous formulation is attained through denial of nu¬ 
merical differences, of similar and dissimilar eternal entities. Thus, the Sankhya 
and the Vaisesika metaphysical structures cannot satisfy the Advaita quest for the 
being as timeless. There is no room for duality in this framework of thinking, as 
it operates with the rigorous conception of the ultimate real (sat) as ‘one-with- 
out-a-second’. Brahman is plenitude ( bbuma ), unlimited and impartite (aparic- 
chinna ), and non-dual (advaita). 



72 


Being as timeless in Advaita Vedanta 


In this connection it is to be observed that Brahman - the ultimate, non-dual* 
being - is, in some Advaita texts like Advaitasiddhi [43], described as trikaldbad - s 
hya y i.e. uncancellable in all three times, past, present, and future. It is that about 
which one could not say - it is not, it will not be, or it was not. In other words, it 
is not being-in-time. But if it is urged that the Sankhya conception of Prakrti is } 
also uncancelled in three times, the difference lies evidently in the Advaita rejec- j 
tion of all modification and change as pertainable to Brahman. 

The same question can be discussed with regard to the idea of ever-presentness f 
(sarvadavidyamanatva). The idea of being as ever-present is another way o£| 
designating being which no time can cancel. To say that there is no conceivable! 
time where absence or negation of being can be maintained is a mode of indica-^ 
ting its ontological status. Now, again one could apply the idea to the Sankhya of| 
the Nyaya-Vaisesika models of nitya-vastu. This is why a thorough rejection of| 
all three kinds of differences is an indispensable and effective conceptual tool for ■ 
arriving at the idea of being as timeless. Thus, being which is ever-present, in| 
other words, which is uncancellable in all three times, when purged of all the| 
three kinds of differences, leads to the idea of being as timeless. J 

Moreover, it is not to be lost sight of that, whereas the principle of change qusgj 
time is objective and ontological in Sankhya, avidya , the corresponding principle! 
in Advaita Vedanta, has merely epistemic status. Time as a product or as aj 
personification of Prakrti is real in Sankhya, whereas time as product or as coevaH 
with avidya is only appearance in Advaita Vedanta. The Nyaya-Vaisesika con-r| 
ception of time as an ontological category is, as is evident, totally unacceptable! 
to Advaita Vedanta. This is why all the various modes of defining the timeles^ 
Brahman are to be taken as accidental definitions - tatastha-laksana - of Brah^ 
man. These are attempts to describe or define Being as timeless from the order of 1 
time, which coincides with that of the phenomenal and empirical. | 




IV 


(i) Time in Jainism 


The metaphysical stand of Jainism, known as anekantavada , is, as the term 
designates, an attempt to construct a conceptual structure which makes room for 
a many-sided view of reality. It aims at formulating a conception of reality, 
which can accomodate both identity and difference, permanence and change. 1 It 
therefore seeks to avoid such views which are branded as ekanta-vada , i.e. thfe 
extreme one-sided views, which either emphasize the reality of the ultimate 
identity at the exclusion of change or maintain the reality of perpetual change at 
the price of identity. The system of Advaita Vedanta, for example, represents the 
former and Buddhism the latter, which are therefore extreme views, designated 
by the Jainas as Brahma-ekantavada and k$anika-ekdntavdda y respectively. Jai¬ 
nism seeks to avoid these extreme views and puts forward a comprehensive 
philosophical position as a metaphysical reconciliation. 

This is a broad outline indicating the conceptual goal of the Jaina philosop¬ 
hers, which was eventually worked out in detail, covering such areas of investi¬ 
gation as logic, epistemology, ethics, eschatology, etc. The Jaina contribution to 
these fields of knowledge saw the development of new concepts in support of its 
metaphysics. The Jaina philosophical tradition has a distinct flavour, which is 
un-Buddhistic and un-Brahmanical, in so far as it sets up a metaphysical struc¬ 
ture peculiar to its own view of reality. 

It is evident, as the philosophical exchanges point to, that the metaphysical 
compromise of the Jaina philosophy found favour with neither the Brahmanical 
nor the Buddhist thinkers. 

The Jaina attempt to avoid the extreme one-sided views gave rise to a distinct 
conception of the real, which is formulated by Umasvati in his Tattvarthadhiga - 
ma Sutra [67] as utpada-vyaya-dhrauvya-laksanam sat , i.e. origin, decay and 
permanency are the characteristics of the real. In other words, this threefold 
characteristic of the real is expressing the Jaina conception which allows for 

1 cf. Jaina Sutra, [70] and Syad-Vada-Manjari [71]. 



74 


Time in Jainism 


difference/change and yet maintains an underlying permanence/identity. This 
becomes even more clear, as one notes the Jaina conception of substance ( dr a - 
vya) which plays an important role in Jaina metaphysics. 

It is indispensable to have an overall idea of the metaphysical structure of the 
system in order to comprehend the conception of time which is eventually the 
subject of interest for our present study. The Jaina metaphysical system is one of 
realistic pluralism. The two important terms, that occur throughout the Jaina 
philosophical literature, are dravya and astikaya. It is only through our under¬ 
standing of these concepts that we can grasp the distinct Jaina formulation of the 
problem of time. 

Dravya or substance is defined by Umasvati as that which possesses qualities 
and modes. 2 To talk about a substance without qualities and modes or vice versa 
is merely an intellectual abstraction which does not do justice to reality. The 
system lists six substances ( dravyas ) as ultimately real, of which one is time. The 
various principles that are employed to classify the substances (dravyas) accor¬ 
ding to their characteristics at once focus on the Jaina view of time as such along 
with its similarities and differences from other substances. The six dravyas that 
are listed as metaphysically real are jiva, pudgala , dharma y adharma, dkdsa and 
kala, i.e. the soul/the conscious substance, matter, the principle of motion, the 
principle of rest, space and time. 

Amongst the six dravyas five are called astikaya, a concept which is significant 
for the conception of time, negatively speaking. The term astikaya, consisting of 
two words asti and kaya, is explained in Dravya-Samgraha [66], 24, an important 
Jaina book of which the author is Nemichandra, as follows - asti literally means 
‘exists* and kaya means ‘body*. The astikayas are therefore those five substances 
which exist and have magnitude/bodies. Here kaya is technically understood as 
consisting of many indivisible particles {pradesa ). Thus, the Jaina text points 
out, five out of the six substances apart from time can be characterised as astikdy- 
as. This becomes clear in the context of the general atomic conception of the 
universe in Jaina thought, as the notion of pradesa indicates. The five substances 
are called astikaya precisely because the particles of which they are composed are 
not separate or at least in principle capable of being mixed up. This specification 
helps one to understand why time (kala) is not classified as an astikaya by the 
Jainas despite that it has existence. 

The Jainas propound an atomic conception of time. 3 The characteristic of the 
time-atoms is, however, such that they are distinct and can never be mixed up. 


2 Gunaparyayavat dravyam. 

3 For the internal differences between the Svetambara and Digambara Jainas, see Nagin J. Shah, 
Nature of Time, [118] p. 63-87. 



Time in Jainism 


75 


This is brought out by saying that kdla has one pradesa only, i.e. the time-atoms 
can never be combined. This explains why time cannot be classified as an asti - 
kdya. This, again, is a clear way of indicating the difference between the atoms of 
space, matter, etc. from that of time. 

An important understanding pertaining to the time-atoms is discussed in the 
Jaina text entitled Pancastikayasara [68] (109). Here it is pointed out that the 
fact, that time has no corpus (kayatva ) or extension, is generated by such an 
arrangement that the time-atoms can constitute a mono-dimensional series which 
is unilateral. This is what in Jaina terminology is called urdha pracaya as oppo¬ 
sed to the tiryak pracaya - a multi-dimensional series or horizontal extension. A. 
Chakravarti writes in his ‘philosophical introduction’ to Pancdstikayasara y "... 
when the simple elements, say the points, are so arranged in a series, where each 
term is an item also in another series, we must have the two-dimensional series, 
which will correspond to surface or extension. Whereever there is such a tiryak 
pracaya we have astikdya. But time or kdla has only urdha pracaya. The elements 
are in a forward direction ...«. 

The Jaina understanding of the time-atoms (kdlanu) as ultimate, absolute and 
real again points to its distinction from the conventional time (vyavahara kdla). 
The conventional time-periods, of which we find a long list in Pancdstikdyasaray 
25, range from the minutest moment called samaya , through nimisa y kdstha , 
kala y nali y to muhurta - equal to two nalis, divaratra - day and night, equal to 
thirty muhurtas y masa - month, equal to thirty days, rtu - season - equal to two 
months, ayana - half year - equal to three seasons, samvatsara - year, which is 
equal to two ayanas. These divisions and subdivisions are linked to the. different 
units of measurement pertaining to conventional time. 

It is said that these distinctions of conventional time-periods are made possible 
only with the help of some extraneous factors. The units of measurement are 
generally based on some changes in the physical world, such as the motion of the 
heavenly bodies. This is why conventional time is never unconditional but de¬ 
pendent on other outside factors, unlike the real, absolute time, which is vario¬ 
usly designated as the great unconditioned time (paramdrthakala ), true time 
(niscayakala) y real time ( dravyakala ). 

The conventional time presupposes the absolute time. In the Jaina text the 
absolute time is described not only as a real existent but also as being potent, i.e. 
it brings about changes in the other substances. Birth, growth and decay of 
things are due to its influence. 

It can thus be noted that the very changes, which determine the use of conven¬ 
tional time, are themselves the effects of absolute time. All distinctions that are 
made of conventional time, short or long and so forth, are dependent on the 



76 


Time in Jainism 


standard of measurements. They are not intrinsic to absolute time as such, nor 
can they affect the latter by any means. 

The absolute time, consisting of instants, is conceived as having no beginning 
or end, whereas conventional time have both. This brings into light an important 
feature of the Jaina understanding of time. The instants arranged unilaterally are 
conceived as permanent ( nitya ), 4 i.e. beginningless and endless. The Jainas agree 
with the other atomic views of time (e.g. Yoga, Buddhist views) in maintaining 
that no two instants can be found simultaneously but unlike them conceive of 
the instants as unperishable. Time (kala) is nitya for Jaina, like the Vaisesika 
philosophers held it to be, but the difference between them is glaring. The 
Vaisesika view of one ubiquitous time is found to be untenable, and the Jainas 
propound an atomic, plural view of time instead. 

The Jaina conception of absolute, real time is to be contrasted to the idea of 
time as appearance. Time, to the Jaina thinkers, is a substance, having an ultima¬ 
te status in the pluralistic metaphysics of the system. No modification in the 
universe can be conceived without time. 

It is significant that Jainism with its realistic framework of thinking accepts the 
reality of change which, in turn, points to the reality of time. To deny the reality 
of time is tantamount to the dismissal of all change involving birth, growth, 
decay or motion. It is precisely this position that is avoided by maintaining the 
reality of time. 

Thus, we find that in the Jaina structure the absolute time-atoms are conceived 
as pervading the space ( lokakdsa , i.e. the space which is occupied by other 
substances as opposed to alokakasa signifying void), the instants having no 
extension or volume ( kdya ). In other words, there can be no question of simulta¬ 
neous moments, the time series consisting of time-atoms is always to be under¬ 
stood as successive. 

The different principles for classifying the dravyas in distinct groups are, as 
already indicated, a source of our knowledge about the Jaina idea of time. In 
chulika 104 of Pancdstikayasdra , dravyas are classified as murta and amurta . The 
substance which is found to have sense-qualities such as contact, smell, colour, 
sound, etc. is called murta. Matter (pudgala) alone is said to be so, whereas the 
other five, including time, are classified as amurta , i.e. devoid of sense-qualities. 
This is indicative of the fact that the absolute time can never be made an object of 
sense-perception. It is arrived at through knowledge by inference. 

Again, according to another principle of classification, on the basis of which 
substance can be said to have consciousness, we find that jtva alone is singled out 


4 Chulika 108, Pancdstikayasdra [68]. 



The Jaina challenge to the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of time 77 

as conscious ( cetana ). All the other substances including time are listed as aceta - 
na or insentient. 

The chulika 105 of the same text brings out the conception of time as non¬ 
active (niskriya). This means that causal activity bringing about any change in it 
is contemplated as inconceivable. On the other hand, no change in matter invol¬ 
ving aggregation or disintegration is conceivable without time. 

These are the main ideas about time that can be traced from the original Jaina 
texts. 

The Jaina challenge to the Vaisesika conception of time is documented in an 
illuminating discussion which will now be taken up. 


(ii) The Jaina challenge to the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of 
singular, ubiquitous time 


An interesting account of the plural conception of time as instants - a Jaina 
conviction sought to be established through a refutation of the Vaisesika concep¬ 
tion of one ubiquitous time-is documented in the reputed Jaina work called 
Prameyakamalamartanda . 5 The Vaisesikas and the Jainas both accept the reality 
of change along with the reality of time. Their notions of time, however, have 
important differences. 

To the Jainas, the absolute time consists of the ultimate time-atoms, which are 
evidently conceived in plural terms, and the conventional time-periods, short or 
long, are all due to the measurements made on the basis of such extraneous 
factors as the motion of heavenly bodies. 

The Vaisesikas, however, maintain that the absolute time is one and indivisib¬ 
le, the conventional time-usages can be many, made on the basis of such units of 
change as the solar motion etc. The Jainas find it untenable and raise a philoso¬ 
phical challenge to this notion of one ubiquitous time. 

The question raised from the Jaina standpoint is, whether the Vaisesika under¬ 
standing of time is that of one partless, indivisible substance ( eka-dravya ) or that 
of a substance composed of parts ( aneka-dravya ). Evidently the reference is to 
the Vaisesika Sutra, where the question has been discussed. The Jaina refutation 
is so formulated as to point out that neither of the two possibilities can be valid, 
thereby demonstrating the vulnerability of the Vaisesika position. 


5 By Prabhachandracharya [69]. 



78 The Jaina challenge to the Ny ay a-Vaisesika conception of time 

The Jaimas argue that time cannot be taken as an indivisible, partless substan¬ 
ce. If it were so, the distinction between time per se and the conventional time - 
which is accepted by the Vaisesikas themselves - would be impossible. If the 
essential nature of time is to be ubiquitous, it can never be divided even with the 
help of any external adjuncts, as that is possible only in the case of a composite 
substance, made of parts. 

On the other hand, if the absolute time is taken as a composite substance 
( ; savayava-dravya ), one need not postulate time as a separate substance, space 
could serve the purpose. Space, in Jainism, is conceived as having parts, which 
makes the conventional use possible. The same space ( lokakasa ) is known so¬ 
mewhere as Lanka, and elsewhere as Kuruksetra. In other words, it is superfluo¬ 
us to accept a notion of time as a composite substance. If time is to be postulated 
as a separate substance, it is to be accepted as atomic in nature. 

The Jaina argument against the notion of time as ubiquitous is that, on the 
basis of such a conception, it is not possible to account for such conventions as 
past, present, future, etc. Such usages as past time etc., the Jaina asks, is it due to 
its relation to past causal efficiency or is it integral to time itself? If one accepts 
the first alternative, the Jaina objection is, that in that case causal efficiency itself 
could be the basis for such conventions as past time, and it would be unnecessary 
to postulate time as a separate substance. Moreover, the further difficulties that 
will arise here are, how should one determine the pastness of a causal efficiency? 
If that involves another causal efficiency, the argument would lead to infinite 
regress (anavastha). On the other hand, if it is urged that the usage of the causal 
efficiency as past is possible only on the basis of time, and again the usage of time 
as past is possible on the basis of causal efficiency, it involves the wrong argu¬ 
mentation of mutual dependence ( anyonyasrayadosa ). 

The second alternative, that such conventional usages can be accounted for as 
integral to time itself, is, however, simply unacceptable. A partless substance, as 
the Vaisesika philosophers hold time to be, cannot have any inherent differences 
as natural to it. 

Again, the Jaina points out that, contrary to the Vaisesika supposition of one 
ubiquitous time as a necessary basis for accounting for such usages as slowness, 
fastness, the atomic notion of time as plural has to be admitted for explaining 
such uses. Slowness and fastness only indicate that which is accomplished by 
more time or less time - both leading to a conception of time as many. It is 
quantitative manyness or scarcity of time which is at the back of it, a convention 
which can never be explained on the basis of a notion of time as one, indivisible 
and ubiquitous. 

The notion of simultaneity is also examined in this connection only to expose 
the untenability of the Vaisesika conception of time as involving fallacious circu- 



The Jaina challenge to the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of time 79 

lar arguments. Simultaneity, when analysed, only shows that though the effects 
are different, they are produced together. On the basis of this it is needless first 
to postulate one indivisible time and then to imagine differences in it with the 
help of adjuncts, i.e. the effects, in this case. 

If it is pointed out that the notion of sequence calls for a conception of 
indivisible time, the Jaina is equally insistent on demonstrating that the very idea 
of sequence demands an atomic view. Simultaneous or successive (i.e. involving a 
sequence) occurrences can be accounted for as pertaining to the same or different 
moments. All these arguments aim at showing, that the conception of indivisible 
time is to be rejected. 



V 


(i) The Buddhist idea of instantaneous being 


Apart from the conceptual models that the study of being and time have given 
rise to in the Brahmanical tradition, there is an entirely novel set of ideas intro¬ 
duced by the Buddhist thinkers in their philosophical treatment of these pro¬ 
blems. 

The Buddhist stand regarding the problems of being and time represented a 
complete departure from the views hitherto emerged in the pre-Buddhistic pe¬ 
riod of Indian philosophy. The Buddhists renounced all conceptions of being as 
permanent, as immutable, as substance and universal. They also rejected all ideas 
about time as unitary, as an all-embracing receptacle and as a real per se. They 
maintained, on the contrary, the idea of reality as flux, involving a conception of 
time as instant and that of being as instantaneous. This has the radical implica¬ 
tion, as the technicalities of Buddhist thinking disclose, that difference of time 
involves difference of being, so much so that at no two instants can a thing be 
said to remain identical. The position is even more drastic. As we shall see 
shortly, the Buddhist brings out the idea, that being as instantaneous and time as 
instant coalesce ontologically, their so-called separation can only be attributed to 
an arbitrary linguistic convention. 

The pressure and the challenge of this radical view gave rise to polemics. With 
the advent of Buddhism the history of Indian philosophy underwent a long 
period of growth and multifaceted development. Conflicts amongst the Brahma¬ 
nical, Buddhist and Jaina schools - leading to a full disclosure of the philosophi¬ 
cal implications and consequences of their respective positions - constituted a 
driving force towards coherency and consistency and thereby contributed to 
greater maturity of philosophical thinking. Some of these exchanges resulting 
from philosophical disputes are selected and dealt with in the next section in so 
far as they are directly relevant and illuminating for a study of the problem of 
time. 

It is a matter of common observation, that destructibility and transitoriness of 
things have often stimulated philosophical and spiritual thinking. The apprehen- 



The Buddhist idea of instantaneous being 


81 


sion of the transitory, the impermanent and the ephemeral has led to the quest 
for the contrast which is variously described as the permanent, the unchanging 
and unchangeable, the beginningless and endless, etc. The Brahmanical philo¬ 
sophers, for example, have all admitted such a category in their conceptual 
frameworks irrespective of their monistic, dualistic or pluralistic bias. 

But a remarkable feature of Buddhist thinking is the marked absence of such a 
category which is exempt from change. Their philosophical investigation focuses 
on the idea, that all without exception is subject to change. The Buddhist raises a 
sequel of arguments to demonstrate logically, that what does not change cannot 
be said to exist. This itself introduces a revolutionary way of looking at the 
universe, leading to a reformulation of all philosophical problems which have 
been hitherto conceived in terms of the category of an abiding substance with 
changing attributes and qualities. A thorough rejection of the substance view and 
a full exploration of the idea of pure change is no easy intellectual task. One has 
to watch the steps, by following which the Buddhist insists upon the fact that 
nothing real can be unchanging. Even more, the Buddhist demonstrates that the 
real as changing is instantaneous in character. This is to be contrasted with the 
idea of longer or shorter duration attributed to a changing object by common 
sense or by systems committed to realism. To the Buddhist, to say that existence 
is flux implies its essentially momentary character. 

Again, this signifies that destruction follows immediately after the moment of 
origination. This Buddhist view is opposed by those who maintain that destruc¬ 
tion is not spontaneous but dependent upon additional, external cause and cir¬ 
cumstance, thus involving the idea that the changing object is not necessarily 
momentary. 

Thus, the problem of change and the problem of destruction with their neces¬ 
sary and inevitable implication for the problem of time undergo a revolutionary 
treatment in the hand of the Buddhist. Change is not modification but substitu¬ 
tion of one entity by another; destruction is total and spontaneous. The Budd¬ 
hist claims that an entity perishes and is replaced by another at every instant. The 
idea of time as instant and its inseparability from being - as instantaneous - is the 
key to the Buddhist conceptual structure. An attempt to understand these radical 
ideas of change and destruction , 1 which are evidently not empirical but transcen¬ 
dental notions involving a specific view of time, would lead us to the heart of 
Buddhist philosophy and lay bare the Buddhist logic which is urged upon us in 
support of the position. 

The Buddhist doctrine of universal momentariness ( ksanikavada ) is of vital 
importance for an appraisal and understanding of this novel philosophical per- 


1 See sections V (iv) and VII (iii). 


6 A study of time 



82 


The Buddhist idea of instantaneous being 


spective, which emerged in the history of Indian philosophical thought with the 
rise of Buddhism. 

Buddhist philosophers of all the three periods, into which the history of 
Indian Buddhism is generally classified, have all explored the view of the real as. 
momentary. The doctrine of universal momentariness, however, has received 
various interpretations from the Buddhist philosophers of different schools in its 
different periods of development..We shall take a brief note of these internal 
differences regarding this doctrine of central importance for all Buddhist 
thought. 

But prior to this let us note how the Buddhist establishes the momentariness 2 
of the real. Evidently, it cannot be established by perception. The Buddhist 
proves it by inference. The Buddhists offered a chain of arguments and analysis 
with the view of establishing their principal thesis that the real is momentary. 
Existence and annihilation, change and motion are analysed in order to focus on 
this very idea. Ideas of duration, extension, permanence and continuity are 
rejected dialectically in consistency with their principal thesis. 

The classical Buddhist arguments formulated by the philosophers of the 
Sautrantika school, belonging to the first phase of Buddhism, were to remain 
famous. The Buddhist scholars in the West as well as in India have noted them in 
great detail over and over again . 3 It is a landmark in the history of Indian 
philosophical speculation and opens the source of a new stream of ideas, which 
challenge the entire structure of Brahmanical metaphysics. The Buddhist argu¬ 
mentation aims at repudiating the permanent, the substance, and the universal. 

A summary of the principal steps in their argumentation is attempted here. 
The criterion that distinguishes the real from the fictitious, the Buddhist argues, 
is causal efficiency. In other words, that which is real is essentially of the nature 
of a cause, i.e. it gives rise to effects . 4 The Buddhist attempts to demonstrate that 
causal efficiency is restricted only to the momentary, the permanent cannot be 
causally efficient. The Buddhist argumentation is a specimen of apagogic proof, 


2 In Vaibhdsika Darsana [60] p. 102 the following definition of momentariness is forwarded, 
making use of the concept of abhava , svddhikarana-samaya-prdgahhdvddhikaranaksanasamband- 
hittva - that is, the momentary is non-related to the moment which is the locus of the antecedent 
non-existence of that moment which is its own locus, e.g. if a momentary thing A occupies a time 
T 2 , then it is non-related to the preceding moment T t , which is the locus of the antecedent non¬ 
existence of T 2 . 

3 See Stcherbatsky, Buddhist Logic Vol. 1 [63] Ch. 1 (p. 79-118) and S. Mookherji, The Buddhist 
Philosophy of Universal Flux [61] Ch. 1-2 (p. 1-38). 

4 The Buddhist refutation of other contending theories concerning the criterion of real, although 
highly interesting, is a separate question and is omitted here. 



The Buddhist idea of instantaneous being 


83 


which is directed towards establishing that the momentary alone can be said to 
be real through a repudiation of the permanent. 

The Buddhist points out that causal efficiency can be exercised in either of the 
two conceivable ways, viz. in succession or simultaneity, and that the idea of the 
permanent is incompatible with it. If it exercises causal efficiency in the very first 
moment, can the permanent be said to exist after that? If so, it would give rise to 
the same effects in the second and in all succeeding moments, ad infinitum, 
which is absurd. If it does not produce effects and yet is imagined to be existent, 
it would then have two contradictory qualities, being efficient in the first mo¬ 
ment and non-efficient in other moments. This manifest difference in the nature 
of the permanent points to the fact, the Buddhist argues, that the entity in 
question cannot be the same identical entity of the first moment. The Buddhist 
dictum is: that cannot be one, about which one could predicate contradictory 
characteristics. 5 In other words, to say that the real or the efficient exercises its 
causal power in the very first moment of its existence is tantamount to saying 
that in the succeeding moment it would cease to exist. Efficiency cannot be 
attributed to a perdurable/permanent entity. 

The Buddhist proceeds to show that the permanent can neither be said to 
exercise efficiency successively. If the permanent has the power to give rise to 
effects in succession, it may be asked whether during its execution of present 
efficiency it also has the past and future efficiencies as well. If it has, the Budd¬ 
hist argues, the inevitable result is that it would exercise them at the same 
moment - thus reducing the problem to that of simultaneous production, discus¬ 
sed above. If it does not have the past and the future efficiencies, it can never 
produce the effects. Thus the permanent has present causal efficiency and not the 
future and the past. To say, that the entity continues to be the same through past, 
present and future moments, is to commit the fallacy - as pointed out before - of 
investing it with contradictory qualities, which militates against its identity. At 
no two moments can the entity remain the same. 

The opponents, however, bring forth further objections to the view, playing 
on the idea of auxiliaries ( sahakari ) as necessary for the operation of efficiency, 
although the cause is the same. 

Thus, the Buddhists had to defend their position against the following criti¬ 
cisms: (a) the thing may exercise its power, i.e. produce its effect gradually and 
not all at once, so that it may last for several moments or a period of time. Or (b) 
it may be that it possesses the power but it does not exercise it. Its power remains 
latent, owing to some factors which hinder it from being exercised. 


5 Yo viruddbadbarmadhyasavan nasav ekah. 


6* 



84 


The. Buddhist idea of instantaneous being 


The Buddhists refute both these criticisms by saying that in either case one has 
to say that the cause waits for some other factors in order to produce its own 
effect. From this they conclude that if it waits for some other factor, then by 
itself it has no causal power. For instance, if the cause is A and it has the power to 
produce the effect E, the Buddhists argue, A will do so, i.e. produce E in the 
very first moment of its existence. If, however, A waits for B in order to produce 
E, we should say that A does not have the power to produce E by itself, which 
contradicts the original hypothesis. 

If one says that A cannot produce E, because O obstructs this causal efficien¬ 
cy, the Buddhists would reply that it would be better in that case to say that the 
causal power does not belong to A by itself, but belongs to A together with the 
absence of O. To sum up, the Buddhists reject the idea of merely latent power, or 
of power which is not exercised. If something has power, that power must be at 
once exercised, and if it is at once exercised, the thing would cease to exist in the 
next moment. The only other alternative to their thesis, according to the Budd¬ 
hists, is that anything may last for ever, which is absurd. 

Thus the permanent, to the Buddhist, coincides with the non-efficient, i.e. the 
unreal. 

In this connection it is to be noted, that in the framework of Buddhist thought 
duration and continuity are nothing but conceptual constructions. The notion of 
duration formed of a chain of successive moments is merely metaphorical, de¬ 
void of any reality. 

The Buddhist philosophical analysis reveals that the real time is instant. Time 
as universal receptacle a la Vaisesika is absurd - as it cannot be said to have 
efficiency apart from the things. Their separation is merely imaginary. The idea 
of time as a real per se is rejected through a refutation of the ideas of duration and 
continuity. 

Stcherbatsky in the following passage puts in an interesting manner the Budd¬ 
hist view that duration is a fiction. 

»If a thing exists at a moment A, it cannot also exist at some moment B, for to 
exist really at the moment A means not to have any real existence at the moment 
B or at any other moment. If we thus admit that the same thing continues to exist 
at the moment B, this could only mean that it at once really exists and does not 
really exist at the moment A. If a thing could have real duration through several 
moments, it would represent a real unity existing at once at different times. 
Either is the enduring unity a fiction and real are only the moments, or the 
moments are fictions and real is only duration. For the Buddhists the moments 
alone are real, duration is a fiction, for if it were a reality, it would be a reality 
existing at different times at once, i.e. existing and at the same time non-existing 
at a given moment]« [63], p. 87. 



Some internal differences regarding the doctrine of momentariness 


85 


The Buddhist tirelessly points out that there is no real continuity and dura¬ 
tion. The homogeneous succession where the posterior moment resembles the 
antecedent gives rise to the notion of so-called duration. In reality, this similarity 
is no continuity of an identical entity, but is due to a lack of perception of the 
subtle distinction of similar moments. In a heterogenous succession the differen¬ 
ce is greater, and therefore the discontinuity is noticed, which is technically 
called the production of a dissimilar moment ( vijatiya-ksana-utpada ). This is 
how the ideas of duration and substance are accounted for from the empirical 
standpoint. These ideas are forged and constructed out of the ultimate real which 
is the point-instant ( svalaksana ) - “the only thing in the universe which is a non¬ 
construction, a non-fiction” [63], p. 106. 

Evidently this specific formulation of the idea of time as instant is intimately 
related to the Buddhist conception of cause. A thorough and searching analysis 
of causation has led to its formulation in terms of functional dependence. The 
doctrine of Pratltya-samutpadaor the law of dependent origination expresses the 
relation of cause and effect thus: this being that happens (asmin sati idam bhava- 
ti). This doctrine, again, is of capital importance for all Buddhist thought. Con¬ 
sequently, it has been interpreted and debated by the different Buddhist schools. 

For the purpose of our present investigation, however, it is vital to note that to 
the Buddhist a non-cause is equivalent to the non-existent. That which is 
real/existent must be causally efficient, and it is only the instantaneous which can 
be so. Time as instant, again, indicates this ever fluctuating, evanescent character 
of being with which it is fused ontologically. 6 Being, time, and causality form a 
conceptual whole in Buddhist thinking. 


(ii) Some internal differences regarding the doctrine 
of momentariness within the Buddhist tradition 


The understanding of the doctrine of momentariness by the Vaibhasika school, 
also belonging to the first phase of Buddhism, features important differences 
from the Sautrantika interpretation of it, which has been just discussed. The 
specific mode in which the Vaibhasika school formulated the idea of momentari¬ 
ness explains why the term sarvastivada 7 was especially coined to designate their 
doctrine. They propounded that all the elements exist in all three times. The 

6 For more on the Buddhist understanding of time, see sections V (iv), VI, VII (iii). 

7 The philosophy of all existents. 



86 


Some internal differences regarding the doctrine of momentariness 


momentariness of the elements consists in their being subject to constant change 
and mutation. But in their noumenal state, so to speak, they are indestructible. 
This position has come down in four versions with slight variations. In the 
Abhidharmakosa of Vasubandhu [51] a good exposition of this stand is given. 
Moreover, both in the Madhyamika-Karika of Nagarjuna [56] and in the Tattva- 
samgraha of Santaraksita [54] the view is referred to and refuted. 

The first objection to this understanding of momentariness consists in label¬ 
ling the position as accepting the elements to be permanent ( nitya ), since they are 
existent in all three times. This, the opponents hold, amounts to giving up the 
doctrine of momentariness. 

The Vaibhasikas have answered to this objection by pointing out that to 
maintain the reality of the elements ( dbarmas ) in three times does not mean to 
accept them as permanent {nitya). Their momentariness is vouchsafed by their 
being subject to the incessant flow of moments, representing their coming into 
being (jdti), decay (jara ), and passing away ( marana) - a sequence which is 
repeated over and over again. If the real were, so to speak, devoid of this 
process, the accusation of giving up the idea of momentariness could be made. 8 

It is interesting to note this Sarvastivada pattern of conceptualisation in the 
context of Buddhist philosophical treatment of being and time. Naturally, many 
scholars have found the Sarvastivada understanding similar to the idea of three 
mutations (parindmas ) described in the Sankhya-Yoga. 

A brilliant attack on this interpretation of momentariness is made by Santarak¬ 
sita in his Tattvasamgraha [54], who found the Vaibhasika understanding totally 
un-Buddhistic. According to the Vaibhasika philosophers the entity is said to 
exist in past, present, and future. This distinction of three times is made possible 
due to the difference of causal efficiency. 

To this Santaraksita raises the question whether causal efficiency should be 
regarded as identical with or different from the entity {vastu). If it is not identi¬ 
cal, he argues, causal efficiency cannot be used as a basis for such usages. But if it 
were, the problem would still remain, as the present causal efficiency was not 
there before and will not be there after. In other words, causal efficiency, being 
only restricted to the present, cannot account for any sequence affecting the real 
or its continuity in three times. 

It is to be noted that the Sautrantika philosophers maintain, precisely therefo¬ 
re, that vastu is existent only at the present moment - when alone it is causally 
efficient -, neither in the past, nor in the future, thus departing from the Vaibha¬ 
sika pattern of understanding, which is found akin to the Brahmanical Sankhya 
and equally un-Buddhistic. 


8 Vaibhasika Darsana, [60] p. 5. 



Some internal differences regarding the doctrine of momentariness 


87 


Again, if it is urged that causal efficiency is restricted not only to the present, 
but is also there before and after as well, the consequence would be that the 
causal efficiency pertaining to the three times is to be taken as one and the same. 
This would lead to the acceptance of the vastu as permanent ( nitya ). The 
Vaibhasikas cannot, therefore, demonstrate the momentariness of the real on this 
premise, as they intend to do. A more serious objection would be that if vastu is 
taken as permanent, the entire chain of arguments, discussed in the previous 
section, would support the Sautrantika thesis, that causal efficiency cannot be 
predicated of it; only the fluxional, the momentary entity can be causally effi¬ 
cient. 9 

The central insight, that is operating in this analysis, is that an investigation 
into the nature of vastu clearly reveals, that the idea of its enduring in three times 
is impossible to maintain. It is equally important to note the conceptual interde¬ 
pendence of causality, being and time in the Buddhist formulation. The critique 
of causality is at once disclosing being and time as ontologically inseparable. It is 
a total rejection of a static view of reality. Thus, the doctrine of momentariness 
contains the fruit of the Buddhist exploration of the problems of being and time. 

Again, in the second phase of Buddhism, which saw the rise of Madhyamika- 
Sunyavada, the doctrine of momentariness received an altogether different in¬ 
terpretation which is described by some as a ‘dangerous crisis’ for the doctrine 
[63] p. 110. 

The protest against the substance-view was there at the very root of Buddhist 
thinking. The Madhyamika school of Nagarjuna offered a major challenge to the 
Abhidharmika position current in the first phase of Buddhism. If the Brahmani- 
cal model of being was rejected already in early Buddhistic thinking, the Sunya- 
vadin Madhyamika philosophers now attempted to expose dialectically the illu¬ 
sive character of the point-instant. 

The dialectical method employed by them is used for the purpose of revealing 
that ‘the world has only phenomenal existence and things are neither transient 
nor eternal, neither produced nor destroyed, neither transient nor eternal, nei¬ 
ther produced nor destroyed, neither the same nor different, neither coming 
forth nor passing away except in appearance. 10 

The Madhyamika standpoint, which sought to transcend the two extreme 
positions represented by the atma- and anatmavadas , reveals a critical awareness 
about the limitations not only of the Brahmanical position, but also equally of 
the difficulties of the philosophical positions that emerged in the first phase of 
Buddhism. 

9 Tattvasamgraha 1786-1856 Karika: Traikalyasiddhi [54]. 

10 Indian Philosophy , Vol. 1, p. 655 by S. Radhakrishnan [8]. 



88 


Some internal differences regarding the doctrine of momentariness 


The Ratna-Kuta-Sutra (Kasyapa Parivarta), belonging to the earliest Mahaya- 
na texts, anticipates and expresses the middle position represented by the school 
thus: 

»‘That everything is permanent’ is one extreme; ‘that everything is transitory’ 
is another ... ‘that atman is’ ( atmeti) is one end (antah); ‘that the atman is not’ 
another; but the middle between the dtma and nairatmya views is the Inexpres¬ 
sible ... It is the reflective review of things ( dharmanam bhuta-pratyaveksa )”. u 

In Nagarjuna’s Madhyamika-Karika [56] (Chapter 19: Kalapariksa) and its 
commentary by Candraklrti one finds a dialectical analysis directly involving the 
problem of time. This investigation has for its aim to expose the untenability not 
only of any idea of time as unitary and absolute, but also of the notion of the 
reality of time as instant. 

The motivation for the Madhyamika dialectic, however, remains the same in 
all cases: 

“It is the consciousness of the inherent contradiction present in the attempts of 
Reason to characterise the unconditioned in terms of the empirical. The dialectic 
exposes the pretensions of speculative metaphysics which seeks illegitimately to 
extend thought-categories beyond their proper field” [62] p. 75. 

The analysis touches upon the pertinent questions which invariably arise for a 
study of time, viz. the conception of time-divisions, the idea of an empty time as 
a real per se or even the idea that treats time as coalesced with being, only to 
focus on the eventual untenability of these positions. 

The existence of time is inferred by some as a basis for the apprehension of the 
three time-divisions such as past, present, and future. The validity of these time- 
discinctions is itself questioned by the Madhyamika philosophers. They challen¬ 
ge the basis on which these time-distinctions are made to rest. The three time- 
distinctions are found to be made in relation to one another, such as the time- 
divisions of present and future are dependent on the past. But in what sense are 
they dependent? The time-divisions are never there all at once; when one is 
present the two others are absent. On the other hand, if they were co-present, 
they would be indistinguishable from one another. Again, if it is argued that the 
time-divisions are not made relative to one another, the question still remains as 
to what serves as basis of these distinctions. A non-relative past, present or 
future is not conceivable. In other words, the attempt to infer the existence of 
time on the basis of the usage of time-divisions is not justified, as the latter is not 
established on a sound philosophical premise. Again, it is significant that time is 
never apprehended independently of the time-divisions. Thus to argue that there 
is time as a real per se apart from the time-divisions makes no sense, as it cannot 

11 Quoted by T.R.V. Murti in The Central Philosophy of Buddhism [62] p. 27-28. 



Some internal differences regarding the doctrine of momentariness 


89 


be established without the latter as vital to its conception. On the other hand, 
time cannot be established on the basis of the apprehension of time-divisions, as 
the analysis has already disclosed its untenability. The Madhyamika philosopher, 
therefore, concludes that a refutation of the validity of time-divisions ( kdla - 
traya-khandana) amounts to a rejection of time as a real per se. 12 

But the opponents protest. They insist on the existence of time as a real per se. 
They point out that since time has magnitude (parimdna ), e.g. moment, day, 
month, etc., it must be existent, as a non-existent thing cannot be said to have 
magnitude, like the sky-lotus. The Madhyamika remarks in reply that only 
when the existence of an entity is established, the question of its magnitude can 
arise. But a want of such a philosophical demonstration - as has already been 
shown - only indicates that the idea of magnitude itself is merely a super¬ 
imposition and, therefore, cannot be used as a ground for accepting the entity in 
question. In other words, the existence of time cannot be established, neither on 
the basis of the apprehensions of time-divisions or time-magnitudes nor inde¬ 
pendent of them. 

The Madhyamika philosophers equally demonstrate the unsoundness of the 
argument involved in the idea of the existence of absolute time as cause for the 
origin, duration and destruction of all that can be characterised as contingent. 
This, evidently the realists’ postition, is neither acceptable. The idea of time as 
absolute, invariable and uniform is incompatible with the idea of cause. The 
causally efficient can never be permanent (unchanging) - the arguments for that 
are well-established in the first phase of Buddhism. Moreover, the self-contra¬ 
dictory character of such notions as origin, duration and destruction has already 
been shown by the Madhyamika dialectical analysis. 13 The Madhyamika directs 
its criticism against those who maintain, that although the idea of time as unitary, 
absolute, etc. is not tenable, the idea of time as instant is valid. The exponents of 
this view point out, that the Madhyamika rejection of the empty, unitary time is 
based on the fact, that time is never apprehended apart from the changing entity. 
The reality of time as instant in relation to the changing entity is therefore to be 
admitted. 

As an answer to this the Madhyamika points out, that if the existence of the 
changing entity can be established, the reality of time as instant could be accep¬ 
ted. But as the former ( bhavapadartha ) has already been shown to be untenable 
by prior dialectical analysis, 14 the reality of time as instant is also to be rejected. 

12 Yatasca evam vicarane kdlatrayam nasti, tasmat kalo na vidyate , kdldbhavacca bhavasadbhavo’pi 

nasti iti siddham; from the commentary Prasannapadd by Candrakirti on Madhyamaka Sastra of 

Nagarjuna [57] 19/4. 

13 Madhyamika Kdrikd [56] 7/33. 

14 Madhyamika Kdrikd 1/5-9. 



90 


Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 


The analysis amounts to a refutation of all views about time, which the Madhy- 
amika would label as dogmatic, the aim being to point to its phenomenal cha¬ 
racter. 

It is interesting to note that although the doctrine of momentariness can be 
said to have received a severe blow in the second phase of Buddhism, the theory 
regains its central importance again in the third phase of Indian Buddhism. It 
saw the rise of Yogacara-Vijnanavada, the school of Buddhist idealism, which 
makes a bold use of the idea once again. But here too, there can be traced an 
interesting difference of opinion concerning the status, so to speak, of momenta¬ 
riness - as to whether it holds good only of the empirical or is to be accepted as 
an ultimate metaphysical truth. This question will be discussed in section VII (i), 
‘time and consciousness*. 


(iii) Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine 
of momentariness ( ksanikavada ) 


The history of the philosophical development of Brahmanical, Buddhist, and 
Jaina thought bears the stamp of mutual influence through interaction and con¬ 
frontation of ideas. The philosophical disputes have played a significant role in 
shaping the views in their actual, full-fledged forms and even in influencing the 
specific orientations of the different schools. 

An appraisal of the different philosophical stands regarding the problem of 
time, therefore, calls for an analysis of the polemics launched by the schools 
against their opponents and their defences put forward in reply to criticisms of 
their respective positions. 

In this context of philosophical controversies one becomes aware of the im¬ 
pact of a specific view of time on the remaining major concepts in a given system. 
Time is a fundamental problem of philosophy, and any position taken regarding 
time is bound to influence the entire conceptual structure where it happens to 
figure. 

The decisive role that a specific conception of time plays in a given system thus 
becomes immediately evident as one examines Buddhist thinking. Here one 
encounters the radical consequence, which a serious involvement with the pro¬ 
blem has given rise to. In the Buddhist universe of thought the understanding of 
time as instant has carved out a conception of being entirely restricted to the 
instant which alone has causal efficiency. The idea of time as instant is so vital to 



Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 


91 


Buddhist thought that it can be said to be “the fulcrum on which the whole 
edifice of reality was made to rest” [63] p. 85. 

A searching analysis has convinced the Buddhist that, ontologically speaking, 
neither is there any empty time nor any being which is non-temporal - positions 
which can be found in un-Buddhistic conceptual structures. The Buddhist un¬ 
derstanding of the fusion of being and time, i.e. the ontological inseparability of 
the instant and the instantaneous, has revolutionary impact on all pertinent 
branches of conceptual enquiry, viz. logic, psychology, metaphysics, eschatalo- 
gy, etc., - all bearing witness to the presence of this view as being of central 
importance. 

A study of the contending philosophical stands regarding the problem of time 
is not complete, if it is based only on the expositions of views found within the 
framework of specific philosophical schools. The immense value of the philo¬ 
sophical controversies is undeniable, as they bring into focus the consequences 
that a specific view of time has on other major philosophical issues which also 
have to be reckoned with. They are consequently indispensable for a proper 
assessment of the strength of the philosophical argumentations in support of the 
respective views of time. 

Note, for example, the consequence of the Buddhist idea of the point-instant, 
which asserts that reality is through and through kinetic, leaving no room for 
any stable, stationary entity. This amounts to a total rejection of any so-called 
eternal metaphysical entity or principle, which is cherished in the non-Buddhi- 
stic conceptual structures. Buddhism has been thorough in its repudiation of 
even the so-called enduring empirical object, which is explained as nothing but a 
conceptual construction, a function of our imagination. Thus the discrete, dis¬ 
continuous, atomic view of time is seen as detrimental and destructive of the 
Brahmanical and Jaina metaphysics, demolishing the conception of being that is 
hailed in these traditions. The idea of instantaneous being is therefore vehement¬ 
ly opposed by the Brahmanical and Jaina philosophers. 

The history of Indian philosophy, thus, covers long records of controversies 
centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness. To its opponents the 
doctrine has appeared to be both against reason and experience. The details of 
these exchanges can make volumes, which handle the problem from various 
angles and on different levels. In a set-up where free philosophical exchanges 
were in full swing, where no religion could survive without a philosophical 
backing, the challenge of Buddhism was taken up with utmost seriousness. 

On the other hand, the Buddhist philosophers too were fully aware of the 
challenges that the doctrine of momentariness offered to the Brahmanical as well 
as the Jaina thinkers. Referring to the idea of instantaneous being, the reputed 
philosopher Kamalasila remarks, 



92 


Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 


By proving this our fundamental thesis alone, we could have repudiated at 
one single stroke the God (of the Theists), the eternal Matter (of the Sarikhyas), 
and all the wealth of (metaphysical) entities imagined by our opponents. To 
examine them one by one, and to compose elaborate refutations at great length 
was a perfectly useless trouble, since the same could have been done quite easily. 
Indeed, no one of our opponents will admit that these entities are instantaneous, 
that they disappear as soon as they appear, that their essence is to disappear 
without leaving any trace behind. We, indeed, are perfectly aware that by pro¬ 
ving the instantaneous character of Being in general, these (metaphysical) entities 
would have been eo ipso repudiated”. 15 

The Brahmanical and the Jaina schools, on their part, took up the challenge 
squarely. It is fascinating to go through the wealth of philosophical literature 
recording the battle of controversies, attacks and counterattacks in the realm of 
ideas. 

A survey of these exchanges indicates that the main charges, that the oppo¬ 
nents brought against the Buddhist stand, can be classified under three principal 
headings. They point out that the theory of instantaneous being is incompatible 
with the idea of causation, that it cannot account for such phenomena as percep¬ 
tion, memory, recognition, etc., that it renders ideas concerning moral retribu¬ 
tion, bondage and salvation meaningless. 

It is interesting to see that if the Buddhists have repudiated the permanent on 
the basis of its incompatibility with the idea of causal efficiency, the opponents 
now raise the objection that no invariable concomitance can be established be¬ 
tween the momentary and causal efficiency. The target of the argumentation is 
evidently to demonstrate that the so-called momentary entity can by no means 
give rise to an effect. 

In Jayanta Bhatta’s Nyaya-Mahjarl [27] one finds a relevant, and pertinent 
discussion about these issues. He objects to the Buddhist definition that causal 
efficiency is the criterion of real. The fictitious entity can exercise efficiency as 
well. Moreover, even if it is granted that real is the causally efficient, it would be 
impossible to establish that the causally efficient be momentary. 

The challenge, that he puts forward against the Buddhist position is centered 
on the question regarding the establishment of invariable concomitance (vyapti) 
between momentariness and causal efficiency in the inference by means of which 
the principal Buddhist thesis c all that is real is momentary’ (yat sat tat ksanikam) 
is sought to be proved. 

The details of the discussion focus also on various aspects of the problem of 
causality. One of the points in the chain of arguments is to focus on the idea that 

15 A condensed translation from Tattvasamgraha-Panjika [63] p. 80. 



Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 


93 


in order to give rise to an effect an entity must be existent at least in the moment 
following its production. An entity which exists but for a moment can by no 
means be admitted as causally efficient. 

In this connection, it is of interest to recall the Nyaya-Vaisesika formulation of 
causation and their classification of cause as material ( samavdyi ), non-material 
(asamvdyi), and as instrumental ( nimitta ) - ideas which indicate a conceptual 
structure operating with a conception of time entirely different from that of the 
Buddhists. 

In Sridhara’s Nydya-Kandali [19] an elaborate discussion can be found regar¬ 
ding the problem of causation focusing on the idea of subsidiaries and auxiliaries 
essential for the production of an effect. An admission of auxiliary cause 
amounts to a refutation of the Buddhist position. 

It is to be noted that the Buddhists, fully aware of this, have rejected the idea 
of auxiliary cause. The critique of causality occupies a central position in Indian 
philosophy. The difference in the views indicates the different manner of con¬ 
ceiving time. 

Controversies between the Nyaya and the Buddhist philosophers constitute a 
prolonged chapter of Indian philosophy. The adherents of the respective schools 
carried on the dispute through the centuries, covering a whole range of problems 
of fundamental importance. 

Udayanacarya’s Nydyakusumanjali 1/16 [24] contains significant analyses of 
the idea of momentariness of the real only to demonstrate the untenability of the 
Buddhist doctrine. One of the various objections that he raises is that no percep* 
tion is possible on the Buddhist premise. Perception presupposes a contact 
between the visual organ and the entity, a possibility which is outrooted by the 
proposition, viz. that which exists is momentary. This amounts to saying that 
perception cannot be used as a valid means (pramana) for establishing the 
momentariness of the real. Again, the Buddhist thesis can neither be established 
by inference for the want of invariable concomitance between the middle and the 
minor terms. Again, in the Atmatattvaviveka 16 one comes across elaborate logi¬ 
cal analyses aimed at disproving the Buddhist position. Kumarila Bhatta, a 
principal exponent of the Mimamsa school, has been a strong adversary to 
Buddhist thought. He points out that no cognition could be explained if the real 
were momentary, as the Buddhists claim. In his Slokavarttika 17 he elaborates his 
criticism against the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness. An analysis of the 
knowledge-situation shows that there cannot be any cognition without an 
object. The object is the cause of knowledge, the latter being effect of it. This 

16 By Sri Udayanacarya [23]. 

17 [32] Karika 152-159. 



94 Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 

causal relation between the two, which is the very presupposition for all our 
knowing, cannot be accounted for on the Buddhist premise. The causal relation 
can be effected only if the cause is present when the effect is produced. But if the 
object ceases to be at the moment when knowing is to take place, no cognition 
can at all happen. The argument is aimed at emphasising that the idea of sequence 
is basic to the idea of causation. The antecedence of the cause is to be accepted 
for the production of an effect. Moreover, the cause must not cease already 
before the effect arises. If these conditions are fulfilled, a satisfactory explanation 
for cognition can be found, but it disproves by the same stroke the Buddhist 
doctrine of momentariness. 

It may be said in support of the Buddhist view, that the object and cognition of 
it are simultaneous, i.e. they appear at the same moment. To this Kumarila 
Bhatta remarks that the idea of simultaneity of cause and effect cannot be 
accepted. However minute, and therefore imperceptible, the difference of time 
may be that intervenes between the cause and the effect, e.g. between the lamp 
and its light, the notion of a necessary, invaribale sequence is indispensable for 
the constitution of causal relation. The simultaneous existence of two facts does 
not constitute a causal relation between them. The production of an effect 
implies that its cause is prior to it. Thus, an investigation into the relation 
between the object and its cognition shows the untenability of the Buddhist view 
that all existents are instantaneous. 

The principal objection of the Jaina philosophers against the Buddhist view is 
also akin to the preceding, exposed above. The Jainas also insist that no know¬ 
ledge can be explained on the premise, that all that is real is momentary. No 
knowledge is objectless. The object is prior, knowledge is posterior. If the 
Buddhist idea is to be taken seriously, it would imply that when knowing should 
occur the object is not existent, and when the latter is present, knowing is not yet 
effected. To maintain that these two are simultaneous is as ridiculous as to say 
that the two horns of a cow are the cause of one another . 18 

The impact of the doctrine of momentariness has been most devastating for 
the Brahmanical philosophers regarding the problem of self (dtman). The idea of 
a permanent self constitutes the very foundation of the Brahmanical tradition. 
The Buddhists, however, in consistency with their principal thesis, has denied an 
unchanging and unchangeable self in their account of the subjective aspect of 
mental phenomena. The cherished Brahmanical doctrine of the permanent self is 
replaced by the idea of everchanging fleeting states of consciousness, succeeding 


18 Arhata-Darsana from Sarvadarsanasamgraha [5] p. 53. 



Controversies centering on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 


95 


one another, - momentariness and causal interdependence remaining its impor¬ 
tant features. 19 . 

The Brahmanical philosophers have attacked the idea of momentariness on the 
ground that such mental phenomena as memory or recognition cannot be 
accounted for by the Buddhist theory. The Mimamsaka philosopher points to 
the fact of recognition as clearly indicating the identity of an entity experienced 
at different points of time. If the entity were different at different times - as 
would be the consequence of the Buddhist idea of momentariness - the phe¬ 
nomenon of recognition would be impossible. 20 Again, without the notion of an 
identical subject, the phenomenon of memory cannot be explained. The one 
who experiences and the one who remembers have to be taken as identical 
subjects, a fact which clearly disproves the idea that all is momentary. 21 

The Buddhists, on their part, have not shirked from these persistent attacks 
launched against their position. They firmly guarded their own stand and 
insisted that the entire series of problems ranging from that of causality, 
phenomena like memory, recognition, ideas of action, and even that of rebirth 
can be explained and accounted for in consistency with their own stand. 

The Buddhists have refuted all the different doctrines of soul which in any 
way can be a vestige of the permanent, in the guise of a spiritual or a mental 
substance. They have attempted to demonstrate that memory, recognition etc. 
do not require the postulation of a permanent self. It is indeed with admirable 
logical skill and philosophical depth that the Buddhist philosophers have 
explained the problem of unity of consciousness and the question of ethical 
responsibility all in accordance with their no-substance stand. A brilliant sum¬ 
mary of the main objections is given by the outstanding Buddhist philosopher 
Santaraksita in his Tattvasamgraba [54] in which he subsequently analyses and 
refutes these objections in defence of the Buddhist stand. 

It is not possible here to go into details of these marvellous philosophical 
exchanges. What remains, however, worthy of notice for the present investiga¬ 
tion of the problem of time, are the drastic consequences which emerge from a 
particular view about time. In other words, all these controversies touching 
upon various problems of philosophy can be said to bear witness to the impact of 
how time is eventually conceived by the contending philosophical schools. The 
problems of being and non-being, creation and annihilation all, implicitly, are 
formulated according to a conception of time that a particular scheme of philoso¬ 
phy cherishes. As an instance we take up in the next section the problem of 

19 See section VII (i). 

20 Sabdanityatd * dhikaranam y Karika 414-415 Slokavdrttika by Kumarila Bhatta [32], 

21 Karika 440-444, Ibid. 



96 


Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy 


annihilation vis-a-vis time, a sample of the many controversies which took place 
between the Nyaya and the Buddhist schools. 


(iv) Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy 


The problem of annihilation, akin to the problem of change, has been an interes¬ 
ting topic of debate between the Nyaya and the Buddhist philosophers. Their 
specific philosophical stands regarding the problem at once indicate the diffe¬ 
rences in their respective views about time, which implicitly underly the mutual 
exchanges and argumentations. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika metaphysical framework makes room for the perishable 
and the imperishable as two distinct and mutually exclusive categories of the 
real. The former covers the range of all that is produced i.e. caused and therefore 
has necessarily being in time. The latter includes all that is classified as uncaused, 
consequently indestructible, and therefore can be said to be outside of any influ¬ 
ence of time ( kalaprabhavamukta ). 

Time itself is conceived in this system of pluralistic realism as a real per se, 
without which no talk of annihilation or production is possible. No beginning or 
end can be attributed to time, as it is only on the presupposition of time as the 
universal receptacle that any beginning or end is conceivable or possible. 

To the Buddhist, however, the annihilatory character of existence is universal. 
In other words, the Buddhist argumentation is directed against the idea of any 
imperishable category of existence - which the Brahmanical tradition cherished. 
The Buddhist insists that all, without any exception, is subject to annihilation. 

It is evident that the thesis, that annihilation characterises all existence, is 
implicit in the principal Buddhist tenet that all existence is momentary. The 
philosophical argumentation for establishing the momentariness of all existence 
has already been exposed in a previous section . 22 Here we are to note how the 
Buddhist analysis of the problem of annihilation is worked out consistently with 
their principal thesis viz. that causal efficiency being the criterion of real, all that 
is real is momentary. The momentariness of the existent primarily indicates its 
evanescent character. This implies - as the Buddhist radically formulates it - that 
annihilation of an entity in question is not dependent on any external agent, but 
is inherent in its very existence, meaning that annihilation is to follow 
immediately after the moment of production. 


22 See section V (i). 



Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy 


97 


The Nyaya challenges the Buddhist view. It is to be noted that both the parties 
in dispute agree that all existents that are produced are liable also to destruction. 
(However, to the Nyaya-Vaisesika metaphysical thinking, there are the absolute, 
eternal,entities which are uncaused and imperishable). But the Nyaya understan¬ 
ding of annihilation is distinctly different from that of the Buddhist. The polemic 
between the two schools regarding the problem is recorded both in the Nyaya
