and the Buddhist literature. The Nyaya insists on the fact that annihilation does 
not necessarily follow in the very next moment of the production of an entity, 
nor can annihilation be said to be without an external cause. This is to say that 
according to the Nyaya the contingent entity continues to exist until the causal 
agent necessary for its destruction appears on the scene. 

In the Atmatattvaviveka [23] we find a firm statement of the Nyaya position 
and its objections against the Buddhist view. On the other hand, in Tattvasam - 
graba there is a brilliant record of the dispute summarising the Nyaya objections 
and the Buddhist answers to them. The exchanges are especially significant for 
understanding the differences in their views, which by implication reveal that the 
respective mode of conceiving annihilation is eventually based on their concep¬ 
tion of what is the nature of ultimate real and its immediate bearing on the 
understanding of time. The pluralistic realism of Nyaya-Vaisesika not only lists 
space and time and a host of other entities to be ontologically real, it also admits 
non-existence to be an objective category of reality. It is, in Nyaya terminology, 
a padartha, that is, not merely a thought construction. The Nyaya insists that 
non-existence 23 of the jar (gbatdbhdva) is produced by the stroke of the hammer. 

The Buddhist, on the contrary, does not grant reality to anything but the 
point-instant. He maintains that non-existence has no objectivity. Annihilation 
of an entity implies its substitution by another. In other words, whereas for 
Nyaya non-existence is something over and above the entity annihilated, to the 
Buddhist it is nothing other than the replacement of one entity by another. 

The controversy leads to pointed exchanges. The Buddhist attempts to lay 
bare the absurdity of the realists’ position that annihilation depends on any 
special cause. The Buddhist points out that since existence and non-existence are 
not on a par, non-existence cannot be said to have a cause similar to existence. 24 
To say that annihilation is caused is to admit that the so-called cause produces 
nothing, which implies that the cause is unreal (since it gives rise to nothing). 
Consequently, the implication would be that non-existence cannot be said to be 
real. 


23 Pradhamsabhava. For the Nyaya-Vaisesika classification of non-existence see p. 231. 

24 It is part of the Buddhist understanding of cause, that it should give rise to a positive entity. 


7 A study of time 



98 


Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy 


The Nyaya refutes the argument with the view of disclosing the absurdity of 
the Buddhist claim by pointing out, that if annihilation were to be taken as 
uncaused, it could never take place. To be uncaused implies that the event in 
question is either fictitious or an eternal fact. Annihilation is an event in time, it 
is real, so it must have a cause. 

This leads to a clarification of what the Buddhist exactly means by annihila¬ 
tion. Annihilation implies the instantaneity of being. The production of a point- 
instant implies the annihilation of the previous point-instant. Annihilation is not 
passing into non-being ( abhava ), to which Nyaya grants objective reality. That 
is a hypostatised notion, a conceptual construction. The Buddhist, on the con¬ 
trary, maintains that no external agent can make an entity cease to exist if it were 
not inherently perishable. Again, to grant that it is perishable by nature is also to 
admit of its momentary character. In other words, the Buddhist maintains that 
for annihilation no other additional factor or circumstance is necessary. Being is 
automatically i.e. by its very nature evanescent. But the Nyaya realists insist that 
if annihilation were not contingent upon any other factor, space and time are to 
be accepted as auxiliary causes. To this the Buddhist answer is that the very idea 
of auxiliary cause is unacceptable (as has been shown in Sec. V (i)). The Buddhist 
is fully aware of the incompatibility of the idea of auxiliary cause with the idea of 
instantaneous being, which he is advocating as real, as free from any conceptual 
construction or interpretation. This is why the Buddhist urges that if an entity 
could continue after the moment of its production, it could - so to speak - keep 
on existing forever. This is also why the Buddhist insists that annihilation is 
uncaused (ahetuka) and denies non-existence any ontological status. The treat¬ 
ment of the problem of annihilation is similar to that of change. The core of the 
Buddhist argument remains the same viz. two or more opposed qualities cannot 
be predicated of the same entity. Thus, in every next moment there is not to be 
found the slightest trace 25 of what was existent in the previous moment. 

The Nyaya evaluation of the problem of annihilation is based on a conception 
of time which is widely different from that of the Buddhists. To maintain that 
annihilation must have a cause indicates that the contingent may endure until the 
appearance of the cause for its destruction. This, in other words, is to deny 
momentariness to be the universal character of being in general - as the Buddh¬ 
ists would have it. 

The conception of total annihilation, pertaining to the Buddhist understan¬ 
ding of being as instantaneous is - as Stcherbatsky 26 puts it - transcendental as 
distinguished from empirical destruction, which is caused as in the example 


25 Niranvaya-vinasa. 

26 [63] p. 94. 



Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy 


99 


cited, viz. the destruction of the jar by the stroke of the hammer. The idea of 
total annihilation which is not produced by any external cause is another way of 
pointing to the same fact that instant and the instantaneous coalesce. Santaraksita 
boldly formulates the idea - ‘the instantaneous thing represents its own annihila¬ 
tion*. 27 The evanescent character of being is brought out in this startling manner. 
This analysis of annihilation brings out the radical Buddhist position that the 
dynamicity of the ultimate real 28 is based on the understanding of the fusion of 
being and time. The evanescence of the instant also implies the annihilation of 
the instantaneous. The difference between the terms ‘instant’ and the ‘instan¬ 
taneous* has no ontological basis. 

The precision with which Kamalaslla [54] brings out the exact Buddhistic 
understanding of the terms instant and the instantaneous is remarkable. It makes 
it clear that the instant - in Buddhist terminology - is nothing other than the 
nature of being itself, which consists in its annihilation immediately after its 
production. The instantaneous refers to the entity which has this nature. The 
verbal distinction between the two terms has no corresponding ontological 
difference, it is linguistic and conceptual in character. 

Stcherbatsky [63] p. 92 gives a pointed summary of the Buddhist stand, 
following the Buddhist text of Tattvasamgraha-Panjika , thus: 

‘... just as existence ... is nothing but a name for the things existing, efficiency 
is the efficient thing itself. Time and space are nothing besides the things residing 
in them, these again are nothing over and above the point-instants of which they 
represent an integration ... finally non-existence or the annihilation of a thing is 
also a mere name, nothing over and above the thing annihilated*. 

To focus on the views of time on the basis of their respective analysis of 
annihilation, it is vitally important to note that for the Nyaya philosophers 
absolute time is the substratum, with reference to which events like production 
as well as annihilation of an entity are at all conceivable. As the further elabora¬ 
tion of the Nyaya philosopohical argumentation points to, time, being instru¬ 
mental to all events, is all-pervasive, a real per se. 

Annihilation is thus an event in time, dependent on the external agent 
responsible for the destruction of the entity in question. The fact that annihila¬ 
tion is experienced at a given moment and not at any other indicates its causal 
dependence on some factor outside of the entity in question. This, they main¬ 
tain, cannot be explained on the Buddhist premise that all that is real is neces¬ 
sarily momentary. The contingent is subject to annihilation, but its annihilation 
is not uncaused, and therefore the entity cannot be said to be automatically 

27 [63] p. 95. 

28 Calabhavasvarupa. 


7 * 



100 


Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy 


evanescent immediately after its production. This analysis is evidently carried on 
in a conceptual framework which operates with a view of time which is un- 
Buddhistic to its core. 

To the Buddhist, on the other hand, to accept that destruction is contingent 
upon any external agent would be tantamount to giving up his principal thesis, 
that momentariness is the universal character of all that is real. Moreover, as their 
sharp analysis of being and time indicates, the moment and the momentary are 
inseparable ontologically, the distinction being merely conceptual. Thus time as 
an independent real per se - like the Nyaya philosophers would have it as a 
support for understanding annihilation as an objective fact - is foreign to the 
Buddhist mode of thinking. Annihilation does not imply that it is something 
apart from the annihilated entity, no more than it is dependent on any external 
destructive agent. The momentary entity represents its own annihilation. Effi¬ 
ciency and evanescence are intrinsic to that into which the instant and the instan¬ 
taneous are coalesced ontologically. 

The different analysis of annihilation bears witness to the radically opposed 
views about time that are implied in the Nyaya-Buddhist controversy. To 
Nyaya, the fact of annihilation itself is an event which presupposes time as an 
imperishable real per se. It is with reference to and in the substratum of this 
ontological real that the non-existence of the contingent is conceivable or is 
possible. To the Buddhist this conception of unitary, absolute time is nothing but 
a thought-construction, having no basis in reality. The real time is the discrete 
instant, which is by its very nature perishable. Moreover, the perishability of the 
instant implies the annihilation of being. Being and time are inseparable ontolo¬ 
gically, expressed as the point-instant whose evanescence is not dependent on 
any additional external cause. This constant annihilation is at the very root of the 
Buddhist understanding that real is dynamic. 



VI 


A note on the problem of time in the perspective of philosophy of 
language and the idea of the timeless as inexpressible 


The philosophy of time knows of inner divergence of many sorts. Some hold 
time to be ubiquitous, others as atomic; some maintain it to be a purely subjec¬ 
tive construction, others insist that it is an objective fact - a real per se. 

However, the linguistic usage of the word ‘time’ is current not only in com¬ 
mon parlance but also in the philosophical literature of the different schools. In 
this connection the question can be raised that if the word ‘time* is universally 
used, is it not necessarily a pointer which indicates that there is a corresponding 
reality? To put it differently, can there be a category of language without a 
category of being? 

The different schools of Indian philosophy are fully aware of the various 
problems concerning the question of the import of words. The implications of 
language as the universal tool of expression itself underwent in due course a 
careful philosophical scrutiny. The history of Indian philosophy of language can 
be traced all the way back to the Vedas. The ancient school of grammar and the 
school of MImamsa are well-known for their intense preoccupation with many 
subtleties pertaining to the area of philosophy of language, and which contain to 
a large extent a systematic working out of the suggestions found in the Vedas. It 
is an enormous field of enquiry giving rise to theories about the origin of lan¬ 
guage, about the relation between the sounds, the letters and the word, regard¬ 
ing whether language reveals or distorts reality etc. etc. 

For the present investigation on time, however, only certain aspects of some 
theories and their implications will be considered with the view of noting how in 
a given system the philosophy of time and the philosophy of language can be 
shown to be in harmony with one another. 

The understanding of the interconnection between language, thought and 
reality are open to diverse interpretations. The rival views have, at least 
implicitly, specific metaphysical presuppositions. The Nyaya-Buddhist con¬ 
troversies are repeated once again in this area. 



102 The problem of time in the perspective of philosophy of language 

The realistic schools of Nyaya-Vaisesika assert boldly that the nameable 
(abhidheya ), the knowable (jheya ) and the real (prameya) are in one to one 
relationship. Language, according to the Indian realists, reveals reality. Thus, the 
linguistic usage ( vyavahdra ) of time ( kdla ) at once indicates it as knowable and as 
objectively real. They maintain that words and facts are congruent with one 
another. It is the objective reality which underlies all linguistic usages. Thus, the 
ontological reality of time in the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophy corresponds to the 
category of language. 1 

Turning to Buddhism, however, one faces an entirely opposed understanding 
of the significance of words. The realists* position, that words necessarily refer 
to reality, is interpreted by the Buddhist as nothing but an unwarranted assump¬ 
tion. They point out that words deal with concepts which are again nothing but 
subjective constructions. But the ultimate real, for them being the point-instant, 
the uniquely particular ( svalaksana ), is necessarily inexpressible. It is thought 
which constructs the empirical reality (samvrti-sat) which is utterable ( abhi - 
ldpya) y imposing imaginary ( kalpita ) general essence (sdmanya-laksanz) on the 
unutterable, unconstructed ultimate real. 

The Buddhist understanding is nominalistic - “Indeed the name can express its 
own meaning only by repudiating the opposite meaning**. 2 This Buddhist doc¬ 
trine of apoha is particularly significant in connection with their understanding 
of duration as metaphorical and the point-instant as the ultimate real. 

With reference to the analysis of the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness 
previously discussed it is clear, that in the Buddhist conceptual framework there 
is no room for an idea of being which endures more than an instant. The being as 
instantaneous is efficient. It is the object of pure sensation which is said to be free 
from any trace of construction. It is evidently not fused with any verbal express¬ 
ion. The image of the object, invariably associated with a verbal expression, is 
formed in the subsequent moments. The idea of duration is thus a conception 
and therefore a construction, following the first moment of sensation, which 
alone grasps the unutterable particular that the point-instant is. 

In this context it is interesting to read the following lines, which explicitly 
bring out the Buddhist understanding as opposed to that of the realists’: 

“When a word is said to denote an object, it does not do anything more than 
this: the word only generates in the subject’s mind a conceptual image, which is 
distinct and different from other concepts and this conceptual image is believed 


1 See p. 41 for Vallabhacharya’s comment indicating the reality of present time from linguistic 
usages. 

2 As quoted and translated from Pramana-Samuccaya of Dignaga [63] p. 459. 



The problem of time in the perspective of philosophy of language 


103 


to be an external reality existing ahead and independently of the thinking mind* 
[61] p. 119. 

The problem of time drew the attention of the great grammarian philosopher 
Bhartrhari. He has devoted a long section of his outstanding work Vakyapadiya 
to the various philosophical considerations about time. This treatise, entitled 
Kalasamuddesa [74] is an interesting document not only for understanding 
Bhartrhari’s reflections on the subject, but also because it provides a sort of 
catalogue of views and opinions that were current regarding the problem of time. 

As a grammarian philosopher Bhartrhari is deeply concerned about the pro¬ 
blems that arise with the question regarding the tense-distinctions in the Sanskrit 
language. This forms a natural step leading towards the problem of time. His 
involvement with the problem is not, however, merely in order to speculate 
about the metaphysical nature of time. The distinct goal and method, that a 
philosophy of grammar involves, is not lost sight of. After a summary of a host 
of varied philosophical positions dealing with time, it is pointed out in Kalasa - 
muddesa (Karikd 58) that “whether it (time) is only something having an exist¬ 
ence in the mind or whether it has an existence outside the mind, expression in 
words is not possible without recourse to the notion of time” [75]. 

Although records of reflections on time from the quarter of the grammarian 
school are scanty, it can be seen that the interest in the notion of time is natural to 
a grammarian as it forms the very basis of tense-distinctions. Speech is incom¬ 
plete without verbs. Verbs invariably refer to one of the three time distinctions, 
past, present or future. As no sentence is meaningful without a verb, all expres¬ 
sions in words can be said to have a necessary temporal dimension. To a gram¬ 
marian the terminations, which are added to the verbal roots, indicate time as the 
basis of tense-distinctions. 

In this context reference may be made to the great grammarian Panini [Sutra 
3/1/183]. He refers to the present-tense terminations as indicating the present 
time - ‘from the commencement of the desired action to its end\ 3 

In the Mahabbasya of Patanjali [72], one comes across the same idea, that time 
is the basis for the tense-distinctions of verbs. 4 Here the question - shifting from 
grammar to metaphysics - is, if time were not granted a distinct existence, how 
could the tense-distinctions of verbs be accounted for? Despite the same verbal 
root e.g. (to cook) the meaning of the three verbs “cooks”, “will cook” 

and “cooked” are at variance with one another. Grammarians reflecting on the 
question tend to admit the reality of time, which is understood as the 
instrumental cause for the tense-distinctions. In other words, an analysis of the 

3 prdrabdhdparisamaptam - Vaiydkaranabbusanasdra by Kunda Bhatta [73] p. 140. 

4 kriyabheddyakdlastu. 



104 The problem of time in the perspective of philosophy of language 

nature of verbs - found to be indispensable for any meaningful expression in 
words - gives rise to a philosophy of time, which seems close to that of the 
Vaisesika realists. As for example, in Patanjali’s Mahdbhasya 2/2/5 one comes 
across the idea of time as that, in association with which bodies grow and decay. 
Again, the usages of day and night etc. are described as possible due to the 
movement of the sun in connection with time. 

Now, to return to Bhartrhari again, it is important to note that the problem of 
time equally touches upon his ingenious analysis of ‘the word*. This implies the 
necessity of understanding the special significance it obtains as he delves into the 
philosophical considerations about the nature of the word. A brief sketch of the 
principal ideas is attempted here. 

Bhartrhari emphatically declares in his Vakyapadiya that there is no cognition 
in this world, which is not intertwined with the word. 5 Bhartrhari’s understan¬ 
ding of the word, however, is to be sharply distinguished from all other linguistic 
analysis found in the various schools of Indian philosophy such as Nyaya, 
Mlmamsa and the schools of Buddhism. Bhartrhari’s thought, as we shall see 
shortly, acts as a conceptual bridge between philosophy of grammar, metaphy¬ 
sics and theology, converging in the common quest for the Ultimate Reality. 

Pushing the analysis of the word to its furthest limit, Bhartrhari comes to hold 
that the ultimate nature of the word - which is also for him the Ultimate Reality 
{sabdatattva Brahman) - is such that it is of the nature of consciousness, that it 
rests in itself, indivisible, without any trace of sequence. 

For a further clarification of the idea and its relevance for the problem of time 
it is to be noted, that for Bhartrhari the overt speech is not the essence of the 
word. His analysis reveals that three levels of it can be distinguished as involved 
in the process of externalisation of the word which is at first internal. The uttered 
word represents the stage of vaikhari where it is fully externalised and has a 
‘fully developed temporal sequence* [76]. In between these two, the internal and 
the external, lies the stage of madhyama vak , where the word is not yet uttered 
but is already conceived. The temporal sequence is in a subtle form. The inner¬ 
most stage is termed pasyanti vak where no temporal sequence can be traced. 

All verbal communication presupposes these stages. The concern with the 
problem of time is tied to the analysis of the situation of verbal communication, 
which necessarily involves the sequence of sounds. What is to be emphasised 
here is that neither the speaker can utter the sounds nor can the hearer hear them 
simultaneously, yet the sounds having sequence manifest the meaning-content in 
its totality. The fundamental idea is that the indivisible, sequenceless word (akra- 
md vak) is present in both the speaker and the hearer. This is why the sequence 


5 Vakyapadiya-Brahmakanda 123 Kdrikd [75a]. 



The problem of time in the perspective of philosophy of language 


105 


of multiple sounds can manifest, as in the case of the speaker, and awaken, as in 
the case of the hearer, the sequenceless word-unity. 

This doctrine of sphota , as it is called, has brought Bhartrhari to delve into 
metaphysical speculations. Time is described as a power (. sakti ) of the Word- 
Principle which is the Ultimate Reality. It is by virtue of this power, that what is 
essentially one can appear as many and yet maintain its unity just as discussed 
with reference to the multiple-sounds and the word-unity. 

However, there is difference of opinion amongst scholars in discerning the 
question as to whether Bhartrhari propounds a doctrine of vivarta as is found in 
Sankara or not 6 . The word vivarta 7 figures in his work as well as the word 
parinama 8 . Metaphysically, the status that would be ascribed to time by him is 
dependent on the solution of the above problem. If he is taken to hold a position 
similar to that of Sankara’s Brahmadvaitavada , according to which all change 
and multiplicity is reduced to a problem of appearance, the power called time 
(kdlasakti) would then be similar to that of Maya - the inscrutable quasi-positive 
principle which makes the one appear as many, the unchanging and unchangeab¬ 
le as undergoing mutation. But if vivartavada is not the proper conceptual 
model for interpreting Bhartrhari’s ideas about the status of change, time would 
yet have to be understood as non-different (abheda) from the Ultimate Reality as 
a non-dual principle. The closest parallel for the metaphysical pattern would be 
that of Kashmir Saivism. 

It is also of significance to note that on the basis of this conception, Bhartrhari 
worked out the idea of salvation as a raising of the consciousness in order to 
attain union with the Word (sabdapurvayoga) y where all sequence is finally 
transcended. Thus, so to speak, the boundary between theology and philosophy 
of grammar is crossed in the common quest of the Ultimate Reality as the 
presupposition of and yet transcending all manifstations involving temporal se¬ 
quence. 

It is interesting in this connection to note the Advaita Vedanta evaluation of 
language. 9 The objective, empirical language relates words to their referrents in 
terms of species, action, quality or connection. If that is so, the import of the 
word ‘Brahman* becomes an important matter for philosophical scrutiny. The 
chief exponents of the philosophy of Advaita Vedanta like Sankara, Vidyaranya 
etc. have discussed the question at depth, giving rise to the understanding that 
the timeless non-dual real cannot be expressed in language in its fullness. No 

6 Cf. Bhartrhari by K.A. Subramania Iyer [76] and Philosophy of Word and Meaning by G.N.Sa- 

stri [77]. 

7 Vakyapadiya, Brahmakanda-Karika 1. 

8 Vakyapadiya , Kdlasamuddesa-Karika 13. 

9 cf. K.S. Murty [48]. 



106 The problem of time in the perspective of philosophy of language 

word can be said to express it, as the texts point out that Brahman has no species, 
no qualities, no activity, no connection, as it is the non-dual real which has no 
internal difference in it and no other to confront it. The idea of the Ultimate 
Reality as inexpressible is worked out in Advaita Vedanta in tune with the 
Upanisadic (Taittiriya) saying - yato vaco nivartante (wherefrom words turn 
back). 

Yet the Upanisads teach about the inexpressible Ultimate Reality through 
words. The Advaitins are fully aware of this predicament. The function of the 
revealed text here is to indicate and not to describe the Ultimate Reality. This is 
why the value of the negative method (neti neti) is stressed, precisely in order to 
point out that the important function of language is first to superimpose (adhya- 
ropa) false descriptions only to negate (apovada) it hereafter. 

The definitions of Brahman are understood in this manner by the Advaitins 
who are fully aware of the implications of their philosophy of language which 
mirrors the Advaita ontology. Thus such statements as that Brahman is real (sat), 
consciousness ( cit ), bliss ( ananda ) or even infinite (ananta), one ( eka ), are not to 
be understood in the Advaita context as qualities pertaining to the Ultimate 
Reality. There is explicit warning that there is no internal difference in Brahman, 
no substance - quality relation can be attributed to it. The purport of the words 
is to negate unreality or illusoriness, materiality, suffering, finitude and multipli¬ 
city of it. The words are intended, in all cases, to indicate the non-dual real - 
which is timeless. 



VII 


(i) Time and consciousness 


A series of problems of philosophical and theological importance can be 
fruitfully studied under this heading. The area of investigation is broad, and it is 
basic to philosophical and theological conceptual structures across cultural 
boundaries. 

In Indian philosophy itself a study of consciousness, as it is recorded in the 
philosophical texts of the different schools, involves a complex set of ideas. 
Analytical and critical investigations covering a wide range of issues have been 
carried out, focusing on various aspects of this important problem. The history 
of Indian philosophy contains a rich variety of views about consciousness, which 
arose as a consequence of intricate and elaborate enquiries of logical, episte¬ 
mological and metaphysical character. 

In the following discussion, however, only a few selected issues will be raised 
where there is direct bearing of the problem of consciousness on that of time. It 
is interesting in the given historical context to examine some conceptual patterns 
in order to note, how a specific formulation about consciousness is interrelated 
with a certain interpretation of the time-experience. 

Let us first return to the Sankhya account. It has been noted, that in Sankhya 
the problem of time coincides with the problem of change. The pattern of 
conceiving change in this system, with reference to an underlying permanent 
substratum (Prakrti), is distinctly different from that of the Buddhist conception 
of change as a total substitution of one entity by another. 1 

In this section, the Sankhya understanding of change is to be appraised on the 
background of the dichotomy of matter and consciousness. It is evident that the 
postulation of the two principles - Purusa and Prakrti - as ultimate and ontolo- 
gically independent of one another is highly significant for a study of time and 
consciousness. The metaphysical dualism of Sankhya involves a deep phl- 


See section VII (iii). 



108 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


losophical interpretation of the problem of change qua time as well as the 
problem of consciousness. 

The elaborate arguments for the postulation of Purusa as underived and 
uncaused, distinctly different from Prakrti, which are worked out fully in the 
Sdhkhya-Kdrikd (17,18,19) and ably commented upon by Vacaspati Misra in his 
Tattva-Kaumudi, are directly relevant here as they bring out in this process the 
philosophical implications of the principle of change for that of consciousness. 

The principal argument for the postulation of the principle of consciousness, 
i.e. Purusa, seems to be implied and required by the very composite nature of 
Prakrti. The Sahkhya argument is, that all which is composite is meant for 
another’s use ( samghdta-pardrthatvat y Sdhkhya-Kdrikd 17). Prakrti, constituted 
of three gunas and thereby complex, is indicative of a teleology or a purpose and 
hence implies the existence of another principle, Purusa. Purusa, in turn, cannot 
again be said to be composite as this would otherwise lead to the assumption of 
composite objects ad infinitum. Thus, in Sahkhya, if Prakrti is conceived as 
composite, everchanging and object of experience, the principle of conscious¬ 
ness, i.e. Purusa, is understood as uncomposite, un-changing and subject of 
experience. Thus, the Sahkhya text further elaborates the uncomposite nature of 
consciousness by saying that there is absence of the three gunas in Purusa. In 
other words, that which goes into the very composition of Prakrti as dynamic 
material principle, is denied of the Purusa, the principle of consciousness. Con¬ 
sciousness as the subject is characterised in Sahkhya in diverse ways: as the 
controlling agency, 2 as the one who experiences. 3 It is not what Prakrti is, viz. 
the common, objective, productive, composite principle of matter. 

The Sdhkhya-Kdrikd 19 brings forth the further important notions of Purusa 
as witness (saksi) and seer (drastz), developed from the understanding of Purusa 
as conscious and non-object. Prakrti, as the unconscious object principle, cannot 
be made to fulfil the function of witnessing, seeing. Purusa, the unchanging, 
uncomposite conscious subject, witnesses what Prakrti exhibits as it evolves. 
The perceptible, experiencable nature implies that there is an observer and 
experiencer, which is Purusa. It is un-productive ( aprasavadharmt) y inactive 
(akartrtvam). It is the ever-constant ( kutastha ) consciousness witnessing the 
manifold evolutes of the ever-active Prakrti. 

Thus, it is at this point that we have to note that to Sahkhya, the principle of 
materiality with its built-in movement is an irreducible ultimate, as is the princi¬ 
ple of consciousness which is ever-constant. This dichotomy, worked out in 
fullfledged philosophical formulations, contains the very significant insight, 

2 Adhisthanat - Sdhkhya-Kdrikd 17. 

3 Bhoktrhhdvdt - Sdhkhya-Kdrikd 17. 



Time and consciousness 


109 


which has important bearing on the problem of time and consciousness, viz., 
that which changes cannot take note of itself as change, being an unconscious 
principle; that which, on the other hand, takes note of change, is the principle of 
consciousness and is itself devoid of all change. 

The Sankhya system conceives of a teleological bond between the gunas and 
the Purusas, thus accounting for the co-operation between the two ultimate 
principles - Purusa and Prakrti - for all worldly experiences as well as salvation. 

The interdependence of the two principles - consciousness and that of change 
qua time - is conceived in clear terms by the school. The principle of change, 
which is held as independent and objective, can be said to be known only when 
consciousness illuminates it, when the unconscious principle of changing matter 
is presented to the unchanging ( aparinami) principle of consciousness. 

Thus, the time-experience, in the Sankhya conceptual framework, can be said 
to be dependent on the duality of Purusa and Prakrti. The principle of objective 
succession is an aspect of the heterogeneous becoming of Prakrti which, as we 
have noted earlier, is not conceived as an event in an empty time. The temporal 
aspect of concrete becoming involves that it is noted by consciousness as a 
sequence. 

In this connection it is also interesting to note that consciousness, in Sankhya, 
is not understood as an attribute or quality of the self. Consciousness is the self. 
Moreover, the constantly changing mental states are just as much a part of 
Prakrti. 

The Sankhya non-distinction of self and consciousness equally holds true of 
the Advaita Vedanta. But the Advaita Vedanta metaphysical non-dualism does 
not make room for any plurality of selves in the final analysis, all plurality 
belongs to the empirical, the phenomenal (vyavaharika). 

But Sankhya, unlike Advaita Vedanta, admits of a plurality of subjects as 
ultimate and final. It is maintained, as Sankhya-Kdrika 18 points out, that it is 
only on this hypothesis that birth, death, differences in activities, etc. pertaining 
to different individual subjects can be accounted for. The Nyaya-Vaisesika 
schools also make room for a conception of plurality of selves in its metaphysical 
pluralism. 

The conceptual pattern of the self (Atman) as unoriginate and indestructible is 
uniformly present in all the Brahmanical schools, despite their internal differ¬ 
ences. In their confrontation with the Buddhists, it is to be emphasised, that the 
principal target of attack was the doctrine of momentariness, precisely because 
its inevitable consequence is a denial of Atman - the permanent essence, 
untouched by the march of time. 

Evidently, the philosophical considerations that lead Sankhya to postulate the 
unchanging principle of consciousness is teleological in character, whereas for 



110 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


the Nyaya-Vaisesika the permanent self is posited as a substance in which inhere 
qualities such as knowledge, volition, etc. 

But here too, the distinction between the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of con¬ 
sciousness and that of the other Brahmanical schools is to be noted. Conscious¬ 
ness is conceived by the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools not as identical with the self 
but as a quality of the self. Time, space and even the mind ( manas ) are all 
accepted as separate substances. In Sarikhya, however, these are regarded as 
evolutes of Prakrti, arising from the combination of the gunas. These differences 
are reflected equally on their theories of causality. The Sarikhya formulation of 
the effect as an actualisation of the potential and that of Nyaya-Vaisesika as a 
production of a new existent, as we have noted previously, are worked out 
within conceptual frameworks where the understanding of time is distinctly 
different. 

With the advent of Buddhist thought, as has been noted already, the emphasis 
on the idea that ‘all is impermanent* (sarvam anityam) has its inevitable sequel in 
the thesis that ‘all that is, is without substance/self* {sarvam anatman ). The 
doctrine of momentariness is incompatible with the idea of a permanent, persis¬ 
ting self. A consistent carrying out of the claim, that the real is the causally 
efficient and hence momentary, leads to the rejection of the idea of an unchan¬ 
ging self, which stands behind the changing states of consciousness. The Buddh¬ 
ist stand of ‘no-self* (anatmavada) is of great consequence for a study of time 
and consciousness. 

The Buddhist idea of universal instantaneousness leaves no room for any 
abiding ‘mental substance* in the flux of consciousness - the stream of sensation, 
perception, volition, etc. The conscious moments, however, follow one another 
not haphazardly but according to the law of dependent origination, i.e. are 
causally inter-chained: one happening, the other happens. 

The Buddhist philosophers have discussed the problem on both logical and 
psychological levels. The fascinating conversation between the monk Nagasena 
and King Milinda recorded in the early Buddhist text Milinda-Panha [55] bears 
witness to the novelty of the Buddhist doctrine of no-self, the questions that 
arise in reaction to it and the Buddhist responses. 

As mentioned earlier, the Buddhists had to meet the challenge of the objec¬ 
tions that the Brahmanical philosophers brought against the idea of no-self. The 
Buddhists attempted to account for such mental phenomena as memory, recog¬ 
nition, and even the idea of moral responsibility in conformity with their stand - 
the no-self theory. 

To give up totally the idea of self and to replace it by a chain of conscious 
moments is no easy task. Even amongst the Buddhists exceptions to the idea can 
be noticed. The Vatslputriyas, who also claimed themselves to be Buddhists, for 



Time and consciousness 


111 


example, are said to have maintained a notion of a quasi-eternal spiritual sub¬ 
stance 4 . They conceived the principle of individuality (pudgala) neither as dis¬ 
tinct from the mental aggregates ( skandhas ) nor as identical with them. 

It is interesting to see that the Vatslputriyas attempted to avoid the charge of 
eternalism by denying the pudgala or the principle of individuality to be distinct 
from the mental aggregates. On the other hand, to account for the unity of 
consciousness, to give meaning to the idea of action with its moral bearing 
(Karma) they found it necessary to deny the identity of the principle of indi¬ 
viduality with the mental aggregates. 

The Sautrantika philosophers have strongly opposed the idea as un-Buddhistic 
and have rejected it along with the different theories of soul that were current. 
With great philosophical skill they have pointed out, that the idea of a permanent 
self is not only an unnecessary postulate for accounting for mental phenomena 
such as memory, recognition, etc., it is indeed an obstacle for moral responsibil¬ 
ity, bondage and salvation. 5 

The Buddhist philosophers held that the mental phenomena of memory, re¬ 
cognition, as well as the unity of mental life, can be explained with the help of 
the idea of the law of dependent origination and does not call for the postulation 
of a substratum underlying the succession of conscious units. 

In this connection it is also important to note, that although the doctrine of 
soul is rejected by the Carvaka materialists as well, the Buddhist understanding 
is distinctly different from the former. The materialists held consciousness to be 
a by-product of matter. Death of the physical body meant a complete cessation 
of the stream of consciousness. They insisted that a denial of a permanent self 
underlying the variable conscious units ought to make the Buddhists accept their 
position. The Buddhist philosophers, however, were operating with the idea of a 
beginningless consciousness-continuum, which does not cease with death. 

The denial of any idea of substratum of the chain of conscious states does not, 
however, amount to a negation of soteriology. Santaraksita faces the materialists 
squarely on various issues in his Tattvasamgraha [54]. It is, indeed, fascinating 
to observe how the ideas of bondage and salvation are worked out fully in 
harmony with the no-self stand within the Buddhist framework. The idea of 
salvation (Nirvana) is, of course, of central importance to all schools of Buddh¬ 
ism. There exist voluminous discussions on the subject unfolding different 
interpretations, emerging in the various phases of the history of Indian 
Buddhism. 6 

4 See The Soul Theory of the Buddhists , by Th. Stcherbatsky [65]. 

5 A good review of the controversy can be found in The Buddhist philosophy of universal flux by S. 

Mookherjee [61]. 

6 See The Conception of Buddhist Nirvdna y by Stcherbatsky [64]. 



112 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


It is impossible here to go into the details of the different accounts of how the 
various Buddhist schools dealt with these problems. But what is of utmost 
significance for the present study is to note, how a specific mode of conceiving 
time is bound to have its impact on a chain of inter-connected issues, which are 
theologically and philosophically of capital importance. 

The problem of salvation and time, for instance, falls within the general dis¬ 
cussion on time and consciousness. It may be asked whether the temporality of 
consciousness is only empirical and is annulled in the state of Nirvana. 

Reputed Buddhist scholars have debated the question. We shall only refer to 
the Yogacara Vijnanavada - the school of Buddhist idealism - as an example of 
the type of controversy concerning Nirvana, that has relevance for the problem 
of time and consciousness. This school, emerging in the third phase of Indian 
Buddhism, maintained the ontological reality of consciousness. 

A difference of opinion arose amongst the earlier and the later exponents of 
this school regarding whether the absolute consciousness in the state of Nirvana 
is to be considered as momentary or not. According to some, the doctrine of 
momentariness is applicable only to the empirical. One can trace an objective 
succession of moments, following the causal chain, in empirical consciousness. 
But consciousness in the state of Nirvana is beyond the chain of causality; here 
no trace of objective succession can be found. From this it is concluded, as did 
the great master Vasubandhu, 7 that the pure consciousness is beyond time. But 
others like Dignaga, Dharmaklrti and Santaraksita denied this understanding and 
vehemently stuck to the position that momentariness is a basic feature of con¬ 
sciousness, empirically as well as transcendentally. 

This leaves the question open as to whether these later Buddhist philosophers 
were searching for a conception of consciousness as a flux, which is to be 
understood neither as a chain of objective succession of momentary states nor as 
an unchanging eternal substance. 

Thus, a whole series of questions can be raised which are intertwined with the 
problem of time and consciousness. It may be asked, what does the awareness of 
time/change imply about consciousness itself? That which takes note of change, 
is it itself to be conceived as changing or must it be an unchanging principle? The 
Buddhist would cling to the first position, the Brahmanical philosophers e.g. of 
Sankhya-Yoga to the second. Again, the analysis of change would yield different 
philosophical results. Sankhya admits the reality of change, but no category of 
time apart from it. The principle of change implies, for Sankhya, the principle of 
consciousness as unchanging. The analysis of change, for the Nyaya-Vaisesika 
philosophers, points to the reality of time as an ontological category. The idea of 

7 Vijnaptimatratasiddhi - Trimsikd 30 [52]. 



A comparative note on the concept of instant 


113 


the permanent self features as a vital constituent of their metaphysical pluralism. 
To the Advaita Vedanta, the unchanging consciousness is the ontological reality, 
the problem of change qua time is a problem of appearance. 

Issues, such as whether consciousness of object and consciousness of consci¬ 
ousness involve a temporal sequence, or are simultaneous happenings, could also 
be fruitfully studied with reference to all the different schools of Indian philo¬ 
sophy. These questions are taken up, and answers have been worked out both 
from the Brahmanical and the Buddhist premises. The Nyaya-Vaisesika philo¬ 
sophers advocated the first stand, that consciousness of consciousness is subse¬ 
quent to the consciousness of object (< anuvyavasaya ), whereas the Vedantins 
maintained consciousness to be self-revealing ( svaprakasa ). The Buddhists also 
insisted on the self-luminosity of consciousness ( svasamvedana ) and developed 
the idea fully. 

Despite all their internal differences, the schools of the Brahmanical tradition 
remained loyal to the original Upanisadic understanding of the self as unchan¬ 
ging and unchangeable. The idea finds its full-fledged expression in the philoso¬ 
phy of Advaita Vedanta. Consciousness as the ultimate non-dual ontological real 
is characterised as trikalabadhya - i.e. unsublatable in all three times. Profound 
philosophical considerations lead them to emphasise that in no experience is this 
self/consciousness (Atman) ever cancelled. 

The famous expression of the Advaita text, Bhamatl , of Vacaspati Misra [39] 
puts it thus - ‘that which is constant in whatever is variable is different from the 
latter, as a string from the flowers’. 8 The philosophy of Advaita Vedanta stands 
as the culminating point of the Brahmanical understanding of consciousness as 
timeless. 


(ii) A comparative note on the concept of instant (ksana) 


Instant (ksana) is a term used not only in common parlance, but also in the 
philosophical literature of the different schools. No study of the problem of time 
can neglect to take note of the divergent technical employment of this term. A 
philosophical analysis, however, discloses the different views of time, which 
determine the import and significance of the idea of instant in the conceptual 
framework of each school. 


8 Yesu vydvartamdnesu yad anuvartate tat tebhya bhinnam , yatba kusumebhyah sutram. Transla¬ 
tion taken from The Philosophy of Advaita by T.M.P. Mahadevan [47]. 


8 A study of time 



114 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


It is indeed interesting to focus on the question as to how the concept of instant 
is formed and evaluated by the different schools of philosophy, whose approach 
and commitment to the problem of time are widely divergent, even conflicting 
with one another. From our survey of the distinct positions developed regarding 
the problem of time we note, that in all the three philosophical traditions of 
India - the Brahmanical, the Buddhist and the Jaina - the term instant is abun¬ 
dantly used in the philosophical literature. An analytical exposition of the diffe¬ 
rent views of time in such systems as Yoga and Vaisesika, the Buddhism and the 
Jaina enables one to make a comparative note of the concept of instant which 
acquires a special technical significance in each school of philosophy. 

The Vaisesika school, as we have seen, is committed to a view of time as 
unitary, objective and absolute. Instant as time is explained, like any other plural 
uses of time e.g. hours, days etc., as being due to the utilisation of external 
adjuncts such as solar motion for pragmatic purpose which, however, leave the 
essential unitary, indivisible time as a category of existence untouched. In Bhasa- 
Pariccheda 9 we find a pertinent discussion on how in four different ways one 
could designate the use of instant with the help of qualifying adjuncts such as 
action. To the Vaisesika philosophers the use of instant or for that matter any 
other pluralistic employment of ‘time’ presupposes the universal substratum of 
the time-substance ( kala-dravya)> which is a metaphysical reality. The universal, 
unitary time is an independent, absolute existent, whereas the instant is a relati¬ 
ve, pragmatic conception dependent upon its relation to an event. It is an ab¬ 
straction. 

In the Yoga metaphysical structure the concept of time as unitary and as an 
independent category of existene is challenged. The Yoga school declares that the 
s idea of time as instant is objectively real, but the idea of time as duration, 
involving a collection of instants e.g. day, month etc., or that of a sequence, 
implying an objective series of preceding and succeeding instants, is a thought- 
construct, it is an ideal representation. In the Yoga-Bhasya 10 3/52, we find an 
explicit, clear-cut commitment to the idea of reality of the instant and the merely 
subjective, conceptual construction of the sequence, being devoid of objectivity. 
Two moments cannot co-exist. Thus the idea of time as a continuum or as a 
substance in the Vaisesika sense is unreal ( vastu-sunya ) for Yoga. But time as 
instant is real ( vastu-patita ). 

The philosophical opposition between the Vaisesika conception of time as 
unitary and the Yoga view of discrete time is evident. Thus emerges the conflict 


9 By Visvanatha [22]. 

10 Vyasa’s commentary on Yoga-Sutra [10]. 



A comparative note on the concept of instant 


115 


regarding the concept of instant in a philosophical appraisal of the views of time 
within the fold of the Brahmanical tradition. 

For all schools emerging in the Buddhist tradition the idea of instant is philo¬ 
sophically of primal importance. The Buddhist philosophers reject the idea of 
the unitary, all-pervading time-substance of the Vaisesika philosophers. The 
notion of time as an all-embracing receptacle of all that is contingent is attacked 
dialectically in order to show the inner contradictions of the idea. The Buddhists 
advocate a discrete view of time. However, the discrete view of time based on the 
reality of the instant alone acquires altogether a different philosophical dimensi¬ 
on at the Buddhist’s hand. 

The Yoga view obviously is a close parallel to that of the Buddhist, in so far as 
it grants reality only to the present instant and denies objectivity to the past or 
the future instant. Moreover the Yoga interpretation of sequence ( krama) as a 
mental construction bears similarity to the Buddhist understanding. 

However, the interesting analogy between the two views has its serious limits 
which must not be lost sight of. The common denial of the ubiquitous time as a 
unitary, objective reality in the pattern of the Nyaya-Vaisesika does not lead to a 
common understanding in their conception of becoming and being. The distinct¬ 
ness of the two views is clear and pronounced. 

The understanding of time as instant in Buddhism has a profound impact on 
the entire philosophical thinking of that tradition, which leads to a radical trans¬ 
formation of the conception of being - as has been discussed earlier, -, which 
entirely demarcates the Buddhist conceptual model of being and time from all 
the diverse models, emerging from the Brahmanical tradition. 

In the Buddhist formulation, being and time coalesce with one another. The 
conception of time as instant at once implies the notion of being as instantane¬ 
ous. Even more, the instant and the instantaneous form an inseparable unity. 
The instant and the instantaneous, in this conceptual structure, are not concei¬ 
vable one without the other. This understanding of the problems of being and 
time is a radical departure from the traditional Brahmanical thinking and marks 
Buddhism as a distinct philosophical tradition. It is to be also noted in this 
connection, that the radical understanding of time as instant leads to a specific 
understanding of consciousness, which is peculiar to Buddhism. Unlike the 
Brahmanical mode of conceiving a permanent self, the Buddhist works out a 
discrete view of consciousness, accounting for all subjective phenomena on that 
premise. 11 In other words, the point to be brought into focus is that the idea of 
time as instant has so radical an impact, that it leads to a reformulation of all the 
basic philosophical issues in harmony with this insight. Thus, the doctrine of 

11 See section VII (i). 


8* 



116 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


momentariness ( ksanikavada) is of fundamental philosophical importance for all 
Buddhist thinking, despite all other internal differences between the various 
schools of Buddhism. 

The idea of time as instant in the Yoga school has its own distinct philosophi¬ 
cal implication. The evanescent character of the instant does not hold true of 
being. In Yoga thinking time as instant does not imply being as instantaneous. 
The conception of dharmi as explicitly brought out in Yoga-Sutra III, 14 focuses 
on a conceptual model of being which is quite evidently distinct from the Budd¬ 
hist model, where two moments imply two things. According to the Buddhist 
emergence and evanescence of the instant and the instantaneous cannot be conce¬ 
ived as distinct events. More precisely, the two conceptions of time as instant and 
of being as instantaneous are fused together. Obviously, the pattern of conven¬ 
ing birth/production, death/destruction is markedly different here from the Yo¬ 
ga system, as a logical consequence of pursuing this mode of philosophising. 

In the Yoga system, the substance is conceived as enduring, as persisting 
through changes. The analysis of parindma or becoming in Yoga presupposes the 
substratum of Prakrti which continues and supports the process of evolution. 
Again, consciousness is unchanging and unchangeable, for Yoga, in its essential 
nature. The system remains true to the atmavada tradition, conceiving the self as 
timeless. 

For the Buddhist, on the other hand, the understanding of the instant is so 
thoroughgoing, that it leaves no room for any category of existence which is not 
instantanous. We have noted the logical persuasion of the Buddhist which goes 
to show that what is existent is causally efficient and consequently instantaneous 
in character. There is no exception to this rule. 

Another distinct expression of a discrete view of time can be found in the 
philosophical literature of the Jainas. The Jainas challenge, in a like manner to 
the Buddhist and the Yoga, the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of ubiquitous ti¬ 
me. 12 However, an analytical exposition of the Jaina conception of time as 
instant clearly indicates the difference of the view from that of the Yoga school or 
the Buddhist. 

The Jainas, taking their stand on the reality of time, advocate an atomic 
conception. The real, objective time is of the character of instant. This is the 
ultimate and absolute nature of time, which is conceived as a mono-dimensional, 
asymmetrical series constituted of the time-atoms ( kalanu ). The conventional 
time-periods as collections of instants are usages dependent on extraneous fac¬ 
tors such as any standard motion. It is thus a relative, conditional concept 
dependent on extraneous factors, whereas the real, objective time-atoms are 


12 In Prameyakamalamartanda , see section IV (ii). 



The views about time and the problem of change 


117 


discrete and in reality can never be combined. The Jaina conception of time- 
atoms has to be distinguished from the Yoga or the Buddhist notion of time as 
instant. To the Jainas the time-atoms are not only real and objective but also 
eternal, i.e. no beginning or end can be ascribed to them. The main difference 
between the space-atoms and the time-atoms is, that whereas the former are 
capable of combining, the latter are not. The instants are many, conceived as 
forming a linear order. 

Thus, the Yoga, the Buddhist and the Jaina views of discrete time, although 
they all entail a refutation of the Vaisesika view of time as unitary, differ funda¬ 
mentally in their understanding of time as instant. 


(iii) The views about time and the problem of change 


The interdependence of specific formulations of the problem of change and the 
different views of time is evident, as one attempts to analyse the positions held 
by the various schools of Indian philosophy. There is an abundance of philoso¬ 
phical speculations regarding the problem of change. On the one hand is the 
metaphysical dispute as to whether it is essentially a problem of appearance or 
not, on the other hand lies the various contending views proposed by those who 
accept the reality of change. It is indeed interesting to study the different posi¬ 
tions about time, viewing them as diverse conceptual models for understanding 
the problem of change. 

The experience of change is universal, as appearance and disappearance of an 
entity, or as a modification of its states and qualities. Reflections about the 
problem of change are necessarily connected with time. For instance, does chan¬ 
ge imply a lapse of time? Is time, then, something apart from the changing 
object? Does change mean modifications of states/qualities of the identical entity 
or does it involve, on the contrary, a replacement of one entity by another? 

The drive for consistency and precision in formulating answers to such que¬ 
stions has given rise to a variety of philosophical stands. Each system deals with 
the problems in a distinct manner, keeping in harmony with the general characte¬ 
ristics of the tradition it stems from. 

In the Brahmanical tradition, the reality of time is expressed in clear terms by 
the Nyaya-Vaisesika system of metaphysical pluralism. It maintains time to be a 
separate, independent reality, outside and apart from all-that-is-in-time i.e. the 
contingent. No change of any kind is conceivable - whether as occurrence, 
disappearance, modifications of states and qualities - without reference to time. 



118 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


Events are in time. The beginning, continuation or end of an entity is explicable 
only on the basis of an idea of time as an ontological reality. One can meaning¬ 
fully talk about the production of an entity only as its first conjunction with 
time, its destruction refers to its disjunction with time. Time is, therefore classed 
as a nitya-dravya a substantial reality, which is beginningless (anadi) and 
endless (ananta). As it is the very presupposition of all contingency and of every 
possible mode in which change is conceivable, no contingency can be predicated 
of it. Time is absolute, ubiquitious and unitary. 

That the contingent bears a necessary temporal reference, is amply brought 
out by the Nyaya-Vaisesika theory of causality - asatkaryavada. The theory, as 
has been already exposed, focuses on the idea of the effect as a beginning 
(drambha ) of a phenomenon in time. Similarly, the destruction of the contingent 
is the termination of its contact with time. This model for understanding change 
underlies the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of time as the receptacle ( adhara ) of 
all that is contingent (janya ). The production, continuation and destruction of 
the contingent are all events-in-time. To put it differently, that which is concei¬ 
vable as not in time, is either a fictitious entity or an absolute entity like god, 
space, etc., entities listed as nitya in the Nyaya-Vaisesika pluralistic metaphysics. 
In other words, only the uncaused is conceivable as bearing no reference to time. 
The contingent is caused, and all causal operation presupposes time, conceived 
as all-pervasive ( sarvavyapi ). 

It is to be noticed that if according to the Vaisesika philosophers change points 
to the existence of time, time itself is not conceived as changing or moving. Time 
is the static ( niskriya) background which makes change possible and explicable. 
Again, the Vaisesika philosophers are aware that time can be measured by move¬ 
ment e.g. the solar motion, but movement is by no means identical with time. 
This leads to the further question regarding the passage of time. The Vaisesika 
philosophers account for such conventions of past, present and future time as 
involving no real movement or division of time but as due to external determina¬ 
tions, which leave the unitary nature of time unaffected. In other words, what 
changes is in time, but time itself cannot change. Therefore, such conventional 
usages do not imply any change of time but are, on the contrary, made possible 
by the ontological existence of absolute, indivisible time. Thus, for the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika philosophers, the reality of change can be explained only on the basis 
of reality of time. In fact, the changing entity is indeed a valid premise for 
inferring time as an ontological category, which is also indicative of why space 
cannot be made to function for time. 

In the Vaisesika literature, the difference in the apprehension with reference to 
the body of an old man and a youth is analysed only to focus on the idea, that 
changes in the body of the old man point to the reality of time as their instru- 



The views about time and the problem of change 


119 


mental cause. The Vaisesika conviction, that the solar revolutions cannot alone 
be made to account for changes in the bodies, is emphasised. The existence of an 
Ubiquitous, all-pervading substance called time is pointed out as necessary for 
explaining, how the solar revolution can mediate its influence on the body, with 
which it has no direct connection. 

Now that the relevance of the problem of change to the particular view of time 
in the Nyaya-Vaisesika structure is clear, including the system’s pattern for 
understanding production, continuation and destruction of an entity, we turn to 
the Sankhya-Yoga conception of the problem. 

In the dualistic metaphysics of Sankhya-Yoga, the conception of Prakrti 
stands for the reality of change. The conceptual model for explaining change, 
including events as production, continuation and destruction, is however mar¬ 
kedly different from that of the Nyaya-Vaisesika. A careful review of the Sari- 
khya theory of causality - satkaryavada - immediately discloses the pattern. The 
formulation of the problem of change here does not call for a notion of unitary 
time as a separate category of existence. The conception of time as a receptacle, 
an idea cherished by the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, is rejected in this con¬ 
ceptual structure as superfluous, even as merely a thought construction. 

In Sankhya, the process of becoming as a movement from the potential to the 
actual is identical with the causal process. Yuktidipika , 13 commenting on San- 
khya-Karika 15, hints time to be identical with this process. In other words, the 
reality of change does not necessitate a conception of time as apart from the 
changing stuff, unlike in the Nyaya-Vaisesika system. 

Satkaryavada , the Sankhya theory of causality, as has been pointed out pre¬ 
viously, does not require to operate with any notion of vacant time. Change, in 
Sankhya, implies a succession of states of the substance, which persists identical¬ 
ly through them. Change and identity are not mutually exclusive in Sankhya as 
in Buddhist thinking. 

The analysis of the conception of laksana-parinama clearly reveals the prob¬ 
lem of change in relation to time as conceived in the Yoga philosophy. It is 
indicated that the substance ( dharmin ) is that, which remains common to all the 
three stages of its changing qualities, which when sub-latent is called the past, 
when rising, present and when indeterminable, future. Thus the empirical time is 
intimately related to the changing qualities of the substance in its successive 
phases. Shortly, there is no event-in-time conception in this system. The ulti¬ 
mate substance as Prakrti is beyond the three phases of empirical time. Prakrti is 
eternal-in-change. 


13 Author unknown, edited by R. C. Pandeya [13]. 



120 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


Production and destruction in Sankhya do not involve any notion of a passage 
from non-being to being - the effect is pre-existent in the cause; or from being to 
non-being - destruction is a going back to a sub-latent state and not annihilation. 
The substratum is a stable reference for understanding any change as its different 
phases. 

Thus we have noted briefly, how the reality of change is understood and 
accounted for on the basis of distinct formulations of the problem of time in the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika and the Sankhya-Yoga schools, all stemming from the 
Brahmanical tradition. 

The conception of reality of change and along with it that of time is challenged 
in the philosophy of Advaita Vedanta, a system which marks the culmination of 
the Brahmanical tradition. A radical formulation of the conception of being 
discards any understanding of it as changing - as could be found in the idea of 
Prakrti in Sankhya. Change is ascribed an empirical reality, having no ontologi¬ 
cal status. Moreover, the ontological being is pronounced as non-dual - a con¬ 
ception which rejects all pluralistic/dualistic views of being. The idea of being as 
timeless is focused on in the Advaita Vedanta. Time and change are conceived as 
appearance. The ontological being and the empirical time are realities of two 
orders. 

The problem of change, however, undergoes a radical transformation in 
Buddhist philosophy. Change here is not an accidental feature, but it is the very 
core of reality. The idea of a stable stuff with changing qualities is totally 
unacceptable to the Buddhist. An entity cannot change and yet remain the same. 
Change and identity are mutually exclusive. The radical understanding of change 
as expressed in this philosophy is, that it implies a total substitution of one entity 
by another. 

Time is conceived as instant, one instant being superseded by another. At no 
two instants can the entity remain identical. The idea of time as instant and being 
as instantaneous and both as fused one with the other is a philosophical event, 
which at once places Buddhism as an original tradition, breaking away com¬ 
pletely from the dtmvada comceptuel framework. 

The Buddhist conceptual model for understanding production, destruction is 
thus quite distinct. Nothing endures, at every instant the entity is replaced by 
another. The succeeding point-instant represents the annihilation of the former. 
Thus the conventional idea, that a thing is experienced to exist through a flow of 
instants, is a conceptual construction and does not correspond to reality, but - as 
the Sautrantika Buddhist would say - the annihililation of the entity is followed 
by a similar entity in the next instant. 

Let us note that the idea of time as universal receptacle for all that is contingent 
is unacceptable to the Buddhist philosophers. In the Buddhist framework there 



The views about time and the problem of change 


121 


is no room for a conception of time as apart from the contingent entity. The 
conceptual model of point-instant is a mode of conceiving contingency, which is 
in total opposition to that of the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception. 

On the other hand, the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers accept the reality of 
change, but do not accept the Buddhist view that change is replacement of one 
entity by another. In other words, the contingent is not necessarily momentary - 
as the Buddhist would have it. The Nyaya-Vaisesika realists maintain time to be 
absolute and an independent category of reality, the instant being intelligible 
only on the background of the universal, unitary time. 

The Buddhist rejects the idea of universal time, the instant alone is real. It is 
clear, that the Buddhist understanding of change is thorough and total, there is 
no room for any notion of unchanging substance, no category of reality as 
beginningless and endless (i.e. nitya). Thus in the Buddhist framework a concep¬ 
tion of time as that of the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers can have no place. Time 
as instant, being as instantaneous, are the inevitable concepts, when the problem 
of change is understood in the Buddhist manner. Even more, the instant and the 
instantaneous are conceived as coalesced, as fused with one another, giving rise 
to the Buddhist model of the point-instant. The interpretation of change in 
Buddhism is radical, giving rise to a novel conceptual model of being and time. 

Now, let us turn to review the Sankhya-Yoga understanding of the reality of 
change and compare it with that of the Buddhist. We find that the conception of 
Prakrti as eternal-in-change is an idea, which the Buddhists have attacked 
vehemently, as here again there is a model of being, where identity and change go 
hand in hand - unlike in the Buddhist model. The problem of change is a central 
problem for both Sankhya-Yoga and Buddhism. Considerable effort and 
involvement towards understanding change/becoming is evident from the pro¬ 
found analyses of the problem in all its aspects found in their philosophical 
literature. Noticeable is, however, the sharp and subtle difference in their respec¬ 
tive conceptual models. 

Stcherbatsky, in his Buddhist Logic Vol. I, pointedly summarises the differ¬ 
ence between the Sankhya and the Buddhist patterns of conceiving movement. 
To the Buddhist, he writes, "movement consists of moments, it is a staccato 
movement, momentary flashes of a stream of energy”, whereas for the Sankhyas 
“movement is compact, is a legato movement, the momentary changes of a 
fluctuating substantial stuff with which they are identical.” “Everything is 
evanescent”, says the Sankhya, “because although never at rest, it represents 
fundamentally one and the same stuff” [63] p. 19. 

The radical difference in their formulation of the problem of change is 
reflected in their respective distinct formulations of being and time (see section 
VII (iv)). 



122 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


In the Jaina tradition, where emerges a conceptual scheme which seeks to 
compromise the reality of change with the idea of being as unchanging, time is 
conceived in order to fit this metaphysical pattern. Here we find a conception of 
time as real and absolute but not as unitary. Time as instant is real, but these 
instants are present in a linear order. In other words, the position agrees with the 
Buddhist’s in maintaining the reality of time as instant and in rejecting the idea of 
universal time. On the other hand, it agrees with the Nyaya-Vaisesika realistic 
conception of time as absolute, unchanging, but disagrees with the idea that time 
is unitary. In brief, the reality of change as conceived by the Jainas necessitates 
time as instants, which are many, but an absolutely existing category of reality. 

The problems of change and time, involving at once the problem of causality, 
evidently form a set of interconnected problems, which are basic to the constitu¬ 
tion of distinct conceptual structures. As the historical record of ideas shows, a 
specific stand regarding any one of these problems necessarily influences the 
formulation of the views concerning the others. 

The problem of change is a challenge faced by the Brahmanical, the Buddhist 
and the Jaina philosophers; each, however, sets forth a model, which is in 
consistency with his own metaphysical stand. As the study shows, in each 
system the thought-pattern is different. Despite the internal differences, it seems 
that the understanding of time and change is greatly influenced by the concep¬ 
tion of being in each tradition. 

The Brahmanical tradition, loyal to the Upanisadic conception of Atman as 
the unchanging and unchangeable reality, developed the alternative metaphysical 
structures, giving considerable thought to the problem of time and change. The 
Nyaya-Vaisesika and the Sarikhya-Yoga schools formulated differently their 
positions regarding the problems of change, causality and time, but both systems 
despite their serious involvement with the aforesaid problems modelled their 
formulations following the conception of being in the atmavada tradition. In 
their metaphysical systems - dualistic and pluralistic - the important core 
remained - viz. the Atman, an ontological category, untouched by time. 

In the system of Advaita Vedanta, where the Brahmanical tradition expresses 
its conception of being in its radical form, the metaphysical structure rejects 
pluralistic, dualistic models. The system, as the very name indicates, advocates a 
philosophy of non-dualism, focuses on the reality of the unchanging and 
unchangeable Atman as the one and only reality, thus expressing conceptually 
the Upanisadic vision of Atman as ekamevadvitiyam. Consequently the pro¬ 
blem of change is reduced to a problem of appearance. Time is no more an 
ontological reality but phenomenal ( vyavaharika ). The Nyaya-Vaisesika con¬ 
ception of time as a metaphysical reality is, therefore, untenable in the Advaita 
structure. As we have noted, in his Khandanakhandakhadya Sri Harsa - an 



Being and time 


123 


Advaitin - directly challenges the Nyaya-Vaisesika conception of time and 
attempts to refute it by pointing out the inner contradictions involved in such a 
conception. 

The Buddhist tradition on the other hand upholds a metaphysical perspective, 
which is in total opposition to that of the Brahmanical. It challenges the entire 
conceptual structure of atmavada - in all its diverse forms. It rejects and refutes 
all philosophical conceptions which are modelled on the dtma- pattern. The 
unchanging and unchangeable reality - a conception which dominates the 
Brahmanical tradition of dtmavdda y finds no root in the Buddhist intellectual 
soil, a tradition consequently known as andtmavada. There is no room here for 
any unchanging ontological entity. On the contrary the Buddhist maintains 
firmly, that whatever does not change does not exist. This philosophical stand is 
based on the idea of causal efficiency as the criterion of reality. Their theory of 
causality - the doctrine of pratityasamutpdda - expresses the radical conception 
of change fashioned after the Buddhist vision of reality. It is clear, that the 
problem of change takes an altogether new dimension with the emergence of the 
Buddhist tradition. The involvement of this tradition with the problem at 
various levels - logical, epistemological, metaphysical - uniformly and con¬ 
sistently focuses on the idea of reality as flux. 

It is clear from the above review of different conceptions of change, that each 
position grasps the problem in accordance with its view of time. The attempts to 
formulate the conceptions with precision bring their mutual differences into 
focus. 


(iv) Being and time 


Being and time constitute the fundamental problems that lie at the heart of any 
systematic philosophical enquiry. A perusal of the history of Indian philosophy 
discloses their central role in all the contending conceptual structures. It is 
evident that the problems of being and time have direct bearing on one another. 
A study of time necessarily involves the understanding of the various conceptual 
patterns of ‘being’ as it is clear from a critical investigation into the different 
schools of philosophy. Thus, the whole spectrum of philosophical stands regar¬ 
ding time, which has been the subject of the present study, can be said to display 
along with it the distinct formulations of the problem of being. All views about 
time are intertwined with specific philosophical interpretations of being. In this 



124 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


section we shall note explicitly the various formulations of being, which are 
implied by the different views of time. 

In the conceptual structures developed in the Brahmanical philosophical tradi¬ 
tions there can be found a variety of formulations. 

In the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophical scheme, being (sattva) is an universal 
(jati). The technical significance of the term becomes clear as one examines their 
conceptual scheme. Being is an objective fact, and it is the universal which makes 
it possible to include all entities falling into the categories of substance (time is 
one of the substances), quality, and action, under one class-concept. Non-being, 
which is also taken as an objective category in this system, naturally is excluded 
from it. Moreover, positive categories such as universal, particular and inherence 
are technically ascribed a self-existence ( svatmasattva ), but cannot be classified 
under the universal being. This understanding of being has received severe criti¬ 
cism from the Buddhist philosophers, which will be pointed out shortly. 

The Sarikhya-Yoga understanding as to what constitutes the essence of sat or 
being is not, however, without similarity to that of the Buddhist, in as much as 
the schools recognise it to be efficiency (arthakriyakaritva ) 14 . Prakrti is sat , as it 
is for enjoyment (bhoga) and salvation (apavarga) of Purusa. Purusa, on the 
other hand, is sat as it is the presupposition (adhisthata) for the evolution {pari - 
nama) of the inert Prakrti. But it is needless to say, that the Buddhist understan¬ 
ding of the universal instantaneous nature of being is totally unacceptable to 
these schools. The Brahmanical adherence to the supremacy of being is steadfast¬ 
ly maintained by both Sankhya and Yoga. The Yoga-Sutra itself contains a 
refutation of the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness (ksanikavada). The con¬ 
cept of Purusa is modelled after the Upanisadic view of Atman, the unchanging 
and unchangeable essence in man. Even the concept of Prakrti - being that 
transforms itself {parinaml nitya) - defies the Buddhist understanding, where 
change does not presuppose an underlying permanence. 

The philosophy of Advaita Vedanta contains the most radical expression of the 
Brahmanical conception of being. That alone is declared to be sat which is 
unsublatable in all three times ( trikalabadhya ). 15 Being {sat) is further identified 
with the self-revealing consciousness (at). In the Bhamati (I,l,i) of Vacaspati 
Misra we find a refutation of three distinct views regarding being, only to focus 
on the Advaita conception of being as timeless. 

The first view is that of the Buddhists, which maintains causal efficiency to be 
the criterion. This is shown to be unacceptable. The argument seeks to bring out 


14 Purusdrthakriyaksamam sat , see the explanation of Vacaspati Misra on Yoga Sutra 2/19, in his 
Tattva-Vaisaradi [ 10 ]. 

15 See section III (iii), 'Being as Timeless in Advaita Vedanta*. 



Being and time 


125 


that if causal efficiency is being, then the former has to be again shown as the 
object of a prior causal efficiency leading to the fallacy of regressus ad infinitum. 
Moreover, the illusory object can also be demonstrated as causally efficient. 

The second view is that, according to which being is an universal - historically 
held by the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers. It is pointed out that it will be 
impossible to establish this view philosophically, which insists on being as an 
universal, as being itself cannot be brought under the scope of another universal, 
thus leading to a contradiction. 

The third of these views under scrutiny regards as being anything which 
makes impression on us, i.e. is apprehensible, attributed to the Prabhakara 
Mlmamsaka. This view, it is pointed out, fails to give a criterion, by means of 
which the erroneous could be discriminated from the right knowledge of reality. 

All the above criticisms are intended finally to focus on the Vedantic thesis, 
viz. Being par excellence is that which no time can cancel. 

To the Buddhist, on the other hand, none of these formulations are acceptable. 
He insists causal efficiency to be the criterion of being (sat). Moreover, as further 
analysis discloses, whatever is causally efficient is bound to be instantaneous in 
character (yat sat tat ksanikam). The chain of arguments that the Buddhists used
