to demonstrate, that an entity cannot be said to exist previous or consequent to 
the moment when it exercises efficiency - and thus denying causal efficiency to 
the permanent - has already been discussed. 

However, it is interesting to note that the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers did 
not approve causal efficiency to be the criterion of being. They insisted that it is 
not only being, which can exercise causal efficiency, but non-being (abhava) can 
also just as well. They pointed out that it is not merely conceivable, that non- 
being can exercise causal efficiency, it is even a presupposition for practical life, 
e.g. the absence of physical obstruction can not only be conceived, but is also an 
indispensable condition for movement. It is to be recalled that in the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika scheme non-being is accepted as an objective category of reality. The 
Buddhists, on the other hand, have vehemently criticised the Nyaya-Vaisesika 
understanding of being. All the categories of relations such as universal, inheren¬ 
ce etc. are demonstrated in the Buddhist analysis to be false, which the Nyaya- 
Vaisesika philosophers take as objectively real. 

At the close of this study it is evident, that at the very core of the philosophical 
tension between the Advaita Vedanta - as the culmination of the Brahmanical 
tradition - and Buddhism, lies their radically opposed understanding of being 
and time. The Brahmanical tradition in and through its metaphysical pluralism, 
dualism and monism has attempted to carve out a conception of being, which is 
outside the influence of time. In the philosophy of Advaita Vedanta ‘being as 
timeless’ is alone granted the ontological status (paramartha-sat), which reduces 



126 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


being-in-time to the phenomenal, empirical order ( vyavaharika-sat ). The Ad- 
vaita dialectical attack on the realistic conception of absolute time is motivated 
precisely to bring out, that it cannot be accepted as a real per se. In order to save 
the complete philosophical integrity of this conception of being, that is uncan- 
cellable in all three times ( trikalabddhya ), the Advaita Vedanta has modelled its 
metaphysical structure with a sharp and subtle philosophical caution - it has 
abandoned the pluralistic, dualistic and monistic structures and, as its very name 
signifies, commits itself to an ontology of non-dualism, which steadfastly focu¬ 
ses on a conception of being - the one-without-a-second ( ekamevadvitiyam ). 

Turning to the Buddhist tradition, one is faced with the radical idea of time as 
instant which is integral to being. The course of the history of Buddhism bears 
witness to the more and more stringent formulation of the idea of being as 
instantaneous, disclosing the strong impact of this key idea about time on a 
specific manner of understanding being as instantaneous. We have noted that the 
Vaibhasika school modelled its conception of being close to that of the Sankhya 
view of Prakrti. Their idea of the ever-changing character of being, which yet in 
its noumenal character ( dharma-svabhdva ) remains in three times, did not satis¬ 
fy the Sautrantika, who ridiculed the conception as a vestige of the permanence 
and therefore as un-Buddhistic. A more philosophically drastic interpretation of 
time as instant discloses its, so to speak, complete infiltration, into being. A new 
conceptual model for understanding being emerges. Not only the conception of 
being as immutable and impartite is abandoned; the pattern, in which the Vai¬ 
bhasika school of the first phase of Buddhism conceived of being, is also found 
to be a half-way position, a compromise. No idea of being, as in the Sarvastivada 
framework, despite its mutations yet existing in three times, is amenable. 

An uncompromising philosophical follow-up of the idea of instant reveals, 
that being is entirely restricted to and partakes of the nature of the instant. The 
fleeting character of time, understood in its radical implications, brings out the 
idea of being as instantaneous. There is no room for the idea of being enduring in 
three times in the manner Sankhya conceived of Prakrti or the Vaibhasika Budd¬ 
hists understood it to be. 

It is very significant that, although this radical interpretation of time and being 
undergoes severe attack from the great Buddhist dialecticians of the Madhyami- 
ka school of Buddhism itself, it yet survives and makes a bold re-appearance in 
the history of Indian Buddhism in its third phase. This understanding of time is 
of capital importance for the Buddhist tradition giving it the flair of originality 
and distinction. A compromise in this philosophical interpretation of time 
would have meant a loss of identity for the Buddhist tradition as the analysis of 
the late Vijnanavada philosophers clearly brings out. 



Being and time 


127 


It is interesting to note in this connection that, although the conceptual mo¬ 
dels for understanding being and time remain the same in the Sautrantika and 
Vijnanavada schools of Buddhism, the conflict between them is centered around 
a realistic or an idealistic interpretation of reality. The Sautrantika philosophers 
were realists. They abandoned the idea of substance in conformity with the 
general pattern of Buddhist thinking. They accepted a chain of conscious mo¬ 
ments on the subjective side and a flow of discrete elements on the side of the 
object. The epistemological situation is conceived along the line of realism. But 
the Buddhist school of idealism, Yogacara Vijnanavada, developed in the third 
phase of its history, launched an attack on the realistic claim of the independent 
existence of object apart from consciousness . 16 They accepted wholly the Budd¬ 
hist thesis that being is instantaneous, but being {sat) here is identified with 
consciousness alone. 

But even at this point there arises a conflict amongst the Vijrianavadins them¬ 
selves. This is a crucial point in our understanding of the vital role, that the idea 
of time as instant plays in Buddhist thought. The point of dispute, referred to 
previously in the study of time and consciousness, centers around the question 
as to whether this instantaneous character of consciousness is to be understood 
as ultimate or not. It is a focal point of interest, as it puts into relief how far- 
reaching and consequential is the notion of time coalesced with being in the 
Buddhist tradition. 

This oscillation between declaring absolute consciousness to be permanent 
(i dhruva , nitya) - as in Vasubandhu - and instantaneous {ksanika) - as in Santa- 
raksita and others - seems natural in a tradition, whose basic postulate was to 
deny the substance-view {atmavada) with all its implications. 

On the Brahmanical side, in the philosophy of Advaita Vedanta, being (sat) is 
also identified as consciousness (cit). The philosophical steps leading to the 
disclosure of being as consciousness is, however, not along the line of subjective 
idealism. Sankara, championing the philosophy of Advaita Vedanta, rejects, as is 
well-known, the Vijnanavada analysis of the epistemological situation. The ab¬ 
solutism of Advaita Vadanta goes along with epistemological realism. 

The philosophy of Advaita Vedanta fulfills the task that the Brahmanical 
tradition sets before itself, by bringing forth to a triumphant glory the idea of 
Being - Consciousness - Bliss ( sat-cit-ananda ). It discards the view of time as a 
real per se, as considered by Nyaya-Vaisesika, and it denies change and multipli¬ 
city ontological status, as advocated by Sarikhya-Yoga, reducing them to the 


Bahirartha-pariksa [59]. 



128 


An overall view of time in Indian philosophy 


realm of phenomena. The doctrine of Maya advocated by Advaita Vedanta is 
indispensable for establishing the idea of Being par excellence (Brahman). 

Thus, if the Brahmanical tradition is seen as achieving a pure conception of 
being freed from any trace of time, so to speak, in the philosophy of Advaita 
Vedanta, the Buddhist tradition can be viewed as reasserting its original intuition 
of the inseparability of being and time in the late Vijnanavadins’ insistence on the 
instantaneity of consciousness as ultimate. The persistent adherence to the idea 
of time as instant and as ultimate, fused with the absolute consciousness, is 
philosophically highly significant. 

Basing itself on the teachings of Buddha, the Buddhists attempted to carve out 
a distinct and original philosophical tradition, breaking away completely from 
the Brahmanical metaphysical pattern. In order to achieve this, the Buddhist 
philosophers embarked on a new conceptual model of being for which their 
main philosophical tool was a thorough new interpretation of time. The ontolo¬ 
gical fusion of the instant with being gave rise to the entirely novel idea of being 
as instantaneous. 

The Brahmanical philosophers, on their side, clearly perceived the philosophi¬ 
cal consequence of the Buddhist interpretation of time for all the varying con¬ 
ceptual models of being that emerged in the course of its history. The total attack 
on the Buddhist doctrine of momentariness launched by all the Brahmanical 
schools without exception emerges as a philosophical necessity in the light of this 
understanding. 

Thus, the problem of time emerges as central and basic to the conflict between 
the Brahmanical and the Buddhist traditions in and through the various stages of 
their philosophical history. At the nucleus of these philosophical traditions lies 
inevitably an interpretation of the problem of time. 



VIII 


(i) The problem of time - an inter-cultural perspective 


Before this study comes to its close, it is valuable to take an over-all view of the 
problems, related to time, hitherto discussed within the framework of the Indian 
philosophical tradition. Here we attempt to extend the field of enquiry to a still 
wider framework such as to include some broad features of the Greek and 
Judeo-Christian contributions, which are exceedingly important sources of ideas 
which go into the making of the very fabric of contemporary western thinking. 

Evidently, this could lead to voluminous studies, as the problem of time has 
offered challenge to the theologian and philosopher across the cultural bounda¬ 
ries. Consequently, the literature covering analysis and expositions of different 
views of time is enormous. 

Our intention here is only to bring into focus a few of the questions directly or 
indirectly relevant for a study of the problem of time, which at once help to 
understand the distinct philosophical tradition of India, side by side noting the 
general conceptual pattern of the Greek and the Judeo-Christian thinking. 

We shall first and foremost take note of the different ideas of creation in order 
to discern the main guiding principle which shapes a tradition. It is needless to 
say that all cosmological speculations inevitably have implications about the 
problem of time. 

A detailed investigation into this material is outside the scope of this work, but 
reference to it is indispensable, as these ideas have bearing on the problem of 
time, opening up an intercultural perspective. Another significant outcome of 
such a study is that when fully explored, it could provide a basis for a fruitful 
dialogue for a possible theology of encounter of religions, to which we shall later 
on make a brief allusion. 

Let us examine the Brahmanical tradition, which offers several distinct philo¬ 
sophical formulations resulting from the cosmological speculations of the princi¬ 
pal schools. 

We have already noted the idea of mechanical combinations of atoms in the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika attempt to understand creation. God is conceived as an efficient 


9 A study of time 



130 


The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


cause ( nimitta katana), the principle of material causation is independent of 
God. The atoms are beginningless and endless, creation and destruction involve 
their combination and separation. 

In the Sankhya-Yoga structure, as we have noted, Prakrti is conceived as an 
independent, beginningless, endless principle - functioning as the material, the 
instrumental and the formal cause - to use the Aristotelian categories. The 
concepts of potentiality and actuality come into play in this structure. Creation 
is an actualisation of that which was potential, whereas destruction involves a 
return to the sub-latent state. 

We have discussed previously in detail the arambhavada of the Nyaya-Vaisesi- 
ka schools along with the specific theory of causality, viz. asatkaryavada, and 
observed its difference from the abhivyaktivada of the Sankhya-Yoga schools 
and their understanding of causality, viz. satkaryavada. We shall not further 
elaborate these positions, but only keep in focus their common and steadfast 
adherence to the principle that being cannot come out of nothing or be reduced 
to nothing. 

The Bhagavadgltd hailed by all denominations, fractions and schools of this 
tradition, clearly expresses the maxim thus: 

na’sato vidyate bhdvo 
na’bhavo vidyate satah 

‘Of the non-existent there is no coming to be; 
of the existent there is no ceasing to be / 1 

The interpretations, that it receives in the hands of the commentators, vary 
according to the conceptual structure of each system. But the tradition does not 
make any exception to this fundamental idea. 

In the philosophy of Vedanta emerge other ideas in keeping with the same 
principle. In the Visistadvaita school of Ramanuja the world is conceived as the 
body of God whereas in the school of Advaita Vedanta the problem of creation is 
reduced to a problem of appearance. The doctrine of Maya advocated by Advai- 
tins focuses on the world as neither having the ontological reality of Brahman 
nor being totally non-existent like the sky-lotus. 

All the different metaphysical structures along with their respective theories of 
causality weave different conceptual patterns, but never violate the principle ex 
nihilo nihil fit. 

It is also important, in this connection, to recall the different philosophical 
positions regarding the problem of time, which our study has brought into 


1 Bhagavadgita 2/16 [ 2 ]. 



The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


131 


focus. The thought models are widely divergent, ranging from time as unitary to 
a discrete view of time, from time as a distinctly separate existent to the view of 
time as inseparable from the process of concrete becoming, from time as a real 
per se to the view of time as appearance. 

The contrast of the views of time in different models of cosmological specula¬ 
tions has to be observed clearly for the purpose of the discussion concerning the 
question of the notion of so-called cyclic time, which will be taken up shortly. 

We shall not dwell any further on the analysis of the various problems regar¬ 
ding the views of time and the cosmological models used by their advocates. We 
shall return to it after noting the conceptual pattern, as it is manifested outside 
the boundaries of the philosophical traditions of India. 

Let us turn to the history of Greek philosophy and note the pattern of cosmo¬ 
logical speculations. There is no conception of absolute creation and destruction 
to be found here. The pattern of cosmological speculations, however much at 
variance from one another, are in keeping with the principle of ex nihilo nihil fit. 
A steadfast adherence to this formula runs through the pre-socratic philoso¬ 
phies, just as much as in the writings of Plato and Aristotle. 

A powerful formulation of the idea can be found in the philosophy of Parme¬ 
nides in his rigorous conception of being, which was to have strong impact on 
subsequent Greek philosophisations. He writes - "only Being is, Not-Being is 
not and cannot be thought”, (Fr. 4,6 f.) Again, "... Being cannot have a begin¬ 
ning or cease to be, for it cannot be created from Not-Being or reduced to Not- 
Being, it was never and never will be but is now, continuous and undivided” (Fr. 
8,5 f.) "... It is motionless and unchangeable, everywhere similar to itself ...”. 2 

It is interesting to note that this conception of being finds its radical metaphy¬ 
sical opposite in the philosophy of Heraclitus. Heraclitus, the immediate pre¬ 
decessor of Parmenides, found in the incessant principle of change - which he 
identified with the substance of fire - the fundamental law of the universe. The 
flux, that he conceived reality to be, is expressed in his writings, claiming “that 
everything flows, nothing is permanent. One can never step twice into the same 
river”, (Fr. 91). 

The metaphysical opposition is total. The problem of change has opposite 
significance in each case. To the Parmenidean view of reality change is illusory, 
to the Heraclitean it is permanence which is unreal. Both regarded them, how¬ 
ever, as due to the deception of sense. This clash of ideas has obvious parallels in 
the Indian tradition - between the atmavada and anatmavada in their radical 
expressions. 

2 Zeller, Outlines of Greek Philosophy , [85] p. 49. 

See also [85] for references to Fragments. 



132 


The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


The natural outcome of the presence of the two extreme views was the rise of 
the three compromise systems of Empedocles, Anaxagoras and Democritus. All 
of them, as their cosmological speculations bear witness to, agreed with Parme¬ 
nides that an absolute creation and destruction is impossible, since being cannot 
come out of nothing, on the other hand the influence of Heracleitos’ thought 
was also strong. 

Zeller quotes Empedocles as holding the view that “what is called creation and 
destruction is in reality the mixture and separation of eternal and unchangeable 
basic substances” (Fr. 8,9) [85] p. 55. 

For Anaxagoras, “all coming into being consists only in a combination, and all 
ceasing to be in the separation of already existing substances, and each change of 
quality rests on a change of material composition” [85] p. 61. 

The third compromise system saw the rise of the atomists. The school, foun¬ 
ded by Leucippus, found its chief exponent in the person of Democritus. Here, 
once again, an attempt is made to reconcile the extreme positions of Parmenides 
and Heraclitus. Democritus maintained all coming into being as a combination 
of atoms and all ceasing to be as due to their separation. But the atoms themsel¬ 
ves “have neither come into being, nor can they cease to be ... capable of no 
qualitative change, but only a change of position ...” [85] p. 66. 

In Plato’s cosmology we find, however, a different formulation. The Platonic 
conception of creation, as found in his Timaeus , is as follows: “The god took 
over all that is visible - not at rest, but in discordant and unordered motion and 
brought it from disorder into order” [78] 30A. 

Aristotle, as is evident from his Metaphysics [80] Bk. IV Ch. V 1009 a, develo¬ 
ped the concept of potentiality as differentiated from that of actuality precisely 
in order to conceive change in keeping with the principle of ex nihilo nihil fit . 

The following comments by Stace, a historian of Greek philosophy, is of 
interest in this connection. 

“Aristotle claims, by means of the antithesis of potentiality and actuality, to 
have solved the ancient problem of becoming, a riddle, propounded by the 
Eleatics, which had never ceased to trouble Greek thinkers. How is becoming 
possible? For being to pass into being is not becoming, for it involves no change, 
and for not-being to pass into being is impossible, since something cannot come 
out of nothing. For Aristotle, the sharp line drawn between not-being and being 
does not exist. For these absolute terms he substitutes the relative terms potenti¬ 
ality and actuality, which shade off into each other. Potentiality in his philoso¬ 
phy takes the place of not-being in previous systems. It solves the riddle because 
it is not an absolute not-being. It is not-being inasmuch as it is actually nothing, 
but it is being because it is potential being. Becoming, therefore, does not 

inirnlvo tlio imnnccikl<i le-in frr\m r» r>rr tr\ cnmptkinrr If lnvrJir^c tU e* tnncifmn 



The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


133 


from potential to actual being. All change, all motion is thus passage of potentia¬ 
lity into actuality, of matter into form” [84] p. 279-280. 

We have now taken some representative examples of the thoughts of pre- 
Socratic Greek philosophers as well as that of Plato and Aristotle - which un¬ 
doubtedly show the flowering of ideas of great diversity - but nowhere have we 
found a transgression of the principle of ex nihilo nihil fit. 

It is to be noted that an analysis of the history of philosophical ideas points to 
the fact, that even in systems where the problem of time is not studied as a 
distinct philosophical problem, the metaphysical kernel of thinking centering 
around such basic questions as being and becoming implicitly contains different 
shades of ideas about it. The relevance of such metaphysical formulations for the 
problem of time can be seen gradually taking shape in the thoughts of the later 
exponents pursuing the same line of thinking. As an example of this, from the 
history of Greek philosophy itself, one could mention the impact of Parmenides 
on subsequent Greek thinking regarding the problem of time. It was precisely in 
support of Parmenides’ philosophy of being as motionless and unhangeable that 
Zeno formulated his famous paradoxes. Zeno’s dialectics directed against the 
reality of motion at once revealed the challenge that is inherent in the problem of 
time, the conception of motion being necessarily connected with the conception 
of space and time. The aim of the dialectics was to silence the opponents of 
Parmenides by pointing out the absurdities involved in no matter which way 
motion may be conceived - as continuous or as discontinuous. 

Zeno’s paradoxes, lending support to the thesis of motionless being of Parme¬ 
nides, demonstrated the unreality of time as well - a theme which has occurred 
again and again in the history of philosophical traditions across the cultures. 
Again, in the history of Greek philosophy itself, this trend of thought can be 
traced much later in the philosophy of Plato, where time is characterised as 
appearance. It is to be noted here that the dialectical defence of Zeno against the 
notion of multiplicity and motion and the philosophical pattern of Platonic 
thought are both deeply influenced by the Eleatic conception of being. The 
influence can still be traced later in neo-platonic philosophy as well. Time as 
appearance - a metaphyical insight which has its advocates in all traditions - is a 
logical sequel of a conception of being, which in its radical formulation denies 
becoming an ultimate metaphysical status. 

The Platonic conception of time unites in it both the Pythagorean and Eleatic 
trends of thinking. Let us first take note of the myth of the creation of time in 
Timaeus [78] 37C-38C, 

"... so as that pattern is the Living Being that is for ever existent, he sought to 
make this universe also like it, so far as might be, in the respect. Now the nature 

t _t>^:___i_i ____:_ :li~ in 



134 


The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


full completeness on the generated thing. But he took thought to make, as it 
were, a moving likeness of eternity; and, at the same time that he ordered the 
Heaven, he made, of eternity that abides in unity, an everlasting likeness moving 
according to number - that to which we have given the name time. 

For there were no days and nights, months and years, before the Heaven came 
into being; but he planned that they should now come to be at the same time that 
the Heaven was framed.” 

Again, a significant idea about time can be read in the following lines, 

“Be that as it may, time came into being together with the Heaven, in order 
that, as they were brought into being together, so they may be dissolved toge¬ 
ther, if ever their dissolution should come to pass; and it is made after the pattern 
of the ever-enduring nature, in order that it may be as like that pattern as 
possible; for the pattern is a thing that has being for all eternity, whereas the 
Heaven has been and is and shall be perpetually through all time”. 

Time thus, for Plato, belongs to the world of appearances. However, he 
conceives it as a numerical moving image of eternity, which is related to the 
movement of the planets: 

"... in order that time might be brought into being, Sun and Moon and five 
other stars - ‘wanderers’, as they are called - were made to define and preserve 
the numbers of time”. 

Plato’s contribution to the problem of time is worthy of notice. Time is not 
understood as an existing frame of the universe but as generated by celestial 
revolutions and yet it is not real. The Platonic understanding of time will again 
be taken up, while we shall attempt to understand the Greek cosmological 
formulations and their relation to time. 

Let us first take note of the Aristotelian contribution to the problem of time. 
It is in his writings, it can be noticed, that the problem of time takes on a 
philosophical depth which is hitherto not found in the Greek philosophical 
literature. Although time is as such not a key problem in the Aristotelian con¬ 
ceptual framework, yet considerable reflection on the problem of time is witnes¬ 
sed in his writings, viz. Physics , Book IV, Chapter X to Chapter XIV [79]. 

These pages of Physics cover a wide range of questions and problems, which 
necessarily come up as one delves into a serious philosophical investigation 
about time. Fully conscious of the enigma that time is, his analysis seeks to 
detect what considerations “might make one suspect either that there is really no 
such thing as time, or at least that it has only an equivocal and obscure existence. 

1) Some of it is past and no longer exists, and the rest is future and does not 
yet exist; and time, whether limitless or any given length of time we take, is 
entirely made up of the no-longer and not-yet; and how can we conceive of that 
which is composed of non-existents sharing in existence in any way? 



The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


135 


2) Moreover, if anything divisible exists, then, so long as it is in existence, 
either all its parts or some of them must exist. Now time is divisible into parts, 
and some of these were in the past and some will be in the future, but none of 
them exists. The present ‘now’ is not part of time at all, for a part measures the 
whole, and the whole must be made up of the parts, but we cannot say that time 
is made up of ‘nows’. 

3) Nor is it easy to see whether the ‘now’ that appears to divide the past and 
the future (a) is always one and the same or (b) is perpetually different” [79] Bk. 
IV Ch. X, 217 b-218 a, (p. 373, 375). 

The many facets of the problem of time, which puzzle the philosophical 
intellect across cultures, find a clear expression as Aristotle proceeds with his 
investigation. The subtle analysis, that follows the above formulation, leads to 
important questions as to how time is related to movement and change, to 
number, to non-existence, even to consciousness. His comments are illuminating 
in each case. We can only cite a few examples of his brilliant analysis. Conside¬ 
ring the problem of time and movement, he writes, 

“Now the most obvious thing about time is that it strikes us as some kind of 
‘passing along’ and changing; but if we follow this clue, we find that, when any 
particular thing changes or moves, the movement or change is in the moving or 
changing thing itself or occurs only where that thing is; whereas ‘the passage of 
time* is current everywhere alike and is in relation with everything. And further, 
all changes may be faster or slower, but not so time; for fast and slow are defined 
by time, ‘faster* being more change in less time, and ‘slower* less in more. But 
time cannot measure time thus, as though it were a distance (like the space passed 
through in motion) or a qualitative modification, as in other kinds of change. It 
is evident, therefore, that time is not identical with movement; nor, in this 
connection, need we distinguish between movement and other kinds of change” 
[79] Bk. IV Ch. X, 218 b (p. 377,379). 

“On the other hand, time cannot be disconnected from change, ... Plainly, 
then time is neither identical with movement nor capable of being separated from 
it ...” 

Many important issues are raised in this connection, and it can be seen with 
what ingenuity he tries to disentangle one concept from another. It is, indeed, a 
pioneering work, of which he is himself aware. After his first formulation of the 
puzzle that time is, he writes - “But what time really is and under what category 
it falls, is no more revealed by anything that has come down to us from earlier 
thinkers than it is by considerations that have just been urged”, [79] Bk. IV Ch. 
X, 218 a (p. 377). 

We shall, for the moment, terminate the discussion of Aristotle after giving 
another example of his awareness of the necessary problem that a study of time 



136 


The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


has to face, viz. that of consciousness. Since the present study has covered the 
problem of time and consciousness in some respects, it is interesting to observe 
how this eminent Greek philosopher conceived of the problem. 

Aristotle is aware that “the relation of time to consciousness deserves exami¬ 
nation ...”. This is followed up in the next paragraph, which runs thus, 

“The question remains, then, whether or not time would exist if there were no 
consciousness; for if it were impossible for there to be the factor that does the 
counting, it would be impossible that anything should be counted; so that 
evidently there could be no number, for a number is either that which has 
actually been counted or that which can be counted. And if nothing can count 
except consciousness and consciousness only as intellect (not as sensation mere¬ 
ly), it is impossible that time should exist if consciousness did not; unless as the 
‘objective thing* which is subjectively time to us, if we may suppose that move¬ 
ment could thus objectively exist without there being any consciousness. For 
‘before* and ‘after* are objectively involved in motion, and these, qua capable of 
numeration, constitute time** [79] Bk. IV Ch. XIV 223 a (p. 419, 421). 

Aristotle’s contribution is of great importance. Ariotti 3 remarks, that “Ari¬ 
stotle ... not only carefully examined their views, but went on to formulate a 
nearly complete theory of time ...’*. 

Now that we have noted, however briefly, the philosophical pattern of cosmo¬ 
logical speculations and some analysis of the problem of time in the history of 
Greek philosophy, we turn to the Judeo-Christian tradition. 

The doctrine of creation ex nihilo , which Christianity holds in common with 
Judaism, stands in contrast to the cosmological speculations found in the Greek 
philosophical conceptualisation. The doctrine denies that the universe is unorigi¬ 
nate or everlasting. “It also excludes the Platonic notion of a Demiurge fashio¬ 
ning the world out of a formless matter and the Neo-Platonic notion of the 
physical universe’s coming to be by emanation from the Absolute. In distinction 
to these ideas the doctrine of creation ex nihilo asserts, that the universe has been 
summoned into existence out of nothing (that is, not out of anything) by the 
creative will and purpose of God.** 4 

Philo of Alexandria is aware of the difference between the Judaic conception 
of creation and the Platonic. He writes, “God when he gave birth to all things, 
not only brought them into sight, but also made things which before were not, 
not just handling material as a demiurge, an artificer, but being Himself its 
creator”, De Somniis y [91], 14, 76. 

Obviously, the formulation of the conception of creation out of nothing in the 

3 The Study of Time II [102]. Here “their" refers to “his predecessors*”. 

4 The Encyclopaedia of Philosophy Vol. 2, p. 105 [105]. 



The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


137 


Semitic account of the absolute beginning of the universe is a departure from the 
Greek mode of conceiving the problem of creation. The confrontation of these 
ideas is naturally a challenge to the respective traditions. Let us note in this 
connection the two following statements, each representing boldly its own phi¬ 
losophical stand - 

"... Non-existence being changed by the Divine Power into Being” - says St. 
Gregory of Nyssa. 5 

"Nothing is ever produced by divine power out of nothing” - says Lucretius. 6 

Our task here is, however, only to search for such exchanges in this controver¬ 
sy, which would throw light on the problem of time eventually. 

Let us first enquire into the term ‘creation ex nihilo*. Erich Frank points out 
that the term “is a rational expression evidently coined as a counterpart of the 
philosophical formula nihil ex nihilo fit . It is foreign to scripture, where the idea 
of creation did not yet constitute a philosophical problem. It first occurs in the 
second century B.C in the Greek version of the Maccabeans (7.28). Subsequent¬ 
ly, the formula is used by Christian thinkers, whenever they wish to express 
their idea of creation in terms of Greek philosophy” [91] p. 74-75. 

Evidently, the idea did not find favour with the Greeks. The opposition is 
however not centered merely in this rational expression, on the contrary, it can 
be said to bear witness to all other differences in ideas which are to emerge. 

Amongst all the new concepts, which the idea of ‘creation out of nothing* has 
brought along, is what is claimed as a linear conception of time as opposed to the 
cyclic understanding of the Greeks. But before we ponder on this important 
question, which has given rise to polemics of different sorts, of inter-cultural 
dimension, it is important to understand the Judeo-Christian conceptual model, 
which is in obvious opposition to that of the Greek cosmological speculations - 
as reflected by these ‘rational expressions’. 

However, as one penetrates into the thought behind the formulation of ‘crea¬ 
tion ex nihilo * and their objection to the Greek cosmological understanding, the 
theological motive comes to the forefront. 

In this connection, let us read from the writings of important representatives 
of both Judaism and Christianity the theological considerations that are opera¬ 
ting, as they attempt to interpret the texts in Genesis - as ‘In the beginning God 
made the heaven and earth*. 

The significance of ‘beginning’ is to be understood as related to time as well as 
with regard to, in what sense precisely it is a challenge to the Greek mode of 
conceiving the origin of the world, etc. 

5 Quoted by Florovsky in Creation and Redemption [90] 

6 Quoted by Frank in Philosophical Understanding and Religious Truth [91]. 



138 


The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


Philo of Alexandria, whose lifetime is supposed to cover that of Jesus Christ 
and John the Baptist, but apparently knew nothing of their life and work, 
already pondered on the question. Equipped in both Judaic and Hellenic 
thought and ideas, he reflects on the account of the world’s creation given by 
Moses, distinguishing the idea from the Greek cosmological speculation. He 
writes, “There are some people who, having the world in admiration rather than 
the Maker of the world, pronounce it to be without beginning and everlasting, 
while with impious falsehood they postulate in God a vast inactivity; whereas we 
ought on the contrary to be astonished at His powers as Maker and Father, and 
not to assign to the world a disproportionate majesty” [86] p. 10. 

Philo’s main objection is that “those who assert that this world is unoriginate 
unconsciously eliminate that which of all incentives to piety is the most benefici¬ 
al and the most indispensable, namely providence. For it stands to reason that 
what has been brought into existence, should be cared for by its Father and 
Maker” [86] p. 11. He argues further, “... but between that which has never been 
brought into being and one who is not its Maker no such tie is formed”. Philo 
therefore condemns it as “a worthless and baleful doctrine, setting up anarchy in 
the well-ordered realm of the world, leaving it without protector, arbitrator, or 
judge, without anyone whose office it is to administer and direct all its affairs. 
Not so Moses. That great master, holding the unoriginate to be of a different 
order from that which is visible, since everything that is an object of sensible 
perception is subject to becoming and to constant change, never abiding in the 
same state, assigned to that which is invisible and an object of intellectual appre¬ 
hension the infinite and undefinable as united with it by closest tie; but on that 
which is an object of the senses he bestowed “genesis”, “becoming”, as its ap¬ 
propriate name. Seeing then that this world is both visible and perceived by the 
senses, it follows that it must also have had an origin. Whence it was entirely to 
the point that he put on record that origin, setting forth in its true grandeur the 
work of God”. 

Thus, the theological considerations underlying the idea that the world has a 
beginning are quite clear, and in the process Philo has also pointed out what 
considerations make him decline the other contending cosmological model 
which claims the world to be unoriginate. 

The utter theological character of these arguments becomes further clear as 
one ponders on the Judeo-Christian understanding of this idea of ‘beginning’ 
vis-a-vis time. Philo is also aware of this question and has reflected on the 
implication of ‘beginning’ in relation to time. 

When Moses says that “in the beginning God made the heaven and earth” what 
he does is, Philo writes, “... taking ‘beginning’ not, as some think, in a chronolo- 



The problem of time - an intercultural perspective 


139 


gical sense, for time there was not before there was a world. Time began either 
simultaneously with the world or after it. For since time is a measured space 
determined by the world’s movement, and since movement could not be prior to 
the object moving, but must of necessity arise either after it or simultaneously 
with it, it follows of necessity that time also is coeval with or later born than the 
world. To venture to affirm that it is elder born would be to do violence to 
philosophic sense. And since the word ‘beginning’ is not here taken as the 
chronological beginning, it would seem likely that the numerical order is indica¬ 
ted, so that ‘in the beginning He made’ is equivalent to ‘He made the heaven 
first”’. 

This gives us a concrete basis for understanding what creation ‘out of nothing’ 
signifies to the Judaic intellect, reflecting on the question, and in what consists 
their polemic with the Greek mode of thinking. 

Let us now turn to Saint Augustine in order to note the Christian understan¬ 
ding of the text of Genesis vis-a-vis the question of time, which he analyses 
remarkably in his theological masterpiece - The Confessions. 

His reflection very clearly puts it into focus, that “In the Beginning Thou 
madest the heaven and earth ...” ([87] Bk. XI Ch. IX) does not signify a begin¬ 
ning in time. He writes, 

“At no time then hadst Thou not made any thing, because time itself Thou 
madest. And no times are coeternal with Thee, because Thou abidest, but if they 
abode, they should not be times” [87] Bk. XI Ch. XIV (p. 262). 

The problem of time acquires a new dimension when considered in the per¬ 
spective of the idea of being as timeless. There is no doubt room for various 
interpretations, as the different conceptual structures indicate. 

However, what is of great interest at this point is to note that the conceptions 
of time and the timeless offer challenge to the theological and philosophical 
minds across the traditional boundaries of all cultures. History of ideas bears 
witness to the formulation of such concepts over and over again. 

It is obvious that a formulation of any conception of the timeless in a given 
conceptual structure has necessary implications for the problem of time. Ex¬ 
amples of the conceptual model of being as timeless are found in all major 
traditions. The Brahmanical tradition especially in itse school of Advaita Vedanta 
expresses a total commitment to a most rigorous formulation of this conception. 
Parallel ideas are not lacking, despite structural differences, in the Greek and 
Christian world of thinking. The formulation of the conception of timeless as 
not only beginningless, endless, but also as that which knows of no change 
whatsoever can be found in all these traditions despite their other important 
differences. 



140 


A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


The equivalent to the conception of kutastha nitya 7 can be found in De mor. 
Munich. 8 as well as in The Confessions 9 of St. Augustine, which also points out 
the theological hierarchy that is there: the timeless as sui generis , the temporal 
having a dependent status. 

Another characteristic way of pointing out the conception of being as timeless 
- which has parallels across the traditions - is to characterise the timeless as ever¬ 
present ( sarvadd-vidyamanatvam ). In other words, being as timeless cannot be 
sublated at any time ( trikaldhadhyatvam ). About it one cannot say that it is not, 
it was not or it will not be. 

Plato in his Timaeus points out that ‘was* and ‘shall be* are forms of time and 
that “we are wrong to transfer them unthinkably” to the being which is not 
subject to time. He continues: 

“We say that it was and is and shall be; but ‘is 9 alone really belongs to it and 
describes it truly; ‘was 9 and ‘shall be 9 are properly used of becoming which 
proceeds in time, for they are motions. But that which is for ever in the same 
state immovably cannot be becoming older or younger by lapse of time; nor can 
it ever become so; neither can it now have been, nor will it be in the future; and 
in general nothing belongs to it of all that Becoming attaches to the moving 
things of sense ...” [78] p. 98 (38A). 

A proper appraisal of the conceptual models of time and timeless, because of 
their very universality, opens up a bridge for further exploration of theological 
and philosophical structures, varying from tradition to tradition. 


(ii) A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


The study of time in its inter-cultural perspective poses a variety of problems 
which call for clarification. The existing literature on the subject is naturally 
enormous, as no philosophical/theological tradition can avoid reflecting on a 
problem of such fundamental importance. 

The attempts at a global appraisal is risky and, as can be seen, often based on 
insufficient informations about other traditions. Not only the philosophical 
ideas but images, symbols and myths stemming from a particular cultural set-up 


7 See section III (iii). 

8 Quoted in section VIII (iv) p. 162 

9 [87] Bk. VII Ch. XI, quoted in section VIII (iv) p. 162 



A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


141 


are liable to misrepresentation and misinterpretation without an intimate acqain- 
tance with them. 

To cite an example, - in a confrontation of the Greek, the Judeo-Christian and 
the Indian traditions regarding the problem of time, the most familiar observa¬ 
tion that is made is centered on the idea of cyclic vs. linear time. It is held that the 
Greek and the Indian traditions have cherished a cyclic conception of time, 
whereas the Judeo-Christian traditions have maintained a linear conception. 
This common point of difference which is often focused upon has, evidently, 
important bearings on different problems connected with time. 

What does the cyclic conception and the linear conception entail about time? 
These are obviously notions drawn on the analogy of geometrical figures, inten¬ 
ded symbolically to represent ideas about time. 

It is, however, not quite so easy to lay one’s hand on a proper formulation of 
these notions, granted that these comments as such are so widespread. Loose 
observations are, however, obtainable which are valuable but not sufficient for a 
proper evaluation. 

The linear notion of time in the biblical context implies that time has a begin¬ 
ning and an end, the cyclic does not grant that. Some consider a cyclic time as 
advocating reversability, others point out that the order of events as such is 
irreversible, but any future event is also a past event - the beginning and the end 
coincide in a cycle. The linear time advocates the unrepeatability, the uniqueness 
of the particulars, it makes room for progress, it makes history meaningful, 
freeing it from mechanical recurrences. It is evident that any attempt at a precise 
formulation would indicate the complexities and difficulties that are involved in 
such geometrical representations of the understanding of time in the different 
traditions. 

Now that we have already discussed the different views of time within the 
philosophical traditions of India, we shall withhold any further comment until 
we take note of the nature of the conflict, as it took shape, philosophically and 
theologically, in the confrontation of the Greek with the Christian thinking. The 
theme of cyclic vs. linear time has already been an issue for a battle of ideas. Let 
us, therefore, note carefully the objections that are raised against the so-called 
cyclic idea of time by its opponents, as this would provide valuable clues to un¬ 
derstand the implications that are read into it. 

At a rather early date of the Christian era, we find Saint Augustine in his City 
of God [88] attacking the idea of circular time, as in Bk.XII, Ch.XIV, XVIII, 
XXI. Thus, in Ch.XIV (p. 63) we read: 

“as those others think, the same measures of time and the same events in time 
are repeated in circular fashion; on the basis of this cyclic theory, it is argued, for 
example, that, just as in a certain age the philosopher Plato taught his students in 



142 


A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


the City of Athens and in the school called the Academy, so during countless 
past ages, at very prolonged yet definite intervals, the same Plato, the same city, 
and the same school with the same students had existed again and again, and 
during countless ages to come will exist again and again. Heaven forbid, I repeat, 
that we should believe that. For Christ died once for our sins, but “rising from 
the dead he dies no more, and death shall no longer have domain over him”” [88] 
Bk. XII, Ch. XIV (p. 63X 

From this significant passage it is possible to gather some of the important 
ideas that are associated with the notion of circular time. ‘The same measure of 
time' repeats itself ‘in a circular fashion’ and along with it recur the same events 
to such minute details that it brings about the same individuals etc. in all exacti¬ 
tude. 

In a closer analysis, one can discern a complex of ideas which underlies the 
concept designated as ‘circular time’ by Augustine. It involves, for example, a 
view about the cosmological processes as strictly periodical. This entails exact 
repetition of all events in the cosmos including the destinies of the individuals. In 
this scheme of thought, thus, there cannot be any room for salvation, which is, 
of course, a matter of great theological consequence. 

These ideas, that Augustine reads into the notion of ‘circular time’ and chal¬ 
lenges, are expressed thus in the following passages: 

“... there will likewise be hereafter an uninterrupted series of revolving ages 
that come and go by. Either these cycles took place in a permanent world or else 
the world, rising and setting at fixed intervals, always displayed as though new 
the same events as had happened in the past and were to come again in the future. 
From this whirligig they are quite unable to free their immortal soul even though 
it has attained wisdom, for in its own uniterrupted circular course it moves back 
and forth between false happiness and genuine unhappiness. 

For how can that happiness which has no assurance of eternity be genuine 
when the soul either is totally uninstructed in the truth so that it knows nothing 
of its future unhappiness or in its happiness has a most unhappy apprehension of 
it?" [88] Bk. XII, Ch. XIV (p. 61). 

We have now before us certain concrete features of the idea of circular time 
and some of its important implications as viewed by Augustine. 

Let us now turn to the history of Greek philosophy in order to obtain more 
information and insight on the topic in question. We find that the cosmological 
models of the Greek thinkers are in conformity with the dogma, which Aristotle 
declares to be “common to all the physicists, that ‘nothing can come out of what 
does not exist’” [79] Bk. I, Ch. IV, 187 a (p. 43). 

The differences in cosmological speculations amongst the Greek thinkers can 
be seen from the critical exposition of such views by Aristotle in his Physics , 



A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


143 


where he critically reviews the positions of his predecessors. To cite an example, 
the difference between the views of Empedocles and Anaxagoras, as he says, lies 
in “that Empedocles supposes the course of Nature to return upon itself, coming 
round again periodically to its starting-point, while Anaxagoras makes it move 
on continuously.” 

However, the idea of world-periods/world-cycles involving a chain of crea¬ 
tion and dissolution of the cosmos has run through the history of Greek 
thought. Anaximanes, Heraclitus, Empedocles, as well as the stoics all indicate 
the belief that the cosmos is eternal but periodically changes and renews itself, so 
to speak. There are, however, different modes in which genesis is conceived, but 
no idea of absolute beginning can be found. 

An important basis for the idea of world-cycle is the astronomical observation 
of the periodical recurrences of the positions of heavenly bodies. It is in this 
connection that the Greek idea of the ‘Great Year’ ( Eniautos ) is formed, being 
the time-period in which the cosmos completes one cycle. This is followed by a 
new cycle and.so on infinitely. 

The idea of an exact repetition of the cosmical revolutions along with recur¬ 
rence of all events in exact details has been referred to the Pythagoreans. Eude- 
mus, a student of Aristotle has referred to this view as follows: “... if one may 
believe the Pythagoreans ... then someday I myself, with this staff in my hand, 
shall talk to you who will sit in front of me, just as you are sitting now, and the 
same will be true of everything else”. 10 This bears witness to the fact that the idea 
of exact recurrence of cosmic processes and that of every event of individual lives 
has been a trend of thinking present in Greece, accounting for ‘a sense of fate ,u 
that was common in the pre-Christian era. 

It is, however, difficult to arrive at a fair evaluation regarding the question as 
to how widespread and accepted the idea was to all concerned or whether it was 
rather one amongst various views that were current. It is clear, nevertheless, that 
the idea of time as associated with circular movement has more than one version. 
It is in Aristotle’s Physics we find, that some such views have been taken up for a 
critical review. The views of his predecessors about time have been noted and 
commented by Aristotle in the following lines. 

“(a) Some have identified time with the revolution of the all-embracing heaven, 
and (b) some with that heavenly sphere itself. But (a) a partial revolution is time 
just as much as a whole one is, but it is not just as much a revolution, for any 
finite portion of time is a portion of a revolution, but is not a revolution. 
Moreover, if there were more universes than one, the re-entrant circumlation of 

10 Quoted by E. Frank [91] p. 83. 

11 Creation and Redemption , by Florovsky [90] p. 127. 



144 


A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


each of them would be time, so that several different times would exist at once. 
And (b) as to those who declared the heavenly sphere itself to be time, their only 
reason was that all things are contained ‘in the celestial sphere’ and also occur ‘in 
time’, which is too childish to be worth reducing to absurdities more obvious 
than itself” [79] Bk. IV, Ch. X, 218 b (p. 377). 

This is instructive as it acts as a check on too hasty a generalisation about what 
could have been the understanding of time in a philosophical tradition like that 
of Greece, which knows of a variety of views regarding specific problems. 

Moreover, the particular Greek view of time that Augustine has in front of 
him - according to which ‘the same measure of time* repeats itself ‘in a circular 
fashion’ - does not seem to have been cherished by at least Aristotle, as we read 
the following passage. 

“Moreover, though time is identical everywhere simultaneously, it is not identical 
if taken twice successively; for the change it measures, likewise, is one when 
considered as present, but not if considered as partly past and partly future. And 
time considered numerically is concrete, not abstract; whereby follows that it 
changes from the former to the latter ‘now’, inasmuch as these ‘nows* themselves 
are different; just as the number of a hundred horses is identical with that of a 
hundred men, but the horses enumerated are different from the men enumera¬ 
ted. Now note further that as there may be movement (of rotation to wit) that 
covers the same course over and over again, in like manner we mark off time by 
the year or by spring or autumn” [79] Bk. IV, Ch. XII, 220 b (p. 397, 399). 

It is illuminating, in this connection, also to read the philosophical explanation 
that Aristotle gives as to why rotation is accepted as a standard for measuring 
time and in what precise sense one habitually speaks of the ‘circle of time’. We 
quote again from his Physics : 

“And now, keeping locomotion and especially rotation in mind, note that every¬ 
thing is counted by some unit of like nature to itself - monads monad by monad, 
for instance, and horses horse by horse - and so likewise time by some finite unit 
of time. But as we have said, motion and time mutually determine each other 
quantitively; and that because the standard of time established by the motion we 
select is the quantitive measure both of that motion and of time. If, then, the 
standard once fixed measures all dimensionality of its own order, a uniform 
rotation will be the best standard, since it is easiest to count. 

Neither qualitative modification nor growth nor genesis has the kind of uni¬ 
formity that rotation has; and so time is regarded as the rotation of the sphere, 
inasmuch as all other orders of motion are measured by it, and time itself is 
standardised by reference to it. And this is the reason of our habitual way of 



A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


145 


speaking; for we say that human affairs and those of all other things that have 
natural movement and become and perish seem to be in a way circular, because 
all these things come to pass in time and have their beginning and end as it were 
‘periodically’; for time itself is conceived as ‘coming round’; and this again 
because time and such a standard rotation mutually determine each other. Hen¬ 
ce, to call the happenings of a thing a circle is saying that there is a sort of circle 
of time; and that is because it is measured by a complete revolution, and the 
whole measurement of a thing is nought else but a defined number of the units of 
its measurements” [79] Bk. IV, Ch. XIV, 223 b (p. 423, 425). 

Again, in Problems , Aristotle has directly posed the question, and his answer at 
once indicates that the closest he could get conceptually to the notion of cyclic 
time would still be not identical to the view that Eudemus or Augustine have 
referred to. The passage is as follows: 

“As therefore in the movement of the heavens and of each star there is a circle, 
what is there to prevent the birth and death of perishable things from being of 
this nature, so they are born or destroyed again? So they say that human affairs 
are a cycle. The suggestion that those who are continually being born are nume¬ 
rically identical is absurd, but we could accept that they are the same in ‘form’” 
[82] Bk. XVII, 916 a (p. 367). 

We shall not, however, pursue the discussion in the Greek context any longer but 
turn to the Indian philosophical scene in order to investigate the import of the 
label of cyclic time in the Indian conceptual framework. The ideas associated 
with the notion of cyclic time - as have emerged in Greek thought and especially 
in its confrontation with Christian thinking - provide a significant background 
for the discussion, as it enables one to discern the distinct characteristics of the 
traditional Indian position regarding the problem. 

It is commonly held that the Indian conception of time is cyclic. Here too the 
designation is often granted and accepted without much further attempt at a 
clear-cut, well-defined understanding regarding the conceptual content of this 
notion. It is, however, important to analyse this designation of cyclic time in the 
Indian context in order to remove any possible misinterpretation regarding its 
implications. 

The idea of world-cycle ( kalpa ) is a general feature of Indian mythology and 
philosophy. In the literature of the Puranas , one encounters a grandiose concep¬ 
tion of the cosmological process in terms of repeated creation and dissolution. 
As an example of the gigantic scale of measurement for time one could refer to 
the Vayu Purana where a world-cycle is conceived as a day of Brahma, the 
creator god, followed by his night, i.e. cosmic dissolution (pralaya ). The time- 
span of a world-cycle is divided and sub-divided according to different scales as 


10 A study of time 



146 


A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


equivalent to so many manvantaras , mahayugas , yugas etc. The whole process is 
calculated in terms of billions of human years. It is very likely that the idea is 
associated with Vedic astronomy. The world-cycles can be compared to one 
another in terms of generic similarity just as one day resembles another, but the 
idea of exact repetition involving the return of the particulars does not occur. 
The idea of world cycles occurs in the epics as well as in the Upanisads. 

The idea of transmigration is equally a general feature of the conceptual frame¬ 
work in India,. It is, however, to be understood as a necessarry sequel of the 
doctrine of Karma. These ideas, in different versions, are current in the Brahma- 
nical, Buddhist and Jaina traditional thinking. To think of transmigration as 
equivalent to mechanical recurrence, which debars the possibility of moral pro¬ 
gress, is a distortion of the idea. The doctrine of Karma implies the inexorability 
of the moral law, formulating the age-old insight of ‘as you sow, so you reap* in 
its utmost consequence. The idea focuses, on the one hand, on that man creates 
his destiny through action. The present actions determine the future. On the 
other hand, the idea entails that one cannot escape the consequences of the 
actions, once performed. 

“It is wrong to identify the doctrine of Karma with fatalism. Karma is not an 
external destiny driving man to his doom, nor a blind mechanical framework 
from which there is no escape”. 12 The idea of Karma refers back eventually to the 
idea of transmigration - metempsychosis, viewing the human existence as going 
beyond birth and death. This idea, shared by the Jaina and the Buddhist along 
with the Brahmanical tradition can be seen, in the long run, as an attempt to 
answer the problem of evil. To pronounce samsdra y the chain of existences, as 
beginningless is strongly motivated by the urge for an understanding of the 
problem of evil - a problem which no theological structure can evade. This has 
been found to be incompatible with the idea of absolute beginning, a conceptual 
possibility which has been also considered. If the problem of an ultimate basis 
for individual differences, in all its different aspects, is left to the play of sheer 
chance, the idea that action determines and conditions the human situation/des¬ 
tiny cannot be established in all strictness. Moreover, in the theistic frame¬ 
works, 13 to make Gcfcl solely responsible for these undeniable differences would 
be tantamount to attributing to Him injustice, which is inappropriate to the 
divine nature. Thus, the complex set of ideas, that goes along with the doctrine 
of Karma, has as one of its necessary implications the idea of pre-existence. 


12 Mahadevan, in “The religio-philosophic culture of India ", [4] vol I p. 163-181 (p. 179). 

13 It is so pointed out by different Brahmanical schools. See Nyaya Sutra 4/1/19 and Sankara’s 
commentary on Brahma Sutra 2/1/34-35. 



A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


147 


Transmigration is, however, not understood by any of these traditions as 
equivalent to a mechanical recurrence of the same individual destiny, as the 
Greek model of the idea which we have considered. The idea of Karma itself 
would be a rebuttal to such an idea'of exact repetition, as the idea of actions 
bringing forth consequences cannot be relevant in that scheme. All the stories of 
Jataka , for example, mythologically express the importance of action, seen in the 
perspective of many lives, in the making of a Buddha. 

Moreover, these ideas, involving transmigration, Karma, etc., often symboli¬ 
sed as the ‘wheel of becoming* is viewed theologically as having salvation for its 
goal (moksayate samsarah). All the traditions have sought to conceive ‘salva¬ 
tion*, each in its own conceptual scheme, as a final freedom from the ‘ocean of 
death-bound existence*. 14 This idea of salvation must not be overlooked in an 
appraisal of Karma, transmigration. Even the idea of world-cycle can be under¬ 
stood in a theistic context in the following manner, as Radhakrishnan exposes it: 

“The universe once created persists through an entire kalpa , or world-period, 
after which it returns into the supreme God, only to issue again from Him. The 
cause of this re-issue is that the works of the soul still survive and demand a fresh 
creation or a renewed existence for their expiation”. ([8] Vol. I p. 514). 

It is to be noted that the wheel of becoming, in the Indian context, does not 
involve a mechanical repetition of the particular/the individual, neither does it 
preclude salvation, nor by any means imply that a released soul can be pulled 
back to this death-bound existence again. 

It is interesting to note the following remark of Sankara, as he criticises the 
Vaisesika categories. Although taken from quite a different context of conceptual 
analysis, still his retort has relevance for our present discussion. 

“If we once allow assumptions, there is no reason why there should not be 
assumed a further hundred or thousand things, in addition to the six categories 
assumed by the Vaisesikas. Anybody then might assume anything, and we could 
neither stop a compasionate man from assuming that this transmigratory world 
which is the cause of so much misery to living beings is not to be, nor a malicious 
man from assuming that even the released souls are to enter on a new cycle of 
existences”. 15 

It is needless to say that in order to do justice to all these complex sets of ideas it 
requires detailed analysis of the subject in question in the framework of the 


14 Bhagavadgita XII, 7. 

15 Vedanta-Sutra , 1,2,17 by Sankara [40] p. 398. 



148 


A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


various schools of thought belonging to the different philosophical traditions. In 
matters of such profound speculations there is room for several versions, as the 
ideas are worked out in each system. Thus, for example, the idea of repeated 
creation and dissolution is accepted by all Brahmanical schools except Purva- 
Mimamsa. Again, the conception of a final dissolution ( maha-pralaya ) is to be 
found in the school of Advaita Vedanta, and it can be understood only in the 
light of the conception of universal salvation ( sarvamukti ). 16 

These ideas, as for example in the Brahmanical traditions those of transmigra¬ 
tion, Karma, world-cycle, salvation, are all built into an inter-connected system 
of ideas which have bearing on each other. They are interrelated and meet at a 
theological frontier. 

Let us note the different ideas in the texts of the Bbagavadgita [2]. There is a 
direct allusion to the idea of periodic emergence and dissolution of the world, 
using the metaphor of day and night: 

“At the coming of day, all manifested things come forth from the unmanifested 
and at the coming of night they merge in the same, called the unmanifested. M 
Here the unmanifested is Prakrti’. (VIII, 18) 

Note the idea of the Transcendent, 

“But beyond this unmanifested, 17 there is yet another Unmanifested Eternal 
Being who does not perish even when all existences perish.” (VIII, 20) 

Again, 

“Veiled by My creative power (yogamaya) I am not revealed to all. This be¬ 
wildered world knows Me not, the unborn, the unchanging.” (VII, 25) 

Note the idea of transmigration, 

“As the soul passes in this body through childhood, youth and age, even so is its 
taking on of another body. The sage is not perplexed by this.” (II, 13) 

And the idea of salvation, 

“From the realm of Brahma downwards, all worlds are subject to return to 
rebirth, but on reaching Me, O Son of Kunti (Arjuna), there is no return to birth 
again.” (VIII, 16) 

It is not possible, however, to sort out relevant passages from each tradition and 
to develop them in a coherent, fullfledged form here. Even when the traditions 
operate with similar ideas, a wide variety of views develop as attempts are made 

16 cf. Warner [50]. 

17 Meaning Prakrti. 



A note on the cyclic and the linear notions of time 


149 


at systematic philosophical formulations with all the concequences and implica¬ 
tions that follow within the conceptual framework of the different systems. 

The basic reason for bringing up this discussion is to point out the importance 
of understanding rightly the appellation of the so-called ‘cyclic or circular time’, 
if the term is not to be a misnomer. A sculptural image of ‘the wheel of beco¬ 
ming’ has to be interpreted in the light of the tradition where it is used signifi¬ 
cantly. 

There is reason to be cautious when we use the same label of ‘circular time’ for 
characterising the Greek as well as the Indian philosophical models of specula¬ 
tions. The presence or absence of certain ideas ought not to be the basis for any 
rash appraisal of traditions. We have noted already that the Greek and the Indian 
conceptual models for cosmological speculations (whatever their ultimate onto¬ 
logical status) are developed in strict adherence to the principle of ex nibilo nihil 
fit . Again, the presence of such ideas as world-cycles, transmigration can be 
found, in different versions, in these Greco-Indian fields of speculations. But the 
important differences of the Indian ideas from the Greek model - that Augustine 
has in front of him - must by no means be lost sight of. The Greek conceptual 
model of ‘circular time’ is completely deterministic, fatalistic. Despite all the 
internal differences within the boundary of philosophical speculations in India, 
that Greek model of ‘circular time’ is nowhere to be found. The ideas of the 
transcendent and salvation pulsate throughout these traditions. The materialistic 
school of Carvaka, of course, does not operate within this framework at all. 

The relevance of this discussion, which as such would be outside the scope of 
this work, is to draw attention to this common appellation of ‘circular time’ - 
apparently a designation which stands for a complex set of ideas - to the Greek 
as well as to the Indian world-view. It is precisely the similarities of certain ideas 
which has been a basis for misunderstanding. Not to speak of much baseless 
polemics, even western authors, who have made important contributions to 
intercultural understanding, have this Greek conceptual model in mind while 
evaluating and confronting the complex world of Indian philosophy. 18 


18 Toynbee, in his A Study of History characterizes the cyclic image of Hellenic and Indie civilisa¬

This points to the importance of understanding the significance of myths and 
symbols of a culture. A proper understanding and evaluation of symbols, verbal 
or pictorial (such as bhava-cakra y the wheel of becoming), presupposes an inti¬ 
mate knowledge of the philosophical background from where it stems. The want 
of a conceptual apparatus to decipher and appraise symbols and myths coming 
from unfamiliar cultural set-ups leads to misinterpretations. These, in turn, 
cause barriers for intercultural understanding, block the possibility of a fruitful 
encounter. 

The theological motives operating behind the ‘linear model* can be seen in the 
writings of Augustine. The cycle represented mechanical recurrence, complete 
determinism, no possibility of a final salvation. The ideas of an absolute begin¬ 
ning - as creation ex nibilo - and an absolute end - eschatology - provide the 
theological framework, along with the specific understanding of the saviour, in 
which the significance of linear time is to be understood. 

Taken merely as an empirical concept, outside of this theological frame, the 
idea of time, geometrically represented by a finite line, i.e. itself having a begin¬ 
ning and an end, would lose its conceptual worth. We have to be aware of the 
pitfalls of such geometrical representations of time as a circle or a line. These 
have to be understood as theological symbolisms, whose intended spiritual signi¬ 
ficance can be grasped within their theological, conceptual structures. 

It is indeed fascinating to go through the records of how the human mind has 
waved to and fro in its considerations of the alternate positions regarding whe¬ 
ther the world can be said to have a beginning and an end, or is it beginningless 
but has /has no end or has a beginning but no end. We find illuminating discus¬ 
sions on the subject by Aristotle in his On the Heavens [81] Bk. I Ch. X-XII 
(pp. 94-129). Again, as Augustine breaks away from the traditional Greek thin¬ 
king, he reflects on the matter at length in his City of God [88] Bk. XII Ch. X, 
XVI. In addition to these contributions from the Greek and the Christian world 
of ideas we have the immense mythological and philosophical insights and con¬ 
cepts, all contributing to the spectrum of speculations, from the rich variety of 
the Indian traditions.
