Cf. also Zimmer [9] p. 18-20. His interpretation of the myth of the Purdna is illuminating. He 
captures the moral significance appropriately, yet his understanding is dominated by the Greek 
model of circular time to which he alludes. 



(iii) Some parallel ideas in the investigation on time in Western 
philosophy 


Investigations into the problem of time display across cultural boundaries some 
similar trends in terms of the questions, that arise in the course of analysis, as 
well as in the formulations of the actual positions. History of ideas provides 
many interesting parallels despite the differences in conceptual structures of 
given systems of thought. 

A spectrum of divergent views pertaining to the problem of time is recorded in 
the history of Western thought. However, in this section reference will be made 
only to some analyses and views contributed by Western thinkers, which are also 
of special interest for the present study regarding the problem of time in Indian 
philosophy. 

We have noted the principal views regarding time in Indian philosophy and 
their bearing on the divergent analysis and interpretation of other major philo¬ 
sophical concepts, which are indispensable for the various specific structures. 

Here, we shall first refer to the classification of the existents and a specific 
aspect of the problem of non-existence found in the Nyaya-Vaisesika schools in 
order to focus on their analysis in the perspective of Aristotelian discussion on 
the problem of time. 

The Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophical interpretation of time is evidently closely 
tied to their general conceptual scheme. In their metaphysical pluralistic structu¬ 
re, the universe of existents (bhava-padartha) is classified as nitya and anitya. 
Time is itself listed in the first category, as one amongst the entities conceived as 
unproduced and indestructible. Here, it is of significance to emphasise that the 
other entities, which belong to the same category as time, do not exist in time. It 
is the existents of the second category - liable to production and destruction - 
which are precisely those which have being-in-time. In other words, the contin¬ 
gent is necessarily in contact with time, a real per se. Absolute time is considered 
the receptacle (adhara) of all that is contingent (janya ). 

It is to be noted that the above classification is made on the basis of how the 
existents are related to time. To exist is not necessarily to be in time. No begin¬ 
ning or end can be attributed to the entities classified as nitya. They are outside 
the influence of time (kdlaprabhavamukta), as time is not their receptacle. It is 
explicitly brought out that these entities co-exist, but cannot be conceived as 
receptacle in relation to one another. 

On the other hand, production, destruction, change, etc., pertaining to the 
contingent, all bear the stamp of time, i.e. they are possible and conceivable only 
in relation to time. They are literally said to be contained (adheya) in time. 



152 


Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 


On the background of this analysis it is illuminating to read the following 
comments from Aristotle's Physics : 

“And it is further evident that to be in existence while time is in existence does 
not constitute being ‘in time', just as neither is a thing constituted as in motion or 
in a place because a motion and place exist while it does. For if this constitutes 
being ‘in' a thing, everything would be in any thing, and the universe in a grain 
of millet, because a grain of millet exists while the universe is in existence. But 
this latter is an incidental coincidence, whereas when a thing is said to exist in 
time, it follows of necessity that there should be time while this thing exists, and 
if it exists in motion, it follows of necessity that there should be motion while it 
exists. 

... so we must add that for things to exist in time they must be embraced by 
time, just as with other cases of being ‘in' something; for instance, things that are 
in places are embraced by place. And it will follow that they are in some respect 
affected by time, just as we are wont to say that time crumbles things, and that 
everything grows old under the power of time and is forgotten through the lapse 
of time ... 

From all this it is clear that things which exist eternally, as such, are not in 
time; for they are not embraced by time, nor is their duration measured by time. 
This is indicated by their not suffering anything under the action of time as 
though they were within its scope” [79] Bk. IV, Ch. XII, 221 a - 221 b (p. 403, 
405). 

The idea of non-existence (abhdva) is equally significant for an appraisal of the 
different views of time. The problem has been much debated by the Indian 
philosophers, as to whether it is an objective category or not, whether it is 
apprehended by perception, inference, or non-perception (< anupalahdhi ), (the 
latter is accepted as a source of knowledge by such schools as Bhatta-Mimamsa 
and Vedanta who emphasised the direct, i.e. non-inferential character of our 
apprehension of non-existence, but maintained that no sense-contact is possible 
with the non-existent). 

This is not the place to go into the details of such discussions, but it requires to 
be stressed that the views regarding the problem are not without implications on 
time. In section V (iv), ‘Annihilation and time - a Nyaya-Buddhist controversy', 
we have studied an aspect of the problem of non-existence and its significance for 
the views regarding time. Here it needs to be emphasised once again, that the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers took a bold stand and attempted a thorough ana¬ 
lysis pertaining to definition, classification, as well as to the epistemological 
questions regarding the problem of non-existence. The Nyaya-Vaisesika classifi¬ 
cation of non-existence into four kinds is highly interesting for its bearing on the 
problem of time. 



Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 


The non-existence that precedes the production of an entity is designators 
pragabhdva or the antecedent non-existence. The production of an entity js the 
termination of its antecedent non-existence. The idea is especially useful for the 
Nyaya-Vaisesika theory of causality, where the effect is conceived as noprexi¬ 
stent prior to the causal operation, which demarcates the theory from that, of 
Sarikhya where the effect is said to be pre-existent potentially in the cause. The 
antecedent non-existence thus cannot be said to have a beginning, but has an end 
(anddih santah). It is indicative of time as past when the contingent entity was 
not. 

Again, the non-existence following the destruction of an entity is termed 
pradhvamsdbhdva or subsequent non-existence. It is conceived as having a be¬ 
ginning but no end, e.g. the non-existence of a jar posterior to its destruction. 
This non-existence can never cease as the particular jar in question cannot come 
into existence any more. If the antedecent non-existence points to time as past, 
consequent non-existence indicates future non-existence of the present contin¬ 
gent entity. 

The temporal dimension of these two kinds of non-existence is evident. With¬ 
out time as their locus the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers could not conceive of 
them as objective categories of reality On the other hand, a clear-cut formula¬ 
tion of the idea of contingency ( kdddcitkatva) regarding a positive entity ( bhdva - 
paddrtha) could not be made possible without the specific ideas of time (kdla) 
and non-existence ( abbava ). The production of a positive entity is the termina¬ 
tion of its antecedent non-existence implying its first conjunction with time. 

The other two kinds of non-existence are, however, of absolute character. This 
is brought out by pointing to their beginningless and endless nature. When a 
connection is denied in all three times, past, present, and future, - e.g. air has no 
colour, we have a case of what is termed atyantabhdva or absolute non-existen¬ 
ce. This kind of non-existence, as opposed to the two kinds previously mentio¬ 
ned, cannot be said to originate or cease in time. This holds equally true of the 
non-existence termed anyonyabhava which covers such cases of logical negation 
of formal character as S is not P. 19 Thus, in these systems the classification of 
non-existence into contingent and absolute bears necessary reference to time. 

In this connection, it is interesting to quote once more a passage from Aristot¬ 
le’s analysis on time in order to note his reflections pertaining to this problem: 

“Clearly, then not all non-existences are in time, but only such as might exist; 
for instance, the commensurability of the diagonal and the side does not exist, 
but its non-existence is not temporal. For, as a general proposition, if time is the 
measure of motion on its own account and of anything else only by incidental 


19 See The Nydya theory of Knowledge by S. Chatterjee [31] 



154 


Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 


coincidence, obviously everything whose existence it measures must have its 
existence in rest or in motion. Accordingly, whatever is destructible or generab- 
le, or (more broadly) sometimes existing and sometimes not, must be embraced 
by time; for there must be some time great enough to exceed the time of their 
duration and therefore the time which measures their being. Among non-exi- 
stents, on the other hand, those which are embraced by time either once were (as 
Homer once existed) or will be (e.g. some future event), according as they are 
embraced by time on one or the other side of the present moment, or, if they are 
embraced in both directions, they can be either past or future; whereas those 
which are not in any way embraced by time neither were nor are nor will be. 
Non-existents of this latter kind are all those things whose opposites eternally 
exist; for instance, the incommensurability of the diagonal eternally exists and 
therefore is not in time. And it follows that neither is its commensurability in 
time; hence it is eternally non-existent, in as much as it is a contradiction of what 
is eternally existent; whereas things of which the opposite does not exist eternal¬ 
ly may either be or not be, and so they can come into existence and vanish from 
it" [79] Bk. IV, Ch. XII, 221 b - 222 a (p. 405, 407). 

It is significant to put in relief that amongst the wide-ranging philosophical 
stands regarding time in Indian philosophy, it is only the Nyaya-Vaisesika school 
that ascribes to time as well as space an independent, absolute, and self-existent 
status. They are conceived as receptacles and are themselves not contained in 
anything else. The conventional time-divisions and space-divisions are explained 
as not inherent in the absolute time and space, but as measures with reference to 
motion and activities and as due to relation to finite bodies, respectively. 

It is interesting to observe - as we shall see shortly - that these ideas about 
space and time are not in the least unfamiliar in western thought, although the 
motivations and considerations in support of such a view might be quite diffe¬ 
rent. 

The Aristotelian interpretation of time continued to exert a dominating influ¬ 
ence until the advent of the Newtonian conception. Newton’s ideas on the 
subject were influenced by his predecessor Galileo’s findings on motion. In his 
Principia , published in 1687, Newton expressed his ideas about space and time. 
We shall quote only briefly in order to indicate some similarities in the general 
pattern with the Nyaya-Vaisesika view. 

“Absolute, true, and mathematical time, of itself, and from its own nature, flows 
equably without regard to anything external, and by another name is called 
duration: relative, apparent, and common time is some sensible and external 
measure of duration by means of motion, which is commonly used instead of 
true time; such as an hour, a day, a month, or a year. 



Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 


155 


“Absolute space, in its own nature, without regard to anything external, re¬ 
mains always similar and immovable. Relative space is some movable dimension 
or measure of the absolute space which our senses determine by its position to 
bodies ...” 

Again, "... times and spaces are, as it were, the places as well of themselves as 
of all other things. All things are placed in time as to order of succession; and in 
space as to order of situation” (as quoted by whitrow [99] Ch 3 p. 56). 

Note, however, that whereas in the Newtonian formulation absolute time 
‘flows’, the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers, on the contrary, conceived it as static 
( niskriya ). 

The influence of Newton’s ideas are far-reaching in the history of Western 
thought. It is evident on John Locke’s philosophical reflections on time. In his 
Essay Concerning Human Understanding (1690) [95] Locke discusses elaborate¬ 
ly, amongst various other problems, the problem of motion and time. The idea 
of linear representation is also found. The following lines contain some of the 
important features of his ideas. 

"... duration is but as it were the length of one straight line, extended in 
"infinitum, not capable of multiplicity, variation, or figure, but is one common 
measure of all existence whatsoever, wherein all things, whilst they exist, equally 
partake. For this present moment is common to all things that are now in being, 
and equally comprehends that part of their existence, as much as if they were all 
but one single being; and we may truly say, they all exist in the same moment of 
time.” 20 

However, the idea of an objective time is no permanent feature of philosophi¬ 
cal speculations in India or in the West as the history of ideas bears witness to. 

The idea is challenged in the history of Indian thought. Time as an all-perva¬ 
ding real per se as the Nyaya-Vaisesika philosophers would have it, or the idea of 
a single objective moment when the whole universe undergoes change, as Yoga 
conceives it, are rejected by the school of Advaita Vedanta. Not only the idea of 
time as an independent real per se but also the idea of an objective principle of 
change, conceived in whatever manner, are given up in this system. Time and 
change are not granted ontological reality, neither are they held to be fictitious or 
nothing. This is precisely their indeterminable character (anirvacaniya). How¬ 
ever, their empirical reality is never questioned, neither their a priori character. It 
is to be noted that the characterisation of the world-process as beginningless is 
present as much in Advaita Vedanta as in any other school of Indian philosophy. 


20 Essay Concerning Human Understanding by J. Locke [95]. Bk. II Ch. XV §11. 



156 Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 

But the emphasis is shifted, in so far as the principle of becoming is denied any 
absolute status and is declared as phenomenal ( vyavaharika-sat ). 

The rejection of an absolute time as objectively given is a philosophical event 
that can be traced in the history of philosophy in many versions. To take an 
example from western philosophy, one may refer to a highly important analysis 
of the problem of time, viz. that of Kant. In his famous Critique of Pure Reason 
[94], Kant focuses on the idea of time as a ‘form’ of inner sense, that is, of the 
intuition of ourselves and of our inner state. He expressly states that “time is not 
something which exists of itself, or which inheres in things as an objective 
determination ...” Kant’s interpretation of time discloses a novel conceptual 
model. He summarises his position thus in [94], p. 78: 

“What we are maintaining is, therefore, the empirical reality of time, that is, its 
objective validity in respect of all objects which allow of ever being given to our 
senses. ... On the other hand, we deny to time all claim to absolute reality; that 
is to say, we deny that it belongs to things absolutely, as their condition or 
property, independently of any reference to the form of our sensible intuition; 
properties that belong to things in themselves can never be given to us through 
the senses. This, then, is what constitutes the transcendental ideality of time.” 

Kant’s understanding of time has been open to much philosophical debate. 
Criticisms against the position are advanced by philosophers and scientists alike. 

Laying aside other important ideas 21 relevant for a philosophical interpretation 
of time, that can he found in the history of Western philosophy, let us focus on 
the phenomenological movement of the twentieth century. 

In the reflections of Husserl, the founder of phenomenology, the problem of 
time comes into the forefront of philosophical investigations and receives a novel 
treatment. In Husserl’s analysis we find a treatment of the problem of time on 
three levels - the objective time in which the transcendant objects have their 
position, the immanent time in which the immanent acts are constituted and the 
absolute flux of consciousness, which is the source of all temporality but is itself 
not in time. 

Husserl’s lectures on internal time-consciousness [93] are mainly concerned 
with the second, viz. the problem of the constitution of immanent acts as tempo¬ 
ral unities. Each act has a temporal stretch or extension, it is analysable into a 
series of ‘nows’, each ‘now’ having its own retentional and protentional horizon 
and sinking back into the past along with its horizon. In Husserl’s analysis of 
time-consciousness the primitive element is no doubt the now, but the now is 
not an isolated discrete, atomic moment, but is intentional in its structure in the 


21 Such as the well-known analysis of Bergson [89]. 



Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 157 

sense that it contains within itself references to the past and the future. This; is 
what he means by its retentional and protentional horizon. This peculiar charac¬ 
ter of the now explains how an act-continuum is possible. If each momentary 
datum were completely disconnected with the other, the discrete nows would 
never form a whole, there would always be nothing but a perishing moment. His 
analysis is a significant contribution towards the problem of time and conscious¬ 
ness. 

Husserl’s ideas exerted an important influence on the existentialists, such as 
Heidegger [92] and Sartre [98], The problem of time, as it stands in their respec¬ 
tive philosophical treatment, is closely connected with human existence and 
consciousness. The idea of an objective or physical time appears in their analysis 
as derived and constructed. Thus Sartre maintains that the ecstatic character of 
consciousness is the origin of all temporality. Temporality is only “the mode of 
being of a Being which is itself outside of itself”. 22 

We shall not go into details of the phenomenological and existentialistic analy¬ 
sis of time. However, it is interesting to note with reference to Sartrean existenti¬ 
alism and the Vijnanavada school of Buddhism 23 the question as to whether self- 
consciousness involves the idea of a subsequent moment or not - a problem 
already referred to in section VII (i), ‘time and conscipusness’. 

The problem is of acute interest to both these schools, who agree in maintai¬ 
ning that consciousness and its awareness of itself does not involve a subsequent 
act, where a temporal gap can be conceived between consciousness of something 
and consciousness of consciousness. The philosophical stand, as worked out in 
their specific conceptual structures, discloses a striking similarity in their respec¬ 
tive argumentations. 

In his principal work Being and Nothingness Sartre discusses this problem 
with much ingenuity. For Sartre, intentionality is the defining character of con¬ 
sciousness, that is to say, all consciousness is consciousness of something. The 
other very important implication of the notion of intentionality, which he makes 
explicit, is that “the necessary and sufficient condition for a knowing conscious¬ 
ness to be knowledge of its object, is that it be conscious of itself as being that 
knowledge” [98]. In other words, Sartre maintains that knowledge would be 
impossible, if consciousness were not aware of itself. It is not possible for me to 
be conscious of the table if “my consciousness were not conscious of being 
conscious of the table” [98]. Thus, for Sartre, “every positional consciousness of 
an object is at the same time a non-positional consciousness of itself”. 24 This is 

22 Being and Nothingness by Sartre [98] p. 136. 

23 cf. A.N. Balslev [100]. 

24 Being and Nothingness [98]. (Italics mine) 


11 A study of time 



158 


Some parallel ideas on time in Western philosophy 


what Sartre expresses by saying that consciousness of something is consciousness 
(of) consciousness. 

It is very important to note that the subject-object dualism, which is so typical 
of the knowledge situation, is said not to hold good in the case of consciousness 
knowing itself. If that were the case, Sartre argues, it would mean that another 
consciousness is required to make consciousness known to itself, and thus one 
cannot avoid the fallacy of infinite regress. Thus, he concludes, all reflection 
presupposes this non-dual awareness on the part of consciousness. 

On the other hand, the Vijnanavadins maintain vehemently that conscious¬ 
ness" awareness of itself is not a judgmental knowledge. Consciousness does not 
know itself as an external object, as consciousness is not an “other” to itself. This 
is precisely what is expressed in the statement “that which reveals itself, being 
free from the subject-object dualism” (grdhya-grdhaka vaidhurydt svayam sa iva 
prakdsyate). 25 Consciousness of consciousness does not involve any duality. It is 
not a relation between two terms. It is not a discursive knowledge. It is said that 
as light reveals and is self-revealing, so is consciousness. The cognised object and 
the cognition are invariably apprehended together (sahopalambba - co-appre- 
hension). Thus for consciousness to be aware of its object is to be conscious of 
itself. “Consciousness” and “self-consciousness”, therefore, are interchangable 
terms. 26 . 

This is the very presupposition of all knowledge. It is only by means of this 
idea of svasamvedana (self-awareness), the immediate, non-cognitive awareness, 
that one can avoid the fallacy of infinite regress ( anavasthd ), as would be inevi¬ 
table if consciousness required another consciousness to make itself known to 
itself. This is how the Vijnanavadins argue that if consciousness is not self- 
revealing (svayamprakasa), knowledge is not possible. It is claimed that all cog¬ 
nitions, feelings are known b^ themselves (sarvam cittacaittdndm atmasamveda - 
nam) [61]. 

Thus, the brief study made above with an inter-cultural perspective points out 
that the conclusion, that no temporal sequence-can be legitimately conceived 
between consciousness of something and consciousness of consciousness, is arri¬ 
ved at by the similar philosophical argument - that the contrary idea would 
inevitably lead to the fallacy of infinite regress, implying the impossibility of all 
knowledge. 

Now, to return to the analysis of time as is found in the phenomenological and 
existentialistic investigations, it can be noted that the source of time-experience 
is traced to consciousness or human existence. 

25 Yogdcdra Idealism by A. Chatterjee [59]. 

26 The Buddhist Philosophy of Universal Flux by S. Mookherji [61]. 



The timeless and the temporal - paradox and predicament 


159 


The philosophical tension between the above understanding of time and that 
of the positivists who assume the objective time of nature as independent of 
consciousness leads to further investigations. 

In his recent work 27 K.E. Logstrup, after considering the stands mentioned 
above, refuses to regard them as alternatives. The key point of his analysis is the 
idea that time is structured by consciousness or human existence, whereas the 
source of its origination is in annihilation and its irreversibility - which is said to 
be independent of consciousness. 

It can be observed that a persistent problem, that appears in the forefront of a 
philosophical analysis of time, consists in accounting for the time-phases which 
form the universal and essential feature of time-experience. The actual, efficient 
character of the present as opposed to the absent character of the past and the 
future time-phases calls for an explanation. The wide variety of views that emer¬ 
ge in the philosophical traditions of India and the West regarding the problem of 
time give different accounts of this pertinent question in harmony with their 
respective conceptual models. 

In the light of the philosophical dispute as to the subjective or objective nature 
of the source to which our time-experience can be traced, it is interesting to 
note 28 that the idea that the time-sequence is a subjective construction, whereas 
the objective principle of change - supporting the idea of the reality of the 
present moment - is independent of consciousness, finds expression in the Yoga 
school of Indian philosophy. 


(iv) The timeless and the temporal - paradox and predicament 


The theological framework of thinking discloses a challenge that involves the 
conceptions of the timeless and the temporal. The theological understanding of 
being as timeless, whether conceived as personal or not - as found in a theistic 
and an absolutistic structure respectively - is prevalent in the theological tradi¬ 
tions across cultures. Equally widespread is the idea of the contingency of the 
temporal on the timeless. This involves a set of ideas which need to be brought 
into focus, as it provides an important area of study with inter-cultural perspec¬ 
tive. 


27 SkabeUe og Tilintetgerelse [96]. 

28 See section II (ii). 



160 


The timeless and the temporal - paradox and predicament 


The notion of the temporal as effect, i.e. as totally dependent on the timeless, 
is a theological notion which gives rise to alternative conceptual models. These 
could be fruitfully studied with reference to diverse theological traditions, which 
would contribute towards & philosophical appraisal as a necessary basis for an 
encounter of religions. 

The idea of cause acquires a special significance with reference to being as 
timeless. The conventional understanding of cause as temporally preceding the 
effect cannot be entertained in a theological context. The conception of the 
timeless as cause does not involve any idea of temporal sequence, i.e. it does not 
precede the effect by time. Some theologians have preferred the coinage of the 
term ‘ground* (adhisthana, as used in Advaita Vedanta) precisely to convey this 
special sense, in which cause is to be understood with reference to being as 
timeless. 

The timeless as the indispensable ground for the temporal is evidently a para¬ 
dox. On the other hand, however paradoxical, it is a theological necessity. We 
find the philosohico-religious literature across cultural boundaries developing 
this theme within its own conceptual scheme. 

The problem that invariably poses itself is the necessity of a conceptual device 
which could adequately indicate the total dependence of the temporal on the 
timeless. The former is caused, the latter is uncaused, sui generis. 

Let us now note, through an analysis of such examples drawn from different 
traditions, the conceptual pattern that runs through the different theological 
structures. The theological demand, that the temporal is contingent upon the 
timeless, has been expressed diversely in different traditions. 

A philosophical appraisal of such key expressions as ‘the world has a begin¬ 
ning’, and ‘the world is an appearance’ - examples stemming from the Christian 
and the Vedantic theological formulations - is valuable in this connection. It is to 
be noted that the two terms ‘beginning’ and ‘appearance’, although derived from 
conventional language, obtain technical significance in the respective theological 
frameworks. These terms perform a symbolic function, i.e. aim at expressing the 
theological insight which is a paradox to discursive thinking. 

The Christian expression, that the world has a beginning, has a natural refe¬ 
rence to the problem of time. The following lines enables us to understand it 
better. 

- “Since the beginning is an existence which is preceded by a time in which the 
thing is not, there must have been a preceding time in which the world was not, 
i.e. an empty time”. These lines quoted from the first antinomy formulated by 
Kant in his Critique of Pure Reason ([94] p. 397) are illuminating in a twofold 
way. Firstly, it indicates the meaning of the conception of ‘beginning’ and what it 
entails with reference to time in a philosophical analysis. But, again, it also helps 



The timeless and the temporal - paradox and predicament 


161 


at once to clearly distinguish the theological meaning of ‘beginning’ as can be 
found in the profound reflections of Saint Augustine in The Confessions . He 
comments on the text of Genesis: “In the Beginning Thou madest the heaven and 
earth”. He writes, “at no time then hadst Thou not made any thing, because time 
itself Thou madest” [87] Bk. XI Ch. XIV (p. 262). 

The theological significance lies in grasping that there is no question of any 
preceding empty time with reference to which ‘beginning’ is to be understood - 
“... before heaven and earth there was no time ...” [87] Bk. XI Ch. XIII. 

The word ‘beginning’ thus can be said to acquire a technical significance of 
precise theological character. In other words, ‘the world has a beginning in time’ 
is the philosophical position which, when subjected to analysis, points to the 
conflict of transcendental ideas as an antinomy of pure reason. 

The Augustinian understanding and interpretation discloses the position - that 
the world has a beginning - as a theological symbolism. The creation of the 
world is no empirical information - it is revelation. 

‘The world is an appearance’ - despite the large spread of Vedantic thought - is 
still an expression liable to misinterpretation. In the Advaita Vedanta framework 
of thinking, to account for the total dependence of the world on Brahman, the 
Being par excellence , conceptually specified as uncancelled in all three times, i.e. 
timeless, is precisely the function of the doctrine of Maya. The temporal and the 
timeless do not have the same ontological status. 

In order to evaluate the Advaita expression of the world as appearance one has 
to take into account the Advaita theological terminology. Let us point out only 
briefly that ‘the world is an appearance’ is not an empirical judgement on the 
world. It is not an empirical illusion, it is transcendental, pointing to the reality 
of being as timeless, as self-existent. It is to be noted that Sankara is not pro¬ 
pounding a subjective idealism through the pronouncement of the world as 
appearance. He has vehemently criticised the subjective idealism of the Vijnana- 
vada school of Buddhism in his commentary on the Brabma-Sutra 2/2/28-29. 
The conception of the transcendental appearance of the world has, in his 
thought, a foundation on epistemological realism. It is not questioning the empi¬ 
rical reality of the world. ‘The world is an appearance’ is a theological symbo¬ 
lism, the function is revelatory. 

In the Advaita-Siddhi [43] appearance is characterised as that which is neither 
being (sat) nor non-being ( asat ). It is, therefore, termed also as ‘indeterminable’ 
(anirvacaniya). Appearance is precisely that which cannot be characterised as 
real per se, neither can it be said to be nothing. This is the status of the temporal 
as appearance in the Advaita structure of thinking. The value of the term has to 
be fully grasped in order to comprehend the Advaita appraisal of the status of the 
world. 



162 


The timeless and the temporal - pa/adox and predicament 


In this connection, let us read the following lines by Saint Augustine, 

“And I beheld the other things below Thee, and I perceived that they neither 
altoghether are, nor altogether are not, for they are, since they are from Thee, 
but are not, because they are not, what Thou art. For that truly is, which 
remains unchangeably” [87] Bk. VII Ch. XI. p. 134. 

The theological structure of Advaita Vedanta operates with the three key ideas 
- paramartha , vyavahara and pratibhasa. Paramartha is being which is variously 
characterised as unsublatable ( abadhya ), as unchanging and unchangable {ku- 
tastha-nitya ), it is reality per se. Vyavahara is the empirical, the realm of change 
and multiplicity. It is that which is transitory ( anitya ). The notion is carefully 
distinguished from paramartha as well as pratibhasa. Pratibhasa is the pure 
nothing, the absolute naught ( tucca ). The world is an empirical reality (vyavaha- 
ra sat). It is not imaginary (, allka ), not nothing (tucca). On the other hand, it has 
only epistemic status and no ontological reality. 

These notions are carefully worked out in all details with all their implications. 
The existing literature on the subject is enormous in the form of commentaries 
and subcommentaries and so on and so forth. The theological appraisal of the 
world as appearance is precisely to focus on the idea of the reality of being as 
unchanging and unchangeable. Appearance is that which calls for a ground 
(adhisthana). Brahman, the real per se, is the ground of the world-appearance. 
The problem of time is a problem of appearance in Advaita Vedanta. This is why 
the Vaisesika conception of time as a real per se was vehemently rejected by the 
exponents of the system. Being as timeless is the real which knows of no duality 
(i ekamevadvitlyam ), no transformation ( aparinami ), uncancelled by three times 
(trikalabadhya). It can be only indicated negatively (as neti neti ). 

Again, it is interesting to read the following lines of Saint Augustine from De 
mor. Manich. Ch I, 

“For that is chiefly to be said to Be, which always exists in one and the same 
way; which is every way like itself; which can in no way be injured or changed; 
which is not subject to time, which cannot at one time be other than at another. 
For this is what is most truly said to be”, (quoted in [87] in footnote to Bk. VII 
Ch. XI p. 134). 

To round up this brief discussion, let us note that the significance of the world as 
created can be grasped only theologically. That the world has a beginning is not 
an empirical information about the world. 

On the other hand, the significance of the world as an appearance comes to 
light within the theological frame of reference which is its own. It is not intended 
to question the empirical reality of the world, but intended as a challenge to any 
claim of the world as self-existent. 



The timeless and the temporal - paradox and predicament 


163 


These themes could be developed at length with all their consequences and 
implications as a conceptual platform for a theology of encounter of religions. 
This is, however, not within the scope of this work. But it is to be noticed that 
the theological purpose of the doctrine of Maya and the doctrine of creation as 
an appraisal of time and the timeless is a matter of profound and careful study. It 
is this alone which could enable us to extract the conceptual nucleus of theologi¬ 
cal structures, freed from all cultural clothings. 

It is also interesting, in this connection, to observe the general conceptual 
patterns in theological frameworks. The clash of ideas is illuminating in many 
ways. An analysis of the Greek cosmological views of the origin of the world 
and the radical departure of the Judeo-Christian idea of creation out of nothing 
involves a set of theological ideas. The world as beginningless or everlasting - an 
idea recurring in Greek thought - is found inadequate, so to speak, from the 
theological standpoint. It grants the world a self-existent, independent status. 
The majesty of the conception of the Transcendent as timeless is such that no 
principle of material causation can be granted any self-existence. The notions of 
the Transcendent, creation and nothingness form a necessary sequel of ideas. 

In the Brahmanical tradition itself, the Advaita Vedanta pronouncement of the 
world as an appearance has also this theological motive of pointing to Brahman 
as the non-dual reality. In other words, Advaita Vedanta cannot accept the 
arambhavada of the Nyaya-Vaisesika or the abhivyaktivada of Sarikhya-Yoga, 
which are operating with an independent principle (or principles) of material 
causation. 

To focus on Brahman as equally the material and instrumental cause ( abhinna 
upadana nimitta karana ), the system propounds vivartavada , reducing the pro¬ 
blem of creation to a problem of appearance. Advaita cosmology is epistemic. 
The causality of Brahman is not understood as temporally prior but as the 
indispensable ground for the occurrence of the effect (appearance). There is no 
temporal passage between Brahman and the world. 

In this connection we shall also draw attention to the interesting point of 
opposition in the expressions of these theologies. In spite of maintaining the 
dependent status of the world, for the Advaita Vedanta the world (as appearance) 
is beginningless ( anadi ); whereas for the Christian theology, the world (as crea¬ 
ted) has a beginning. It is fruitful for an encounter of theologies to attempt to 
unmask such opposed expressions in order to estimate, how wide is the concep¬ 
tual gulf in their philosophical understanding of such problems. 

These questions immediately refer to the problem of time amongst other 
important theological considerations as basis for such pronouncements. 

The Advaita Vedanta, like the other schools operating within the framework 
of Brahmanical tradition, takes the world as beginningless (anadi) i.e. there 



164 The timeless and the temporal - paradox and predicament 

never was a time when the world was not. In other words, the world cannot be 
said to have a beginning in time, prior to which it could be taken as non-existent. 

The antithesis of the position, viz. the world has a beginning in time, is not the 
intended meaning for those who maintain that the world has a beginning - as we 
have noted in Saint Augustine’s discussions on the problem. 

This calls for reflection - which is important for a theology of encounter - as 
to where the various expressions differ genuinely in their intended import and 
where the border is not clear-cut, despite their apparent opposition. 

The Transcendent as timeless and the world as temporal confronts the theolor 
gical mind across the boundaries of cultures. The conception of time and the 
timeless can be studied in all aspects with reference to different theological 
structures that operate with them. 

The intellectual struggle to express and conceptualize these basic theological 
insights requires a linguistic medium which is naturally symbolic in character. 
Myths, allegories, images and a rich variety of symbols come to play an impor¬ 
tant role, as the conventional, empirical medium has to perform the significant 
function of pointing beyond all that is ‘of this world’.

Based on Sanskrit source material, this book is a unique attempt at presenting a compre¬ 
hensive review of the widely divergent views about time in Indian thought. Clearly writ¬ 
ten, it succeeds in setting out the issues of discussion pointedly and cogently. Since the 
concept of time intertwines with such major concepts as that of causality, being and non- 
being etc., this book also ‘serves as a general introduction to the classic heart of Indian 
philosophy’. The author ‘has demonstrated a rare ability to translate technical doctrines 
from one tradition of thought into the language of another’, and thus has made it possi¬ 
ble—for all those who are concerned with the question of time but do not have access in 
the Indian conceptual world to appreciate the contributions of Indian thought with re¬ 
gard to this complex question. Noteworthy is the fact that this book is the first attempt 
which ‘successfully exposes the simple falsity’ of such cliches as that the Indian view of 
time is ‘cyclic’ as opposed to the Judaeo-Christian understanding of linear time. A Study 
of Time in Indian Philosophy therefore, renders a valuable service to all those who are 
concerned with cross-cultural and interreligious exchange. 

Anindita N. Balslev did her Ph.D. from University of Paris. Presently she is based in 
Denmark and engaged in promoting a program entitled Cross-Cultural Conversation. 
Her other published work is Cultural Otherness: Correspondence with Richard Rorty 
(Shimla/New Delhi, 1991).
